Heegaard Floer Symplectic homology and Viterbo’s isomorphism theorem in the context of multiple particles
Roman Krutowski, Tianyu Yuan

Abstract
. Given a Liouville manifold M M , we introduce an invariant of M M that we call the Heegaard Floer symplectic cohomology S ​ H κ ∗ ​ ( M ) SH^{*}_{\kappa}(M) for any κ ≥ 1 \kappa\geq 1 that coincides with the symplectic cohomology for κ = 1 \kappa=1 . Writing M ^ \hat{M} for the completion of M M , the differential counts pseudoholomorphic curves of arbitrary genus in ℝ × S 1 × M ^ \mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times\hat{M} that are required to be branched κ \kappa -sheeted covers when projected to the ℝ × S 1 \mathbb{R}\times S^{1} -direction; this resembles the cylindrical reformulation of Heegaard Floer homology by Lipshitz. These cohomology groups provide a closed-string analogue of higher-dimensional Heegaard Floer homology introduced by Colin, Honda, and Tian. When M ^ = T ∗ ​ Q \hat{M}=T^{*}Q with Q Q an orientable manifold, we introduce a Morse-theoretic analogue of Heegaard Floer symplectic cohomology, which we call the free multiloop complex of Q Q . When Q Q has vanishing relative second Stiefel-Whitney class, we prove a generalized version of Viterbo’s isomorphism theorem by showing that the cohomology groups S ​ H κ ∗ ​ ( T ∗ ​ Q ) SH^{*}_{\kappa}(T^{*}Q) are isomorphic to the cohomology groups of the free multiloop complex of Q Q .

[bib,biblist]nametitledelim,

2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 53D40; Secondary 57R17.

1.
Introduction

Since its introduction in Viterbo’s foundational paper
[
Vit99
]
, symplectic cohomology has emerged as a powerful and useful invariant of Liouville domains. Let
(M,\lambda)
be a Liouville domain, and
\hat{M}=M\cup_{\partial M}([1,\infty)\times\partial M)
be its completion. One way to define the symplectic cohomology of
M
(or of
\hat{M}
) is to count pseudoholomorphic cylinders connecting closed orbits of the usual one-particle motion Hamiltonian system in the completion
\hat{M}
according to
[
Sei08
,
AS10a
]
. In this paper, we construct an invariant of
M
where, instead of one-particle motion, we consider the dynamics of multiple identical particles in
\hat{M}
. The closed orbits then can be regarded as loops in the unordered configuration space
\operatorname{UConf}_{\kappa}(\hat{M})
, where
\kappa\geq 1
is the number of particles under consideration.

Naturally, one would want to count pseudoholomorphic cylinders in the symmetric product
\operatorname{Sym}^{\kappa}(\hat{M})
connecting orbits as above. The main obstacle is that, in general,
the symmetric product of a manifold is not a smooth manifold. One strategy is to study Floer theory of the symplectic orbifold
\operatorname{Sym}^{\kappa}(\hat{M})
and it has been undertaken in
[
MS21
]
, based on the approach from
[
CP14
]
, for
\kappa=2
to obtain non-dispaceability results for Lagrangian links in
S^{2}\times S^{2}
. Instead, in the present work we study moduli spaces of curves in
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times\hat{M}
mimicking Lipshitz’s cylindrical reformulation
[
Lip06
]
of the Heegaard Floer homology. This provides a closed string analogue of higher-dimensional Heegaard Floer homology (HDHF) introduced by Colin, Honda, and Tian in
[
CHT20
]
. We recall that HDHF provides a model for the Lagrangian Floer homology of the symmetric product
\operatorname{Sym}^{\kappa}(M)
.

In this paper, we present two closely related versions of such a sought-after invariant which we refer to as the
unsymmetrized version

SH^{*}_{\kappa,\text{unsym}}(M)
of Heegaard Floer symplectic cohomology (HFSH) and the
partially symmetrized version

SH^{*}_{\kappa,\text{psym}}(M)
of HFSH.

Theorem 1.1

(Theorems
2.41
,
2.53
and
)
.

The Heegaard Floer symplectic cohomology groups
SH^{*}_{\kappa,\text{psym}}(M)
and
SH^{*}_{\kappa,\text{unsym}}(M)
are well defined and are invariants of the Liouville domain
M
, independent of all the intrinsic choices of the Floer data required for its setup.

The main obstacle for translating the HDHF setup to the closed string context is that unlike in
[
CHT20
]
the tuples of orbits of cumulative time
\kappa
may contain multiply covered orbits. This may lead to the multiply covered curves in the boundary of moduli spaces of solutions to an appropriate
\bar{\partial}_{J,H}
-equation. The tool we develop here to tackle this issue is a certain perturbation scheme on a branched manifold that we associate with Hurwitz spaces
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
serving as moduli spaces of branched
\kappa
-sheeted covers of the cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
. We point out that this setting simplifies numerous problems that arise in the context of higher-genus SFT
[
EGH00
]
.

Let us now consider the case where
\hat{M}=T^{*}Q
. We recall that Abbondandolo-Schwarz
[
AS06
]
and Abouzaid
[
Abo11
]
show that for an orientable manifold
Q
there is an isomorphism between the symplectic cohomology
SH^{*}_{b}(T^{*}Q)
twisted by a certain background class and the homology of the free loop space of
Q
. Abbondandolo and Schwarz construct a chain map from the Morse complex associated with action functional
\mathcal{A}_{L}
on the space
\Lambda^{1}(Q)
of free loops in the class
W^{1,2}
to the symplectic cochain complex. They prove that this chain map is a quasi-isomorphism, whereas Abouzaid constructs a chain map in the opposite direction and proves that it is the inverse of the former.

In this paper, in an attempt to compute the unsymmetrized version of Heegaard Floer symplectic cohomology, we introduce a Morse-theoretic model which is intended to generalize the Morse complex
CM_{*}(\Lambda^{1}(Q))
. We consider the space
\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)
of
free multiloops
of class
W^{1,2}
which can be understood as free loops in
\operatorname{UConf}_{\kappa}(Q)
of class
W^{1,2}
. The differential of this Morse complex
CM_{*}(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q))
counts not only gradient trajectories but also
piecewise gradient trajectories
where the multiloops are allowed to undergo
switchings
at finitely many moments. These switchings are meant to correspond to ramification points on the Heegaard Floer side. This model is parallel to the Morse model for HDHF in
[
HKTY
]
.

For any orientable manifold
Q
of dimension
n
with vanishing second Stiefel-Whitney class
w_{2}(TQ)\in H^{2}(T^{*}Q;\mathbb{Z}/2)
we then construct a map

\mathcal{F}\colon SH_{\kappa,\text{unsym}}^{*}(T^{*}Q)\rightarrow CM_{\kappa n-*}(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)),

which for
\kappa=1
coincides with the chain map constructed by Abouzaid. It also extends the map constructed in
[
HTY a
]
in the context of HDHF for
Q
of dimension
2
to higher dimensions. We then prove the following:

Theorem 1.2

(Theorems
4.20
and
4.11
)
.

For
Q
an orientable manifold with vanishing second Stiefel-Whitney class
w_{2}(TQ)\penalty\ \in\penalty\ H^{2}(T^{*}Q;\mathbb{Z}/2)
the map
\mathcal{F}
is a chain map and induces an isomorphism on the homology

SH^{*}_{\kappa,\text{unsym}}(T^{*}Q)\cong HM_{\kappa n-*}(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)).

In the situation of ordinary symplectic cohomology Abouzaid
[
Abo11
,
Abo15
]
constructs an isomorphism of this kind when
w_{2}(TQ)\neq 0
between the symplectic cohomology with twisted coefficients and the Morse homology of the free loop space. We hope that
\mathcal{F}
as above also can be extended to such a more general setting. The first work which highlighted the necessity of twisted coefficients is that of Kragh
[
Kra18
]
.

There are numerous questions one can ask regarding HFSH and the Morse model above, and here we list just some of them. First, the original symplectic cohomology possesses various algebraic structures and we refer the reader to
[
Sei08
,
Rit13
,
Abo15
]
for a broad review. Therefore, it is natural to ask:

Question 1.3
.

How much of various algebra structures including the BV-algebra structure, Viterbo restriction, TQFT-structure, etc., can be extended from symplectic cohomology to HFSH?

We expect most of the algebraic operations related to symplectic cohomology to have their analogues in the context of HFSH. We further hope that the corresponding structures will come in handy to show the generation criterion for HDHF wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}_{\kappa}(T^{*}Q)
, extending the result of Abouzaid
[
Abo10
]
. More precisely, we pose the following:

Conjecture 1.4
.

Given a closed oriented manifold
Q
with vanishing relative second Stiefel-Whitney class, the HDHF wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}_{\kappa}(T^{*}Q)
is split-generated by any
\kappa
-tuple of disjoint cotangent fibers.

Furthermore, based on the work of Ganatra
[
Gan12
]
we expect a close relationship between Hochschild homology
HH_{*}(\mathcal{W}_{\kappa}(\hat{M}))
of the wrapped HDHF Fukaya category and HFSH and we state it as the following:

Question 1.5
.

What is the relationship between the Hochschild homology
HH_{*}(\mathcal{W}_{\kappa}(\hat{M}))
and the Heegaard Floer symplectic cohomology
SH^{*}_{\kappa}(M)
for
M
a Liouville domain?

Structure of the paper.

Section 2 is devoted to the setup of Heegaard Floer symplectic cohomology. We first introduce basic definitions and notation in Sections
2.1
-
2.3
in the context of the partially symmetrized Heegaard Floer symplectic cohomology. In Sections
2.4
-
2.5
we introduce Hurwitz spaces, which serve as model spaces for the Floer data required to define HFSH. Our treatment is based on the long lineage of work devoted to the study of Hurwitz schemes, for instance,
[
ACV03
,
Deo14
,
HM98
,
HM82
,
Ion02
,
Pr“”u17
]
.
We adapt this circle of ideas to our particular problem in the SFT setting. Furthermore, we introduce a branched manifold structure on the Hurwitz spaces in the sense of
[
McD06
]
in Section
2.6
and assign consistent Floer data to Hurwitz spaces.

In Sections
2.9
-
2.11
we show that the above data indeed provide a chain complex
SC_{\kappa,\text{unsym}}(\hat{M};H,J)
, where
(H,J)
denotes some Floer data adapted to a quadratic at infinity Hamiltonian function
H_{0}
and a time-dependent almost complex structure
J_{t}
. Following the original approach in
[
Sei08
]
we give an alternative definition of the partially symmetrized HFSH via tuples of linear Hamiltonian functions in Section
2.12
. We also show that this definition via the direct limit over all tuples of linear Hamiltonians coincides with the definition given by means of a quadratic at infinity
H_{0}
as above. The approach in terms of linear Hamiltonians is known to provide a simple way of showing the invariance of symplectic cohomology and we adopt it to show that the partially symmetrized HFSH also gives an invariant of a Liouville domain
M
.

In Section 3 we introduce the free multiloop complex associated with an oriented manifold
Q
. This section is mostly devoted to showing that multiloops with a differential given by counting piecewise pseudo-gradient trajectories form a chain complex. The methods we use rely greatly on the approach of Abbondandolo-Schwarz in
[
AS10
]
and Abbondandolo-Majer in
[
AM06
]
.

In Section 4 we prove Theorem
1.2
. The chain map is constructed by means of moduli spaces of pseudoholomorphic curves associated with branched
\kappa
-sheeted covers of the half-cylinder
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times S^{1}
extending the ideas in
[
Abo11
,
Abo15
]
to the Heegaard Floer context.

2.
The Floer complex

2.1.
The Liouville manifold

Let
(M,\lambda)
be a
2n
-dimensional Liouville domain, i.e.,
M
is a compact exact symplectic manifold with boundary,
\lambda
is a primitive of a symplectic form, and the Liouville vector field
V
(satisfying
L_{V}d\lambda=d\lambda
) points outwards along the boundary
\partial M
.

The Liouville form
\lambda
restricts to a contact form
\alpha\coloneqq\lambda|_{\partial M}
on
\partial M
.
We then complete
M
to
\hat{M}
by attaching the symplectization end
([1,\infty)_{r}\times\partial M,r\alpha)
along
\partial M
.
Denote the Reeb vector field of
(\partial M,\alpha)
by
R
.
We require that

•

all Reeb orbits of
\alpha
are non-degenerate.

To define an integral grading for the Floer complex, we further require that

•

2c_{1}(M)\in H^{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})
vanishes.

2.2.
Tuples of Hamiltonian orbits

Here, we introduce the cochain complex for the partially symmetrized version of Heegaard Floer symplectic cohomology. The cochains to be introduced correspond to tuples of Hamiltonian orbits of some function of cumulative time
\kappa
. Describing them in this context requires working separately with each partition of
\kappa
. That is why we would have to reserve significantly more Hamiltonians than
\kappa
, which is enough for the unsymmetrized version; see Section
2.13
.

Let
\bm{\mu}=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{l(\bm{\mu})})
satisfying
\mu_{1}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\ldots\geq\mu_{l(\bm{\mu})}
be a partition of
\kappa
, where
l(\bm{\mu})
denotes the
length
of
\bm{\mu}
, i.e., the number of elements in the partition. Following standard notation, we set
\bm{\mu}!\coloneqq\mu_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot\mu_{l(\bm{\mu})}
. We put

(2.1)

\displaystyle m_{i}(\bm{\mu})=\#\{j\colon\mu_{j}=i\},

(2.2)

\displaystyle R_{\bm{\mu}}=\prod_{i}m_{i}(\bm{\mu})!,

(2.3)

\displaystyle N_{\bm{\mu}}=\bm{\mu}!\cdot R_{\bm{\mu}}.

For the
i
-th element
\mu_{i}
of the partition
\bm{\mu}
we assign an additional ordering given by

ord_{i}(\bm{\mu})=i-\sum_{\mu_{i}<k\leq\mu_{1}}m_{k}(\bm{\mu})
. This ordering is meant to record the encountering of each particular value in the partition
\bm{\mu}
, e.g., for
\bm{\mu}=(2,2,1,1,1)
we have
ord_{4}(\bm{\mu})=2
and therefore
\mu_{4}
is the second term in this partition of value
\mu_{4}=1
.

Let
H_{0}\colon S^{1}_{t}\times\hat{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a time independent Hamiltonian function on
\hat{M}
, which is quadratic away from some compact subset of
\hat{M}
, i.e.,
H_{0}=r^{2}/2
on
[R,\infty)_{r}\times\partial M
for some
R\geq 1
. We denote the class of such functions by
\mathcal{H}(\hat{M})\subset C^{\infty}(S^{1}\times\hat{M})
.
We choose
F_{0}\colon S^{1}\times\hat{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
to be a smooth nonnegative function satisfying:

(1)

the functions
F_{0}
and
\lambda(X_{F_{0}})
are uniformly bounded in absolute value;

(2)

all time-
1
periodic orbits of
X_{H_{0}^{\text{tot}}}
, the Hamiltonian vector field of the function
H_{0}^{\text{tot}}=H_{0}+F_{0}
, are non-degenerate.

The choice of such
F_{0}
for a given
H_{0}\in\mathcal{H}(M)
exists by
[
Abo10
, Lemma 5.1]
. As we are interested in tuples of Hamiltonian orbits of
H^{\text{tot}}_{0}
of cumulative time
\kappa
we now introduce further perturbations to ensure that such tuples are non-degenerate.
That is, we choose a collection of functions
F_{m,j}
for
1\leq m\leq\kappa
,
1\leq j\leq\lfloor\frac{\kappa}{m}\rfloor
satisfying

(1)

F_{m,j}\colon\mathbb{R}/m\mathbb{Z}\times\hat{M}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is non-negative such that
|F_{m,j}-F_{0}|
is absolutely bounded by a small constant and vanishes away from the locus of time-
m
orbits of
H^{\text{tot}}_{0}
;

(2)

all time-
m
orbits of
H^{\text{tot}}_{m,j}=H_{0}+F_{m,j}
are non-degenerate and each such orbit lies in a small neighborhood of the corresponding time-
m
orbits of
H^{\text{tot}}_{0}
;

(3)

Hamiltonian time-
m
orbits for different
H^{\text{tot}}_{m,j}
’s are disjoint and embedded;

We further denote the collections of Hamiltonians

\bm{H}^{\text{tot}}=\{H^{\text{tot}}_{m,j}\}
satisfying all of the above properties by
{\mathcal{\bm{H}}}_{S^{1}}^{\kappa}(\hat{M})
.

Let us note that, due to rotational symmetry, a non-multiply covered time-
m
orbit of
H_{0}^{\text{tot}}
it corresponds to
m
orbits of
H_{0}^{\text{tot}}
with the same image. Therefore, these
m
orbits produce
m
distinct orbits of
H^{\text{tot}}_{m,j}
for any
j
,
1\leq j\leq\lfloor\frac{\kappa}{m}\rfloor
. See Figure
1
for an illustration of this phenomenon with
m=2
.

Figure 1
.
The black curve denotes a time-
2
orbit of
H_{0}^{\text{tot}}
. The green and red curves denote the two nearby orbits of
H^{\text{tot}}_{2,1}
. The dots correspond to the starting points of these orbits.

More generally, assume we are given a tuple of Hamiltonian orbits of
H^{\text{tot}}_{0}
of cumulative time
\kappa
with no multiply covered orbits among them, and let the periods of these orbits form a partition
\bm{\mu}
of
\kappa
. There are
R_{\bm{\mu}}
ways to order these orbits preserving the time-ordering. Given such an order on the orbits
x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell(\bm{\mu})}
, there are
\bm{\mu}!
distinct tuples of orbits of the form
x_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,x_{\ell(\bm{\mu})}^{\prime}
, where each
x_{i}^{\prime}
is a Hamiltonian orbit of
H^{\text{tot}}_{\mu_{i},ord_{i}(\bm{\mu})}
. All these
\mu!
tuples are in the small neighborhood of the initial tuple
x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell(\bm{\mu})}
.
Hence, in total near the initial tuple, there are
N_{\bm{\mu}}
new “desymmetrized” tuples with disjoint images, since there are
R_{\bm{\mu}}
orderings and a total of
\bm{\mu}!
rotations.

We set
\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}
to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}\coloneqq\left\{\bm{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l(\bm{\mu})})\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{$x_{i}\colon[0,\mu_{i}]\rightarrow\hat{M}$ is the integral flow of $\phi^{t}_{H^{\text{tot}}_{\mu_{i},ord_{i}(\bm{\mu})}}$,}\\
\text{$x_{i}(0)=x_{i}(\mu_{i})$, $\bm{\mu}$ is a partition of $\kappa$.}\end{array}\right.\right\}

Figure 2
.
A sample tuple of Hamiltonian orbits in
\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}
for
\kappa=5
.

To an orbit
x_{\mu_{i}}
we associate a differential operator

(2.6)

D_{x_{\mu_{i}}}\colon W^{1,2}(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C}^{n})\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C}^{n}),

following
[
Abo10
, Section C.6]
. Using this operator, one defines the
orientation line
of
x_{\mu_{i}}

(2.7)

o_{x_{\mu_{i}}}=|\operatorname{det}(D_{x_{\mu_{i}}})|,

i.e.,
o_{x_{\mu_{i}}}
is a rank-
1
free abelian group with two generators representing two possible orientations of
\operatorname{det}(D_{x_{\mu_{i}}})
and the relation that their sum is zero; see Section
2.14
for the notations that we use here.

Definition 2.1
.

The
orientation line

o_{\bm{x}}
of
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}
is given as follows:

(2.8)

o_{\bm{x}}=o_{x_{\mu_{1}}}\otimes\ldots\otimes o_{x_{\mu_{\ell(\bm{\mu})}}}.

Regarding
o_{\bm{x}}
as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module we set

(2.9)

o_{\bm{x}}^{\mathbb{Q}}=\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}o_{\bm{x}}

We then set

(2.10)

V_{\kappa,\text{psym}}=\bigoplus_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}}o_{\bm{x}}^{\mathbb{Q}}.

Each
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}
comes with a grading that we discuss in Section
2.3
. Hence the above is a graded vector space with

(2.11)

V^{i}_{\kappa,\text{psym}}=\bigoplus_{|\bm{x}|=i}o_{\bm{x}}^{\mathbb{Q}}.

Consider a formal variable
\hbar
with grading
|\hbar|=2-n
. We then set
SC^{*}_{\kappa,\text{psym}}(\hat{M})
to be a graded vector subspace in
V_{\kappa,\text{psym}}[[\hbar]]

(2.12)

SC_{\kappa,\text{psym}}^{m}(\hat{M})\coloneqq\sum_{i+(2-n)j=m}V^{i}_{\kappa,\text{psym}}\hbar^{j}=\sum_{i+(2-n)j=m}V^{i}_{\kappa,\text{psym}}\bigl[(n-2)j\bigr],

where in the last term we used the shift convention from Section
2.14
and the summation sign means direct product. We notice that this space is a graded
\mathbb{Q}[h]
-module (but it is not a
\mathbb{Q}[[\hbar]]
-module). In particular, multiplication by
\hbar
induces a
(2-n)
-degree shift on
SC^{*}_{\kappa,\text{psym}}(\hat{M})
.

For each tuple of Hamiltonian orbits
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell(\bm{\mu})})\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}
, we define the action of
\bm{x}
by

(2.13)

\mathcal{A}(\bm{x})=\sum^{\ell(\bm{\mu})}_{i=1}\mathcal{A}(x_{i}).

where the action of the orbit
x_{i}
is given by the expression

(2.14)

\mathcal{A}(x_{i})=\int^{\mu_{i}}_{0}x_{i}^{*}\lambda-\int^{\mu_{i}}_{0}H^{\text{tot}}_{\mu_{i},ord_{i}{\bm{\mu}}}(t,x_{i}(t))dt,

We will further denote by
\bm{\mu}(\bm{x})\vdash\kappa
the partition associated with
\bm{x}
.
A tuple of Hamiltonian orbits
\bm{x}
can be also viewed as a loop in
\mathrm{Conf}_{\kappa}(\hat{M})
by considering a collection of
\kappa
paths, where each path takes the form
\gamma\colon[0,1]\rightarrow\hat{M}
with

\gamma(t)\equiv x_{i}(k+t),\,t\in[0,1]

for some
x_{i}\in\bm{x}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
0\leq k<\mu_{i}
.

Remark 2.2
.

The main difference between
\mathcal{P}_{\text{unsym}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}
which we introduce in Section
2.13
, is that an unperturbed loop in
\mathrm{Conf}_{\kappa}(\hat{M})
(given by
\kappa
time-
1
flows of
H_{0}
) provides
\kappa!
elements of
\mathcal{P}_{\text{unsym}}
and only
N_{\bm{\mu}}
(see (
2.2
) and (
2.3
)) elements of
\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}
, where
\bm{\mu}!
stands for a number of ways to choose a starting point on each of the orbits and
R_{\bm{\mu}}
is responsible for ordering the orbits.

In the toy example with a tuple consisting of Hamiltonian orbits of
H_{0}
of respective times
\bm{\mu}=(2,1,1)
we have
\bm{\mu}!=2
, as there are two ways to pick the starting point on the time-
2
orbit, and
N_{\bm{\mu}}=2
as there are two ways to give an order on the
2
time-
1
orbits. Therefore, in this example
\#\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}=4
and
\#\mathcal{P}_{\text{unsym}}=24
.

2.3.
Almost complex structures and gradings

Denote the contact hyperplane field
\operatorname{Ker}\lambda|_{\partial M}\subset T(\partial M)
by
\xi
.
Then we can write

(2.15)

T([1,\infty)\times\partial M)=\mathbb{R}^{2}\oplus\xi,

where
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is spanned by
\partial_{r}
and the Reeb vector field
R
.

Let
\mathcal{J}(\hat{M})
be the set of almost complex structures on
\hat{M}
tamed by
d\lambda
which are of
rescaled contact type
on the symplectization end:

(2.16)

-\frac{1}{r}\lambda\circ J=dr,

that is,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}J\colon\xi\rightarrow\xi,\\
J(R)=\partial_{r},\end{array}\right.

on
[1,\infty)\times\partial M
for
J\in\mathcal{J}(\hat{M})
. From now on we fix a generic family of almost complex structures of rescaled contact type

(2.19)

J_{t}\colon[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{J}(\hat{M}).

Given any almost complex structure in
\mathcal{J}(\hat{M})
, we can define a grading on
SC_{\kappa}(\hat{M})
.
Since
2c_{1}(M)=0
, the bicanonical bundle
(\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}T^{*}\hat{M})^{\otimes 2}
is trivial. One can then define the usual Conley-Zehnder indices of Hamiltonian orbits.

For a tuple of Hamiltonian orbits
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}
we set its grading as well as the grading of the associated orientation line
o_{\bm{x}}
to be

(2.20)

|\bm{x}|=\operatorname{deg}(o_{\bm{x}})=\kappa n-CZ(x_{1})-\ldots-CZ(x_{\ell(\bm{\mu}(\bm{x}))}).

Remark 2.3
.

Note that for
\kappa=1
the grading above agrees with the convention in
[
Abo10
]
.

2.4.
Moduli space of marked cylinders

Definition 2.4
.

A
stable cylinder
is a tuple
\bm{C}=(C,j,B,D)
, where

•

(C,j)
is Riemann surface consisting of the
main component
which is a cylinder
C_{0}=\mathbb{R}_{s}\times S^{1}_{t}
with
j|_{C_{0}}
a standard complex structure and a collection of projective planes (i.e.,
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s up to biholomorphism) with
j
restricting to the standard complex structure;

•

B
is the finite ordered tuple comprised of
marked points

q_{1},\ldots,q_{|B|}
on
C
;

•

D
is the set consisting of unordered nonintersecting pairs of points
\{d,d^{\prime}\}\subset C
which are disjoint from
B
and belong to different components, where any such pair represents a node.

•

each component is
stable
;

•

the nodal Riemann surface
\bm{C}^{D}\coloneqq C/\sim_{D}
, where
d\sim_{D}d^{\prime}
if
\{d,d^{\prime}\}\in D
, is connected and the genus of
\bm{C}^{D}
equals
0
.

Essentially, one may regard the main component
\mathbb{R}_{s}\times S^{1}_{t}
as a sphere
\mathbb{P}^{1}
punctured at
2
marked points
q_{+\infty}=\infty\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
q_{-\infty}=0\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Compactifying the main component yields a genus
0
curve with two marked points
q_{-\infty}
and
q_{+\infty}
.
From this perspective, assuming that
|B|=k
, stable cylinders up to automorphism (by this we mean biholomorphisms that preserve
B
as an ordered set, and
D
as a set) may be regarded as a part of Deligne-Mumford compactification
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,k+2}
of the moduli space of genus zero curves with
k+2
marked points
(q_{+\infty},q_{-\infty},q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})
. Namely, classes of stable cylinders up to isomorphism are in bijection with automorphism classes of genus zero nodal curves with
q_{+\infty}
and
q_{-\infty}
staying in the same rational component.

For the main component of a given stable cylinder
\bm{C}
as above to be stable, it has to contain at least one special point (a marked point or a node). A cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
with no special points would be further called a
trivial cylinder
.

We also note that one may identify
S^{1}\times\{\pm\infty\}
with the corresponding space of tangent directions

(2.21)

S_{q_{\pm\infty}}\mathbb{P}^{1}=T_{q_{\pm\infty}}\mathbb{P}^{1}/\mathbb{R}.

We denote the set of all stable cylinders with
b
marked points by
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b}
.
Denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b}
the space of stable cylinders (i.e., cylindrical buildings of height
1
without nodes) with
b
marked points and by
\mathcal{C}_{b}
its quotient by the
\mathbb{R}
-action on the cylindrical component and by all biholomorphisms on the rational components.

Definition 2.5
.

A
cylindrical building
of height
n
is a Riemann surface

\bm{C}=(\overrightarrow{C}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}),j,B,D=D_{r}\cup D_{s}),

where each
C_{i}
is a stable cylinder and
B
is an ordered set of marked points obtained as a union of marked points of each
C_{i}
. The set of
regular nodes

D_{r}
is an unordered collection of nodes of all the
C_{i}
and the set of
special nodes

D_{s}
is a set of all the pairs
\{0_{i},\infty_{i+1}\}
, where
0_{i}
is a point
0
of the main component of
C_{i}
and
\infty_{i+1}
is a point
\infty
of the main component of
C_{i+1}
.

Notice that by smoothing all the nodes (together with special ones) of the height
n
cylindrical building one obtains a genus
0
connected nodal Riemann surface.

We denote the set of all cylindrical buildings of height
n
with
b
marked points by
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,n}
. In the special case of
n=1
we have
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,1}=\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b}
.

Definition 2.6
.

We say that a cylindrical building
\bm{C}
of height
n
is
smooth
if the set
D_{r}
is empty; equivalently each
C_{i}
consists only of the main component.

We denote the set of smooth cylindrical buildings of height
n
by
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\text{sm}}_{b,n}
.
On
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\text{sm}}_{b,n}
there is a natural
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-action given by vertical parallel transports on the main components of the
C_{i}
’s. Let us denote the quotient by

(2.22)

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{b,n}=\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\text{sm}}_{b,n}/\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Similarly, we denote by
\mathcal{C}_{b,n}
the quotient of
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,n}
by the above-mentioned
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-action on the main components and further action by biholomorphisms on the compact components.

For any cylindrical building
\bm{C}
of height
n
we denote by
\text{Aut}_{v}(\bm{C})
the group of
vertical automorphisms
that acts by vertical translations on the main components and is the
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by which we have been taking quotients above, e.g. in (
2.22
).

Remark 2.7
.

We stress that we are not going to quotient out the
S^{1}
-action on the main components in our considerations.

By taking the disjoint union of all
\mathcal{C}_{b,n}
one obtains a moduli space

(2.23)

\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{b}=\bigsqcup\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\mathcal{C}_{b,n}.

It has a subspace
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{b}\subset\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{b}
serving as a moduli space of smooth cylindrical buildings

(2.24)

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{b}=\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\,\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{b,n}.

Remark 2.8
.

The space
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{b}
is the portion of the full compactification
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{b}
of our main interest, as for the actual moduli space of pseuholomorphic curves all other forms of degeneration do not appear generically; see Section
2.10
. We use hat-notation to describe the portion of a “presumable” compactification consisting of curves with multiple
smooth
(i.e., without nodes) levels.

Remark 2.9
.

Notice that for a given convergent sequence
\bm{C}_{k}
of smooth cylindrical buildings such that no two marked points “collide” it holds that
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\bm{C}_{k}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{b}
.

Remark 2.10
.

The set of
cylindrical buildings
up to obvious automorphism (by this we mean automorphisms that restrict to automorphisms of each
C_{i}
) forms the full Deligne-Mumford compactification
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,k+2}
. The height of a building is just the distance between the leaves corresponding to
q_{+\infty}
and
q_{-\infty}
in the tree associated with the building regarded as a nodal rational curve.

We set the topology on these spaces following
[
BEHWZ03
]
. One can show that

Lemma 2.11
.

The space
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{b}
is a compact metric space and serves as the compactification of the space
\mathcal{C}_{b}
, i.e., it coincides with the closure of
\mathcal{C}_{b}
viewed as a subspace of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{b}
. In particular, every sequence of smooth cylinders with
b
marked points has a subsequence that converges to a cylindrical building.

Sketch of the proof.

For simplicity, assume we are given a sequence of smooth cylinders
\bm{C}_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{b}
. Following
[
HT07
, Section 2]
one can pick a subsequence
\bm{C}_{n_{k}}
with marked points denoted by
q_{1}^{k},\ldots,q_{b}^{k}
and a partition
\bm{\mu}\vdash b
, such that

•

there is a constant
D
satisfying
\forall j,j^{\prime}\in\mu_{i}\colon\text{dist}(q^{k}_{j},q_{j^{\prime}}^{k})<D
for any term
\mu_{i}
of
\bm{\mu}
;

•

if
j
and
j^{\prime}
do not belong to the same element of
\bm{\mu}
, then
\text{dist}(q_{j}^{k},q_{j^{\prime}}^{k})>k
.

In other words, one may divide branched points into “clusters” such that
t
-coordinate difference between points of different clusters goes to
\infty
. Moreover, the elements of
\bm{\mu}
can be ordered by their height (i.e., the
t
-coordinates of elements of one cluster are bigger than the
t
-coordinates of the points in another cluster). If no branched points “collide” then the limit of such sequence would be a smooth height
\ell(\bm{\mu})
cylindrical building. Otherwise, there would appear bubbles and the limit would be a stable height
\ell(\bm{\mu})
cylindrical building.
∎

For further usage, we note that boundary
\partial\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{b}
can be covered by images of the natural maps

(2.25)

\bigsqcup_{b_{1}+b_{2}=b}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{b_{1}}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{b_{2}}\rightarrow\partial\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{b},

(2.26)

\bigsqcup_{b_{1}+b_{2}=b}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{b_{1}}\times\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{b_{2}}\rightarrow\partial\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{b},

where the space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{b_{2}}
in (
2.25
) refers to bubbling on the body of a cylinder and (
2.26
) describes the appearance of a new level in the building.

Remark 2.12
.

Despite forcing out levels that could be trivial cylinders for elements of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{b}
later on we would consider cylindrical buildings with a finite number of trivial cylinders inserted between some of the levels. Clearly, a sequence of cylindrical buildings that converges to an element of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{b}
also converges to the one obtained this way.

To shed more light on the relation between moduli space of cylindrical buildings
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{b}
and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,b+2}
(see Remark
2.10
) we note that there is an
U(1)
-principal bundle

(2.27)

U(1)\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{b,1}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{0,b+2},

where the
U(1)
acts by counterclockwise rotations on a given cylinder. Denote by
\mathcal{M}_{0,(b_{1}+2,b_{2}+2)}
the subset of
\partial\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,b+2}
consisting of nodal curves with
2
components
S_{1}
,
S_{2}
, such that
q_{+\infty}\in S_{1},q_{-\infty}\in S_{2}
and
b_{1}+b_{2}=b
. Then there is a principal bundle

(2.28)

U(1)^{2}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{b,2}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{0,(b_{1}+2,b_{2}+2)},

and more generically there is
U(1)^{n}
-principal bundle

(2.29)

U(1)^{n}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{b,n}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{0,(b_{1}+2,b_{2}+2,\ldots,b_{n}+2)}

where the last space is defined similarly.

We note that
\mathcal{M}_{0,(b_{1}+2,b_{2}+2)}
is the image of the natural map

(2.30)

\mathcal{M}_{0,b_{1}+2}\times\mathcal{M}_{0,b_{2}+2}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,b+2}

given by gluing two curves
(C_{1};q_{+\infty},q_{1},\ldots,q_{b_{1}},q_{-\infty})
and
(C_{2};q^{\prime}_{+\infty},q^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,q^{\prime}_{b_{2}},q^{\prime}_{-\infty})
at the node
\{q_{-\infty},q^{\prime}_{+\infty}\}
.

We denote by
L_{q_{i}}
the line bundle over
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,b+2}
with fiber
T_{q_{i}}^{*}C
over

(C;q_{+\infty},q_{1},\ldots,q_{b_{1}},q_{-\infty})\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,b+2}.

We now recall facts from deformation theory following
[
Ion02
]
; we refer the reader to the related descriptions of a neighborhood of a boundary stratum given in
[
HK14
]
,
[
IP04
]
,
[
Sie97
]
.
Namely, the local coordinates in the normal direction near
\mathcal{M}_{0,(b_{1}+2,b_{2}+2)}

are given by

(2.31)

\zeta\in L^{*}_{q_{-\infty}}\otimes L^{*}_{q^{\prime}_{+\infty}},

which is a line bundle over
\mathcal{M}_{0,(b_{1}+2,b_{2}+2)}
with fiber
T_{q_{-\infty}}C_{1}\otimes T_{q^{\prime}_{+\infty}}
.
It turns out that the line bundle given in (
2.31
) is essentially the normal complex line bundle to
\mathcal{M}_{0,(b_{1}+2,b_{2}+2)}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,b+2}
. In local coordinates
(w_{1},w_{2})
on
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
near the node

w_{1}w_{2}=0

the curve associated with
\zeta
is locally a solution of

(2.32)

w_{1}w_{2}=\zeta.

Analogously, we may define a complex line bundle

(2.33)

\hat{L}^{*}_{q_{-\infty}}\otimes\hat{L}^{*}_{q^{\prime}_{+\infty}}

on
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{b,2}
with fiber
T_{q_{-\infty}}C_{1}\otimes T_{q^{\prime}_{+\infty}}C_{2}
at a point
((C_{1};q_{+\infty},\ldots,q_{-\infty}),(C_{2};q^{\prime}_{+\infty},\ldots,q^{\prime}_{-\infty}))
. More explicitly, over
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,2}
both bundles with fibers
T_{q_{-\infty}}C_{1}
and
T_{q^{\prime}_{+\infty}}C_{2}
are trivializable (by the means of global coordinates on cylinders) and
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-equivariant. Thus, these vector bundles induce quotient vector bundles on
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{b,2}
which we denote by
\hat{L}^{*}_{q_{-\infty}}
and
\hat{L}^{*}_{q^{\prime}_{+\infty}}
. We additionally note that projectivizations of real vector bundles
\hat{L}^{*}_{q_{-\infty}}
and
\hat{L}^{*}_{q^{\prime}_{+\infty}}
are trivial. Hence, for (
2.33
) viewed as a real
2
-dimensional bundle there is a global choice of angle coordinate
\varphi
.

We now describe a normal neighborhood of
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{b,2}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{b}
similar to (
2.31
). First, we pick a global section of
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,2}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{b,2}
as follows. Given a representative

((C_{1};q_{+\infty},\ldots,q_{-\infty}),(C_{2};q^{\prime}_{+\infty},\ldots,q^{\prime}_{-\infty}))

of a point in
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{b,2}
we assign to it another representative of the same point
(\widetilde{C}_{1},\widetilde{C}_{2})
obtained by translating
C_{1}
vertically by a negative of the minimum of the
s
-coordinates of its marked points and translating
C_{2}
by a negative of the maximum of the
s
-coordinates of its marked points (to make such section smooth one should pick smooth approximations of
min
and
max
differing by not more than a constant). The point
(\widetilde{C}_{1},\widetilde{C}_{2})
will then always have all marked points of
\widetilde{C}_{1}
above
s=0
and all marked points of
\widetilde{C}_{2}
below
s=0
.

Now pick an
R\in(0,+\infty]
and glue
(\widetilde{C}_{1},\widetilde{C}_{2})
via the standard procedure. Namely, set

\widetilde{C}_{1}^{R}=\widetilde{C}_{1}\setminus(-\infty,-R]\times S^{1},

\widetilde{C}_{2}^{R}=\widetilde{C}_{2}\setminus[R,+\infty)\times S^{1},

\widetilde{C}^{R}=\widetilde{C}_{1}^{R}\sqcup\widetilde{C}_{2}^{R}/\sim,

where the identification
\sim
is given by solving

(2.34)

e^{s_{1}+2\pi\sqrt{-1}t_{1}}\cdot e^{-s_{2}-2\pi\sqrt{-1}t_{2}}=e^{-R}

for
-R\leq s_{1}\leq 0
and for
R=+\infty
we have
\widetilde{C}^{R}=\widetilde{C}_{1}^{R}\sqcup\widetilde{C}_{2}^{R}
.

Hence, we just constructed a diffeomorphism of a neighborhood of
\widehat{C}_{b,2}\subset\widehat{C}_{b}
and

(2.35)

\widehat{C}_{b,2}\times[0,+\infty].

Let us notice that given a parameter
R
as above we can assign a section

(2.36)

\tilde{\zeta}\in\hat{L}^{*}_{q_{-\infty}}\otimes\hat{L}^{*}_{q^{\prime}_{+\infty}}

with
\varphi(\tilde{\zeta})=0
(recall
\varphi
is the angular coordinate) and, moreover,
\tilde{\zeta}=e^{R}
in the local trivialization associated with
(\widetilde{C}_{1},\widetilde{C}_{2})
as in (
2.34
). Hence in local coordinates on cylinders, the equation (
2.34
) is given via

(2.37)

w_{1}w_{2}=\tilde{\zeta},

which is the analogue of (
2.32
). We point out that unlike in the case of moduli space of curves
\mathcal{M}_{0,b+2}
the only sections of
\hat{L}^{*}_{q_{-\infty}}\otimes\hat{L}^{*}_{q^{\prime}_{+\infty}}
that give a family of curves close to breaking are those with
\varphi=0
.

More generally, there is a similar description of a neighborhood of
\widehat{C}_{b,n}
diffeomorphic to

(2.38)

\widehat{C}_{b,n}\times[0,+\infty]^{n-1}.

Given any curve
C
and a point
q\in C
we introduce the notation

(2.39)

\mathcal{S}^{1}_{q}\bm{C}=(T_{q}C\setminus\{0\})/\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

For a nodal curve
\bm{C}
given by two components
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
at the node
\{q_{-\infty},q_{+\infty}^{\prime}\}
as above we additionally introduce

(2.40)

\mathcal{S}^{1}_{q_{-\infty},q_{+\infty}^{\prime}}C=\bigl(T_{q_{-\infty}}C_{1}\otimes T_{q_{+\infty}^{\prime}}C_{2}\setminus\{0\}\bigr)/\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

The circle
\mathcal{S}^{1}_{q}C
can be then regarded as the set of angular coordinates at point
q\in C
. There is a natural
S^{1}
-bundle
\mathcal{S}^{1}_{q}
over
\mathcal{C}_{b}
with such a fiber on
C\in\mathcal{C}_{b}
. Similarly, there is a line bundle
\mathcal{S}^{1}_{q_{-\infty},q_{+\infty}^{\prime}}
over
\widehat{C}_{b,2}
with a fiber
\mathcal{S}^{1}_{q_{-\infty},q_{+\infty}^{\prime}}\bm{C}
.

We now note that
\mathcal{S}^{1}_{q_{-\infty},q_{+\infty}^{\prime}}
is a trivial
S^{1}
bundle, as it is essentially the projectivization of the trivial bundle (
2.33
). Using unit-circle coordinates, we will denote the
0
-angular coordinate as in the discussion above by

(2.41)

1\in\mathcal{S}^{1}_{q_{-\infty},q_{+\infty}^{\prime}}\bm{C}.

For future use we also introduce the following notation for the projectivization of the tensor power of the above line bundle by setting:

(2.42)

(\mathcal{S}^{1}_{q_{-\infty},q_{+\infty}^{\prime}})^{\otimes k}C=\bigl((T_{q_{-\infty}}C_{1}\otimes T_{q_{+\infty}^{\prime}}C_{2})^{\otimes k}\setminus\{0\}\bigr)/\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

2.5.
Hurwitz type spaces

In this subsection, we describe the moduli spaces of certain branched covers of a cylinder and their compactifications closely following
[
HM82
]
. In particular, we describe the smooth structure near the boundary of the compactification of the Hurwitz space in the case of multiple-level smooth degenerations; see Theorem
2.21
for the precise statement.

Pick two partitions
\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}\vdash\kappa
.

Definition 2.13
.

The
Hurwitz space

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
is the space of isomorphism classes
branched covers
, where a branched cover

(S,\pi\colon S\penalty\ \mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\penalty\ \mathbb{R}\penalty\ \times\penalty\ S^{1},\bm{p}^{+},\bm{a}^{+},\bm{p}^{-},\bm{a}^{-},B)

is the following collection of data:

(1)

A Riemann surface
S
without boundary of Euler characteristic
\chi
punctured at positive punctures
\bm{p}^{+}=\{p^{+}_{1},\ldots,p^{+}_{l(\bm{\mu})}\}
and negative punctures
\bm{p}^{-}=\{p^{-}_{1},\ldots,p^{-}_{l(\bm{\mu}^{\prime})}\}
. Denote by
\overline{S}
the compactification of
S
such that
S=\overline{S}\setminus(\bm{p}^{+}\cup\bm{p}^{-})
.

(2)

For each positive puncture
p_{i}^{+}
(resp. negative puncture
p_{i}^{-}
) an
asymptotic marker

a_{i}^{+}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}_{p_{i}^{+}}S\coloneqq(T_{p_{i}^{+}}\overline{S}\setminus\{0\})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}
(resp. an asymptotic marker
a_{i}^{-}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}_{p_{i}^{-}}S
).

(3)

A holomorphic branched cover
\pi\colon S\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
with simple branched points away from
\pm\infty
. At
+\infty
(resp. at
-\infty
) the branching profile is given by
\bm{\mu}
(resp. by
\bm{\mu}^{\prime}
), i.e., a point
p_{i}^{+}
(resp. a point
p_{i}^{-}
) is a ramification point of multiplicity
\mu_{i}
(resp. multiplicity
\mu_{i}^{\prime}
) of an extended map
\overline{\pi}\colon\overline{S}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow S^{2}
. Here
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}=S^{2}\setminus\{+\infty,-\infty\}
. We additionally require that the map induced by
\pi
from
\mathcal{S}^{1}_{p_{i}^{+}}\overline{S}
to
\mathcal{S}^{1}_{+\infty}S^{2}
sends
a_{i}^{+}
to the marker corresponding to
\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}
which is regarded as
1\in\mathcal{S}^{1}_{+\infty}S^{2}
together with similar requirements for negative punctures of
S
.

(4)

An ordered tuple
B
of points
q_{1},\ldots q_{b}
in
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
of simple branching, where
b=-\chi
according to the Riemann-Hurwitz theorem.

Two branched covers
(S,\pi,\bm{p}^{\pm},\bm{a}^{\pm},B)
and
(S^{\prime},\pi^{\prime},\bm{p}^{\prime\pm},\bm{a}^{\prime\pm},B^{\prime})
are said to be
isomorphic
if there exists a biholomorphism
\alpha\colon\overline{S}\rightarrow\overline{S^{\prime}}
such that
\alpha(p^{\pm}_{i})=p_{i}^{\prime\pm}
,
\alpha(a^{\pm}_{i})=a_{i}^{\prime\pm}
and
\pi^{\prime}\circ\alpha=\pi
. Isomorphic branched covers represent the same point of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
.

Figure 3
.
A sample element of
\mathcal{H}^{(2,2),(3,1)}_{4,-4}
.

Remark 2.14
.

Notice that given a geometric branched cover
\pi\colon S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
for each puncture
p_{i}^{+}
(resp.
p_{i}^{-}
) there are only
\mu_{i}
(resp.
\mu_{i}^{\prime}
) possible choices for the marker
a_{i}^{+}
(resp.
a_{i}^{-}
).

Recall that
\text{Aut}_{v}(\mathbb{R}\times S^{1})\cong\mathbb{R}
is a group of vertical translations on the standard cylinder.
This group induces an
\mathbb{R}
-action on
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
by translating the target of a given branched cover. We denote by
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
the quotient space by this action.

Remark 2.15
.

Note that to get the standard Hurwitz scheme
\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
as defined in
[
Ful69
]
or alternatively in a more analytic setting in
[
Ion02
]
one has to drop the asymptotic marker data and take a quotient by the action of the whole group
\text{Aut}(\mathbb{R}\times S^{1})
containing
\text{Aut}_{v}(\mathbb{R}\times S^{1})
as its subgroup.

Definition 2.16
.

A
branched cover building

\left(\bm{C},S=S_{1}\cup\ldots\cup S_{n},\pi,(\bm{v}^{1},\ldots,\bm{v}^{n-1}),(\bm{m}^{1},\ldots,\bm{m}^{n-1})\right),

with Euler characteristic
\chi
and profile
(\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime})
consists of the following data:

(1)

A cylindrical building
\bm{C}=(\overrightarrow{C}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}),j,(q_{1},\ldots,q_{b}),D))\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{b}
.

(2)

A punctured nodal Riemann surface
S
decomposed as
S=S_{1}\cup\ldots\cup S_{n}
with punctures at
\bm{p}^{+},\bm{p}^{-}
, a marked collection of
special nodes

v^{1}_{1},\ldots,v^{1}_{\ell_{1}},v^{2}_{1},\ldots,v^{2}_{\ell_{2}},v^{3}_{1},\ldots,v^{n-1}_{1},\ldots,v^{n-1}_{\ell_{n-1}}

and maybe some other nodes. We require that
S_{i}\cap S_{i+1}=\{v^{i}_{1},\ldots,v^{i}_{l_{i}}\}=\bm{v}^{i}
; writing each node as
v^{i}_{j}=(v^{i}_{j,+},v^{i}_{j,-})
, we use the convention that
v^{i}_{j,+}\in S_{i+1}
and
v^{i}_{j,-}\in S_{i}
.
We regard each tuple
\bm{v}^{i}
as an
unordered collection
of nodes and the lower indices above are used here to simplify the notation.

(3)

An
admissible cover

\pi\colon S\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\bm{C}
, i.e., a branched cover that is simply branched at
q_{1},\ldots,q_{b}
, has a branching profile
\bm{\mu}
at
+\infty_{1}
(by which we mean
+\infty
of the
C_{1}
level of
\bm{C}
) and a branching profile
\bm{\mu}^{\prime}
at
-\infty_{n}
and is possibly branched at nodes.
It is required that
\pi^{-1}(C_{i})=S_{i}
and that the preimage of a node
\{-\infty_{i},+\infty_{i+1}\}
under
\pi
is given by
S_{i}\cap S_{i+1}
. The covering
\pi
has the same degree when restricted to each of the
S_{i}
.

(4)

For each positive puncture
p_{i}^{+}
(resp. negative puncture
p_{i}^{-}
) an
asymptotic marker

a_{i}^{+}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}_{p_{i}^{+}}S=(T_{p_{i}^{+}}\overline{S}\setminus\{0\})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}
(resp. an asymptotic marker
a_{i}^{-}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}_{p_{i}^{-}}S
), satisfying that
\pi_{*}(a_{i}^{+})=1\in\mathcal{S}^{1}_{+\infty}\overline{C_{1}}
(resp.
\pi_{*}(a_{i}^{-})=1\in\mathcal{S}^{1}_{-\infty}\overline{C_{n}}
).

(5)

For each of the nodes
v^{i}_{j}
as above, there is an assigned
matching marker
given by the choice of
m^{i}_{j}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}_{v^{i}_{j}}S
such that it is mapped to

1\in\mathcal{S}^{1}_{q_{-\infty}^{i},q_{+\infty}^{i+1}}\bm{C}=(T_{q_{-\infty}^{i}}C_{i}\otimes T_{q_{+\infty}^{i+1}}C_{i+1}\setminus 0)/\mathbb{R}_{>0}

under the following composition (see (
2.40
) and (
2.42
) for the notation that we use here)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{1}_{v^{i}_{j}}S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{S}^{1}_{v^{i}_{j}})^{\otimes e_{\pi}(v^{i}_{j})}S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{1}_{q_{-\infty}^{i},q_{+\infty}^{i+1}}\bm{C}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi\mapstochar\rightarrow\xi^{e_{\pi}(v^{i}_{j})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}

Here
e_{\pi}(v^{i}_{j})
is the ramification index of
\pi
at the node
v^{i}_{j}
and the map
\pi_{*}
is induced by natural maps linear associated with
\pi
:

(T_{v_{j,-}^{i}}S_{i+1})^{\otimes e_{\pi}(v^{i}_{j})}\rightarrow T_{q_{-\infty}^{i}}C_{i},

(T_{v_{j,+}^{i}}S_{i})^{\otimes e_{\pi}(v^{i}_{j})}\rightarrow T_{q_{+\infty}^{i+1}}C_{i+1}.

We refer the reader to (
2.39
), (
2.40
) and (
2.42
) for the notation we use here. We point out that the horizontal arrow in the diagram above is nothing but an
e_{\pi}(v^{i}_{j})
-sheeted cover of a circle by another circle and that
\pi_{*}
is a diffeomorphism. We will denote the tuple of matching markers corresponding to the tuple of nodes
\bm{v}^{i}
“connecting”
S_{i}
and
S_{i+1}
by
\bm{m}^{i}
.

Two branched cover buildings

\left(\bm{C},S=S_{1}\cup\ldots\cup S_{n},\pi,(\bm{v}^{1},\ldots,\bm{v}^{n-1}),(\bm{m}^{1},\ldots,\bm{m}^{n-1})\right),

\left(\bm{C}^{\prime},S^{\prime}=S_{1}^{\prime}\cup\ldots\cup S_{n}^{\prime},\pi^{\prime},(\bm{v}^{\prime 1},\ldots,\bm{v}^{\prime n-1}),(\bm{m}^{\prime 1},\ldots,\bm{m}^{\prime n-1})\right)

are
isomorphic
if there exists a pair
(\bm{\alpha}\colon S\rightarrow S^{\prime},\bm{\beta})
with
\bm{\alpha}
consisting of biholomorphisms
\alpha_{i}\colon S_{i}\rightarrow S_{i}^{\prime}
and
\bm{\beta}\in\text{Aut}_{v}(\bm{C})
(see Section
2.4
) is a collection consisting of
\beta_{i}\in\text{Aut}_{v}(C_{i})
, satisfying:

(1)

\alpha_{1}(p^{+}_{i})=p_{i}^{\prime+}
,
\alpha_{n}(p^{-}_{i})=p_{i}^{\prime-}
;

(2)

\alpha_{1}(a^{+}_{i})=a_{i}^{\prime+}
,
\alpha_{n}(a^{-}_{i})=a_{n}^{\prime-}
;

(3)

\bm{\alpha}(\bm{v}^{i})=\bm{v}^{\prime i}
as unordered tuples; given some orderings on
\bm{v}^{i}
and
\bm{v}^{\prime i}
we denote by
j(\bm{\alpha})
some index satisfying
\bm{\alpha}(v^{i}_{j})=v^{\prime i}_{j(\bm{\alpha})}
;

(4)

for the orderings as above
\bm{\alpha}^{i}_{*}(m^{i}_{j})=m^{\prime i}_{j(\bm{\alpha})}
, where
\alpha^{i}_{*}
is the induced diffeomorphism

\alpha^{i}_{*}\colon\mathcal{S}^{1}_{v^{i}_{j}}S\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{1}_{v^{\prime i}_{j(\bm{\alpha})}}S^{\prime};

(5)

\bm{\beta}(q_{j})=q_{j}^{\prime}
and
\bm{\beta}\circ\pi^{\prime}\circ\bm{\alpha}=\pi
.

We stress that in the definition above the tuples
\bm{v}^{i}=\{v^{i}_{1},\ldots v^{i}_{l_{i}}\}
are unordered (i.e., reordering these collections in the definition above does not change the class of a branched cover building).

Remark 2.17
.

A matching marker as above can be interpreted as a choice of gluing between two cylindrical ends corresponding to a given node.

We denote by
\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
the set of all branched cover buildings.

Definition 2.18
.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
be the subset of
\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
consisting of
branched cover buildings with smooth levels
by which we mean those branched cover buildings

\left(\bm{C},S=S_{1}\cup\ldots\cup S_{n},\pi,(\bm{v}^{1},\ldots,\bm{v}^{n-1}),(\bm{m}^{1},\ldots,\bm{m}^{n-1})\right)

for which the only nodes of
S
are the special nodes connecting different levels, i.e., those nodes which are contained in the tuples
(\bm{v}^{1},\ldots,\bm{v}^{n-1})
.

Remark 2.19
.

As per Remark
2.8
, the space of branched cover buildings with smooth levels
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
is of main interest to us as we will be able to show that it is enough to consider only such types of domains for moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic curves involved in the definition of Heegaard Floer symplectic cohomology; see Theorem
2.38
for details.

We now discuss the relationship between Harris-Mumford compactification via admissible covers
\accentset{\circ}{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
and
\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
, continuing the discussion in Remarks
2.10
and
2.15
. In particular, we give a more precise description of the role of the matching data.

As mentioned in Remark
2.15
there is a fiber bundle

(2.43)

\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\rightarrow\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}.

We claim that this map extends to

(2.44)

\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\rightarrow\accentset{\circ}{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}

and fits into the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\accentset{\circ}{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{-\chi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,-\chi+2}.}

As in the case of the Hurwitz scheme, there is a map

(2.45)

\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\chi,\ell(\bm{\mu})+\ell(\bm{\mu}^{\prime})},

defined by taking the domain of a branched cover. Here
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\chi,\ell(\bm{\mu})+\ell(\bm{\mu}^{\prime})}
is the moduli space of nodal, possibly disconnected curves of Euler characteristic
\chi
with
\ell(\bm{\mu})+\ell(\bm{\mu}^{\prime})
marked points (recall that
\ell(\bm{\mu})
refers to the length of the partition
\bm{\mu}
).

Given two nodal curves
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
with
n_{1}+k
and
n_{2}+k
marked points one may produce a new curve
C
by taking the disjoint union of
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
and identifying the last
k
marked points of both curves. This construction induces the
attaching map

(2.46)

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\chi_{1},n_{1}+k}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\chi_{2},n_{2}+k}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\chi_{1}+\chi_{2},n_{1}+n_{2}}.

The attaching map gives rise to the description of boundary strata for both
\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
and
\accentset{\circ}{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
which arises from the following diagram (we draw the diagram only for
\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
)

(2.47)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\chi_{1},\ell(\bm{\mu})+\ell(\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime})}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\chi_{2},\ell(\bm{\mu}^{\prime})+\ell(\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\chi,\ell(\bm{\mu})+\ell(\bm{\mu}^{\prime})}.}

The maps induced by the attaching map fit into the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\accentset{\circ}{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\accentset{\circ}{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\accentset{\circ}{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}.}

Note that to define a map given by the arrow in the top row of the diagram above one has to assign matching data based on asymptotic markers at punctures corresponding to
\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}
-profiles, unlike the arrow in the bottom row where all this data is not present.

Definition 2.20
.

We define

(2.48)

{\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times_{\bm{m}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}}\subset\partial\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}

to be the part of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
consisting of branched cover buildings with smooth levels of height
2

\left(\bm{C}=(C_{1},C_{2}),S=S_{1}\cup S_{2},\pi,\bm{v}=\{v_{1}^{1},\ldots,v^{1}_{\ell(\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime})}\},\bm{m}=\{m_{1}^{1},\ldots,m_{\ell(\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime})}^{1}\}\right)

with:

•

\chi(S_{1})=\chi_{1}
,
\chi(S_{2})=\chi_{2}
and
\chi=\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}
;

•

ramification indices of
\pi
at nodes
\bm{v}
constituting a partition
\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}
which appears in the notation (
2.48
).

We point out that an element of
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times_{\bm{m}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}
, unlike an element of the direct product
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}
, possesses a choice of asymptotic markers at nodes corresponding to cylindrical ends with assigned partition
\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}
, i.e. to the nodes
\bm{v}
. On the contrary, there is a natural “forgetful” covering

(2.49)

bf^{1}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times_{\bm{m}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}

given by matching the negative asymptotic markers of a given element of
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}
with corresponding (via the order on punctures) positive asymptotic markers of
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}
and forgetting these two sets of asymptotic markers.
Explicitly, given a pair

(2.50)

\left((S_{1},\pi_{1},\bm{p}^{\pm}_{1},\bm{a}^{\pm}_{1},B_{1}),(S_{2},\pi_{2},\bm{p}^{\pm}_{2},\bm{a}^{\pm}_{2},B_{2})\right)\in\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}

We set
\bm{C}
to be simply a union of two cylinders
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
with one special node. Further, we set
S
to be the union of
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
glued along pairs of punctures
v^{1}_{i}=\{p^{-}_{1i},p^{+}_{2i}\}
. To each such pair, we associate a matching marker

(2.51)

m^{1}_{i}=a^{-}_{1i}\otimes a^{+}_{2i}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}_{\{p^{-}_{1i},p^{+}_{2i}\}}S=(T_{p_{1i}^{-}}\overline{S_{1}}\otimes T_{p_{2i}^{+}}\overline{S_{2}}\setminus\{0\})/\mathbb{R}.

Then the map (
2.49
) sends a pair as in (
2.50
) to

(\bm{C}=(C_{1},C_{2}),S=S_{1}\cup S_{2},\pi=\pi_{1}\cup_{\bm{v}}\pi_{2},\bm{v}=\{v^{1}_{1},\ldots,v^{1}_{\ell(\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime})}\},\bm{m}=\{m^{1}_{1},\ldots,m^{1}_{\ell(\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime})}\}).

Essentially, the subspace
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times_{\bm{m}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
is the image of
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}
under the top row map in the diagram (
2.47
). It is evident that this forgetful map is an
N_{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}
-sheeted covering (see (
2.3
)). The reason for this is that for any matching
m_{i}^{1}
there are
\mu^{\prime\prime}_{i}
pairs of asymptotic markers that are sent to
m^{1}_{i}
under (
2.51
) and there are
R_{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}
way to give an order on the nodes of an element of
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times_{\bm{m}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}
.

Similarly, for the space of branched cover buildings with smooth levels of height
n

\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}_{1}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times_{\bm{m}^{1}}\ldots\times_{\bm{m}^{n-1}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}_{n}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{n}},

defined in a similar fashion to Definition
2.20
, there is forgetful covering

(2.52)

bf^{n-1}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}_{1}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\ldots\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}_{n}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{n}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}_{1}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times_{\bm{m}^{1}}\ldots\times_{\bm{m}^{n-1}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}_{n}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{n}}

of degree
N_{\bm{\mu}_{1}^{\prime\prime}}\cdot\ldots\cdot N_{\bm{\mu}_{n}^{\prime\prime}}
.

We now give an interpretation of the matchings in terms of local deformations following
[
Ion02
]
. In particular, we claim that the neighborhood of the image of
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}
is smooth.

We pick some element

(2.53)

\left(\left(\pi_{1}\colon S_{1}\rightarrow C_{1}\right),\left(\pi_{2}\colon S_{2}\rightarrow C_{2}\right)\right)\in\mathcal{H}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}^{\mu,\mu^{\prime\prime}}\times_{\bm{m}}\mathcal{H}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}^{\mu^{\prime\prime},\mu^{\prime}}.

Recall that the normal bundle to the boundary stratum containing
(S_{1},S_{2})\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\chi,\ell(\mu)+\ell(\mu^{\prime})}
given by

(2.54)

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\ell(\mu^{\prime\prime})}L^{*}_{p_{1,i}^{-}}\otimes L^{*}_{p_{2,i}^{+}}

at this point has fiber

(2.55)

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\ell(\mu^{\prime\prime})}T_{p_{1,i}^{-}}S_{1}\otimes T_{p_{2,i}^{+}}S_{2}.

A section of
L^{*}_{q_{-\infty}}\otimes L^{*}_{\tilde{q}_{+\infty}}
given by
\zeta\in T_{q_{-}\infty}C_{1}\otimes T_{q_{+}\infty}C_{2}
sets a restriction on sections of (
2.54
) which give rise to the deformation of the point (
2.53
) regarded as a point of
\accentset{\circ}{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
as shown in
[
Ion02
]
. More specifically, such a section given by

(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{\ell(\mu^{\prime\prime})})\in\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\ell(\mu^{\prime\prime})}T_{p_{1,i}^{-}}S_{1}\otimes T_{p_{2,i}^{+}}S_{2}

has to satisfy

(2.56)

\zeta=a_{1}\xi_{1}^{\mu^{\prime\prime}_{1}}=\ldots=a_{\ell(\mu^{\prime\prime})}\xi^{\mu^{\prime\prime}_{\ell(\mu^{\prime\prime})}}_{\ell(\mu^{\prime\prime})},

where
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell(\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime})}\in\mathbb{C}
.
We now recall that in the context of
\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
the section
\tilde{\zeta}
as in (
2.36
) giving rise to local perturbation of
(C_{1},C_{2})\in\mathcal{C}_{b,2}
has to satisfy
\varphi(\tilde{\zeta})=0
. We then claim that a section of (
2.54
) produces a point of
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
near (
2.53
) whenever

(2.57)

\tilde{\zeta}=a_{1}\xi_{1}^{\mu^{\prime\prime}_{1}}=\ldots=a_{\ell(\mu^{\prime\prime})}\xi^{\mu^{\prime\prime}_{\ell(\mu^{\prime\prime})}}_{\ell(\mu^{\prime\prime})}.

At last, we claim that given a matching marker
m^{1}_{j}
as in Definition
2.20
there is a unique choice of
\xi_{j}
satisfying (
2.57
) with
[\xi_{j}]=m^{1}_{j}
in
\mathcal{S}^{1}_{v^{i}_{j}}S
.

Hence, the coordinate chart (
2.35
) (and, moreover, the chart (
2.38
)) near the boundary of the space of cylindrical buildings gives lifts to coordinate charts near the corresponding portions of the boundary of
\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
.

Notice that
{\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times_{\bm{m}}\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}
is a smooth manifold, as there are no non-trivial automorphisms of a curve fixing a marked point and an asymptotic marker at this point.

We conclude this discussion with the following theorem and refer the reader to
[
HM82
,
Moc95
,
Ion02
]
for further details.

Theorem 2.21
.

The space
\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
is a compact metric space and serves as a compactification of the space
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
, i.e., it coincides with the closure of
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
viewed as a subspace of
\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
. Moreover, there is a smooth normal neighborhood near the space of height
2
buildings with smooth levels

\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times_{\bm{m}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}

diffeomorphic to

(2.58)

\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times_{\bm{m}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}\times[0,+\infty].

More generally, there is a normal neighborhood of

\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}_{1}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times_{\bm{m}^{1}}\ldots\times_{\bm{m}^{n-1}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}_{n}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{n}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}

diffeomorphic to

(2.59)

\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}_{1}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times_{\bm{m}^{1}}\ldots\times_{\bm{m}^{n-1}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}_{n}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{n}}\times[0,+\infty]^{n-1}.

Remark 2.22
.

We note that the whole
\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
is not a manifold with corners or even an orbifold since there may be non-special nodes corresponding to bubbles on the cylindrical side. Nevertheless, the choice of matching data allows us to get a smooth structure on the preimage of smooth cylinders
\widetilde{C}_{-\chi}
. Recall that in the algebraic setting to get a smooth stack serving as compactification of
\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
one should add
twisted covers
as introduced in
[
ACV03
]
and more generally in
[
Deo14
]
. Working with
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{-\chi}
instead of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,\chi+2}
allows us to avoid introducing “stacky” covers.

We will refer to
[0,+\infty]^{n-1}
part in (
2.59
) as
neck coordinates
.
For further use, we also discuss orientations on Hurwitz spaces. Any space
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
is naturally oriented as it is a cover space over
\text{Conf}_{-\chi}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,\infty\})
which is a complex manifold. The choice of vertical direction
\partial_{s}
leads to a further natural choice of orientation on
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
. We fix such choices on all spaces
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
.

We then claim that boundary orientation on the image of
{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}
under the map described in (
2.47
) coincides with the product orientation. In other words, there is a natural isomorphism

(2.60)

|v|\otimes|{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}|\otimes|\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}|\cong|\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}|

where
v
is an outward pointing vector (corresponding to the normal direction described in (
2.58
)). Here we use conventions discussed in Section
2.14
.

Remark 2.23
.

We point out that assuming an order on the set of branched points as in the Definition
2.13
allows us to get a description of the portion of the boundary of
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
which is of interest to us. Nevertheless, in practice, it leads to “overcounting” geometric curves connecting tuples of Hamiltonian orbits. We ignore this disadvantage since Heegaard Floer symplectic cohomology is eventually defined over
\mathbb{Q}
. To eliminate this nuance one could use the approach of
[
Moc95
]
towards compactification of Hurwitz scheme with unordered branched points.

2.6.
Review of branched manifolds

We start by reviewing the general framework of
weighted nonsingular branched groupoids
from
[
McD06
]
.

Denote the spaces of objects and morphisms of a
small topological category
\mathscr{X}
by the letters
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
respectively. The source and
target maps are denoted via
s,t\colon X_{1}\rightarrow X_{0}
, and the identity map is given by
x\mapstochar\rightarrow\text{id}_{x}
is

\operatorname{id}\colon X_{0}\rightarrow X_{1}
.
The composition map is set as

m\colon{X_{1}}\;_{s}\!\times_{t}X_{1}\;\rightarrow\;X_{1},\quad(f,g)\mapstochar\rightarrow f\circ g.

The space of morphisms from
x
to
y
is denoted by
\text{Hom}(x,y)
.

Definition 2.24
.

A
nonsingular (oriented) sse (smooth, stable, étale) groupoid with corners

\mathscr{X}
is a small topological category such that the following conditions hold:

•

(Groupoid)
All morphisms are invertible, i.e., there is a structure map
\iota\colon X_{1}\rightarrow X_{1}
that takes each
f\in X_{1}
to its inverse
f^{-1}
.

•

(Smooth)
The spaces
X_{0}
of objects and
X_{1}
of morphisms are (oriented) manifolds with corners, and all structure maps (
s,t,m,\iota,\text{id}
) are smooth (and orientation-preserving).

•

(Étale)
If in addition to the above all structure maps are local diffeomorphisms.

•

(Nonsingular)
For each
x\in X_{0}
the set of self-morphisms
\text{Hom}(x,x)\eqqcolon G_{x}
is trivial (
stability
is a weaker condition which only requires finiteness of these groups).

We denote by
\mathscr{X}^{\vee}
the quotient space of a small topological category groupoid
\mathscr{X}
given by
X_{0}/\sim
, where
x\sim x^{\prime}
if
\text{Hom}(x,x^{\prime})
is non-empty.
Denote by
\pi
the projection
X_{0}\rightarrow\mathscr{X}^{\vee}
.

Further, we will simply call
\mathscr{X}
a
nonsingular sse groupoid
if
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are manifolds (without boundary or corners).

Let us recall that any topological space
Y
admits a maximal Hausdorff quotient
(f,Y_{H})
, i.e., a projection
f\colon Y\rightarrow Y_{H}
to a Hausdorff space
Y_{H}
, such that any other continuous surjection
f^{\prime}\colon Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
with
Y^{\prime}
Hausdorff factors through
f
.

For a given sse groupoid with boundary
\mathscr{B}
denote by
\check{\pi}_{H}
the projection
\mathscr{B}^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}^{\vee}_{H}

and by
\pi_{H}
the projection
B_{0}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}^{\vee}_{H}
. Furthermore,
U_{H}=\pi_{H}(U)
denotes the image
of
U\subset B_{0}
in
\mathscr{B}^{\vee}_{H}
.

Definition 2.25
.

A
weighted nonsingular branched groupoid with corners
(or
wnbc groupoid
for short) is a pair
(\mathscr{B},\Lambda)
consisting of an oriented, nonsingular sse groupoid with boundary together with a weighting function

\Lambda\colon\mathscr{B}^{\vee}_{H}\rightarrow(0,\infty)
that satisfies the following
compatibility conditions.
For each
p\in\mathscr{B}^{\vee}_{H}
there is an open neighborhood
N
of
p
in
\mathscr{B}^{\vee}_{H}
, a collection
U_{1},\ldots,U_{\ell}
of disjoint open subsets of
\pi_{H}^{-1}(N)\subset B_{0}
(called
local branches
) and a set of positive weights
m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}
such that:

•

\check{\pi}_{H}^{-1}(N)=U_{1}^{\vee}\cup\ldots\cup U_{\ell}^{\vee}\subset\mathscr{B}^{\vee}
;

•

for each
i=1,\ldots,\ell
the projection
\pi_{H}\colon U_{i}\rightarrow(U_{i}^{\vee})_{H}
is a homeomorphism onto a relatively closed subset of
N
;

•

for all
q\in N
,
\Lambda(q)
is the sum of the weights of the local
branches whose image contains
q
:

\Lambda(q)=\underset{i\colon q\in\,(U_{i}^{\vee})_{H}}{\sum}m_{i}.

We call
(\mathscr{B},\Lambda)
a
wnb groupoid
if
\mathscr{B}
is a nonsingular sse groupoid (i.e., with no boundary).

Later, we also use the notion of an
orbibundle
over a wnb groupoid
\rho\colon\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
. This can be thought of as a vector bundle
\rho_{0}\colon E_{0}\rightarrow B_{0}
with
E_{1}
representing identifications between fibers
E_{x}
and
E_{y}
for any
f\in\text{Hom}(x,y)
; see
[
McD06
]
for details. A section of such a bundle is a smooth functor
\sigma\colon\mathscr{B}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
.
A section
\sigma
is said to be transverse to the zero section if
\sigma(B_{0})
is
transverse to the zero set
of
E_{0}
and
d\sigma\colon T_{x}\mathscr{B}\rightarrow E_{x}
is surjective (here
T\mathscr{B}
is the naturally defined tangent bundle of a wnb groupoid
\mathscr{B}
) for any intersection point
x\in B_{0}
. Notice that a structure of a wnb groupoid on
\mathscr{B}
induces a structure of a wnb groupoid in the zero set
\mathscr{Z}(\sigma)
for transversal
\sigma
.

2.7.
Branched structure on Hurwitz spaces.

In this section, we define a wnbc groupoid
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
by means of an inductive procedure. The motivation behind the construction to follow is discussed in Section
2.8
.

(a)

Figure 4
.
Here is a toy picture of
\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{(2),(1,1)}_{2,-3}
, i.e., we have
\kappa=2,\,\chi=-3,\,\bm{\mu}=(2),\,\bm{\mu}^{\prime}=(1,1)
, and the interior
\mathcal{H}^{(2),(1,1)}_{2,-3}
is in gray. The black horizontal ray corresponds to a portion of the boundary associated with
\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}=(2)
and the vertical ray corresponds to
\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}=(1,1)
. We also regard it as a cartoon for Step 0 in our construction.

Step
k=0
.
We start with
(\mathscr{B},\Lambda)
, where
B_{0}=\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
,
B_{1}
consists only of
\text{id}_{x}
for
x\in B_{0}
and
\Lambda\equiv 1
. Here, the convention is that
B_{0}
denotes the objects of
\mathscr{B}
and
B_{1}
denotes its morphisms. We then modify
(\mathscr{B},\Lambda)
in the next steps.

Step
k=1
: adding buildings of height
2
.

Recall that by Theorem
2.21
for any codimension-
1
portion of the boundary
\partial\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
consisting of smooth buildings of height
2
of the form

\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times_{\bm{m}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}
(for any pair
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
such that
\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}=\chi
) there is a neighborhood of the form

\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times_{\bm{m}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}\times(R;+\infty]

for any
R\geq 0
. We then introduce a “thickened” forgetful covering

(2.61)

\overline{bf}^{1}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}\times(R;+\infty]\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times_{\bm{m}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}\times(R;+\infty],

which is equal to
(bf^{1},\operatorname{Id})
. We also pick some
R^{\prime}>R
.
Set

U_{0}=\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\setminus\bigcup_{\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}=\chi,\,\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}\vdash\kappa}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times_{\bm{m}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}\times[R^{\prime};+\infty)

We update
(\mathscr{B},\Lambda)
by putting

(2.62)

B_{0}^{\text{new}}=U_{0}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}=\chi,\,\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}\vdash\kappa}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}\times(R;+\infty].

We then set the manifold
B_{1}^{\text{new}}
to record the following identifications corresponding to the thickened forgetful maps. Namely given a point
x\in\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}\times(R;R^{\prime})\subset B_{0}
we then set
\operatorname{Hom}(x,\overline{bf}^{1}(x))
and
\operatorname{Hom}(\overline{bf}^{1}(x),x)
each to consist of one arrow, and these arrows are meant to be inverses of one another. We then complete
B_{1}^{\text{new}}
by adding all the necessary arrows to make it into a groupoid. The weight function
\Lambda^{\text{new}}
is set to be equal to
1
on the closure of
\pi_{H}^{\vee}(U_{0})
and equal to
\frac{1}{N_{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}}
on the image of
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{2}}\times(R^{\prime};+\infty]
under
\pi_{H}^{\vee}
for any
\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}=\chi
and
\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}\vdash\kappa
(clearly all of these images are pairwise disjoint and all of them are disjoint from the closure of
\pi_{H}^{\vee}(U_{0})
). Evidently, the updated pair
(\mathscr{B}^{\text{new}},\Lambda^{\text{new}})
is a wnbc groupoid.

Inductive step
k=n
: adding buildings of height
k+1
.

In our construction, we obtain
B_{0}^{\text{new}}
from
B_{0}^{\text{old}}
(the one we got in step
k-1
) by truncating a portion of
B_{0}^{\text{old}}
and adding a disjoint union of all possible “thickened” spaces of buildings of height
k+1
. Given that, let us consider

(2.63)

\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{1}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\ldots\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k-1},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{k}}\times(R;+\infty]^{k-1}\subset B_{0}^{\text{old}},\,\chi_{1}+\ldots+\chi_{k}=\chi.

Given
i
such that
1\leq i\leq k
,
\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}\vdash\kappa
, and a pair
\chi_{i}^{\prime},\chi_{i}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\chi_{i}^{\prime}+\chi_{i}^{\prime\prime}=\chi_{i}
, there is a subset of the above of the form

(2.64)

\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{1}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\ldots\times(\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{i},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{i}^{\prime}}\times_{\bm{m}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{i+1}}_{\kappa,\chi_{i}^{\prime\prime}})\times\ldots\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k-1},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{k}}\times(R;+\infty)^{i-1}\times(R^{\prime};+\infty]\times(R;+\infty]^{k-i}

by Theorem
2.21
.
We then set
U_{0}
to be equal to
B_{0}^{\text{old}}
with subsets of type (
2.64
) subtracted from all the possible subsets of the form (
2.63
) in
B_{0}^{\text{old}}
. Furthermore, we put

(2.65)

B_{0}^{\text{new}}=U_{0}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi_{1}+\ldots+\chi_{k+1}=\chi,\\
\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}\vdash\kappa\end{subarray}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{1}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\ldots\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{k+1}}\times(R;+\infty]^{k}

As in Step
1
there are thickened forgetful maps

(2.66)

\displaystyle\overline{bf}^{1}_{i}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{1}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\ldots\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{k+1}}\times(R;+\infty]^{i-1}\times(R,R^{\prime})\times(R,+\infty]^{k-i}\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{1}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\ldots\times(\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{i},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{i}^{\prime}}\times_{\bm{m}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{i+1}}_{\kappa,\chi_{i}^{\prime\prime}})\times\ldots\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k-1},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{k}}\times(R;+\infty]^{i-1}\times(R,R^{\prime})\times(R;+\infty]^{k-i}

We set
B_{1}
to track identifications between points with the same images under these maps. At last,
\Lambda^{\text{new}}
is set to coincide with
\Lambda^{\text{old}}
at the points of
U_{0}
and is set to be equal to
\frac{1}{N_{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot N_{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}}}
on the portion of the image under
\pi_{H}^{\vee}
of

(2.67)

\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{1}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\ldots\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{k+1}}\times(R;+\infty]^{k}

disjoint from
\overline{\pi_{H}^{\vee}(U_{0})}\subset\mathscr{B}_{H}^{\vee}
.

The verification that the resulting
(\mathscr{B},\Lambda)
is indeed a wnbc groupoid is straightforward.

(a)

Figure 5
.
Step 1 of the construction of the wnbc groupoid
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{(2),(1,1)}_{2,-3}
is shown here. For
\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}=(1,1)
we have
N_{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}=2
and it counts the number of ways to order a set of two orbits. For
\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}=(2)
we have
N_{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}=2
and it counts the number of ways to choose an asymptotic marker on a time-
2
orbit. Therefore, the construction provides four boundary neighborhoods given in blue. The weight function takes the value
\Lambda=\frac{1}{2}
in each of these regions.
The red curves depict
\mathscr{M}^{-3}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
for some
\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}
.
