In each of the blue regions, the Floer data introduced in Section
2.8
force the red projection to be slightly altered.

The wnbc groupoid obtained after the termination of the process above is further denoted by
(\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi},\Lambda_{\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}})
. We denote the interior wnb groupoid (obtained by throwing out the boundary at the level of
B_{0}
) by
\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
.

There is a natural projection

(2.68)

\mathscr{BF}\colon(\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi})^{\vee}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}

induced by forgetful maps. That is, for any
x\in(\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi})^{\vee}
we may pick a maximal height
k
such that
x
belongs to a portion of

\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{1}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\ldots\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{k+1}}\times(R;+\infty]^{k}

left in our inductive construction and we then send
x
to
bf^{k}(x)
. These locally defined maps clearly patch into a continuous map
\mathscr{BF}
.

We will use the notation
\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
to denote the branched structure on
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
induced by
\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
.

Remark 2.26
.

The reasoning for this construction is provided in the next section. Roughly speaking, for breaking of profile
\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}
one might want to have not only matching data and a non-decreasing order of entries of
\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}
but a choice of actual markers (there are
\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}!
choices) and a total order on partition
\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}
(there are
R_{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}}
such orders).

Remark 2.27
.

We point out that this branched structure can be extended to the whole of
\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
.

2.8.
Floer data

For each
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
and all elements
(S,\pi,\bm{p}^{\pm},\bm{a}^{\pm},B)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
we choose a
cylindrical ends data
on
S
such that
\bm{p}^{+}
is the set of
inputs
and
\bm{p}^{-}
is the set of
outputs
, i.e., a collection of holomorphic embeddings
\{\delta^{i}_{+}\}_{i=1}^{i=\ell(\bm{\mu})}
and
\{\delta^{i}_{-}\}_{i=1}^{i=\ell(\bm{\mu}^{\prime})}
satisfying compatibility conditions that vary smoothly:

•

\delta_{+}^{i}\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq R_{i}^{+}}\times\mathbb{R}/\mu_{i}\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow S,\,\lim\limits_{s\rightarrow+\infty}\delta_{+}^{i}(s,\cdot)=p_{i}^{+},\,\lim\limits_{s\rightarrow+\infty}-\frac{d}{ds}\delta_{+}^{i}(s,0)=a_{i}^{+};

•

compatibility with
\pi
:
\pi(\delta_{+}^{i}(s,t))=(s,t)
, where

\pi\circ\delta_{+}^{i}\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq R_{i}^{+}}\times\mathbb{R}/2\mu_{i}\pi\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\pi\mathbb{Z};

•

\delta_{-}^{i}\colon\mathbb{R}_{\leq-R_{i}^{-}}\times\mathbb{R}/\mu_{i}^{\prime}\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow S,\,\lim\limits_{s\rightarrow+\infty}\delta_{-}^{i}(s,\cdot)=p_{i}^{-},\,\lim\limits_{s\rightarrow-\infty}-\frac{d}{ds}\delta_{+}^{i}(s,0)=a_{i}^{-}
;

•

compatibility with
\pi
:
\pi(\delta_{-}^{i}(s,t))=(s,t)
, where

\pi\circ\delta_{+}^{i}\colon\mathbb{R}_{\leq-R_{i}^{-}}\times\mathbb{R}/\mu_{i}^{\prime}\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}.

In particular, the constants
R_{i}^{+},R_{i}^{-}
vary smoothly across
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
.
It is required that the above choices are invariant under the
\mathbb{R}
-action on
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
which provides a choice of cylindrical ends data on
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
.

A choice of cylindrical ends data on
(\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi})^{\vee}_{H}
is similar to the above; one has to choose such data on objects
\left(\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\right)_{0}
making sure that it coincides for the pairs of points identified under morphisms
\left(\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\right)_{1}
. In particular, any cylindrical ends data on
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
provides cylindrical ends data on
\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
.

Now assume there are choices of cylindrical ends data for all
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi^{\prime}}
for all
\chi^{\prime}
with
\chi^{\prime}>\chi
. Then we say that a choice of cylindrical end data is
consistent
if for any (see (
2.65
))

(2.69)

([S=S_{1}\cup\ldots\cup S_{k+1},\pi],R_{1},\ldots,R_{k})\in\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{1}}_{\kappa,\chi_{1}}\times\ldots\times\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi_{k+1}}\times(R;+\infty]^{k}\subset B_{0}

with
R_{1},\ldots,R_{k}\gg 0
the cylindrical ends coincide with the ones induced from positive (resp. negative) ends of
S_{1}
(resp.
S_{k+1}
).

Furthermore, for a point of
[S^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}]\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
represented by (
2.69
) (by applying Theorem
2.21
to the image of (
2.69
) under the corresponding forgetful map
bf^{k}
) we may assign
finite cylinders
data. That is, the domain
S^{\prime}
comes with
\ell(\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{j})
necks of length
2R_{j}
induced by the gluing procedure described in (
2.34
) and (
2.57
) for each
j
with
1\leq j\leq k
.

Moreover, since
[S,\pi]
above is the element of a product of Hurwitz spaces the cylindrical end data induces trivializations of portions of the necks as above. Namely, let
p^{i,-}_{j}
be the
j
-th negative puncture of
S_{i}
and
p^{i+1,+}_{j}
be the
j
-th positive puncture of
S_{i+1}
. Both of the punctures come with associated cylindrical ends
\delta^{j}_{i,-}\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq R^{i,-}_{j}}\times\mathbb{R}/\mu^{\prime\prime}_{ij}\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow S^{\prime}
and
\delta^{j}_{i,+}\colon\mathbb{R}_{\leq R^{i,+}_{j}}\times\mathbb{R}/\mu^{\prime\prime}_{ij}\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow S^{\prime}
. Then under the gluing procedure dictated by the matching marker as in (
2.51
) a finite portion of
\delta^{j}_{i,-}
is glued to a finite portion of
\delta^{j}_{i,+}
. This gives rise to a
finite cylinder

•

\delta^{j}_{i,r}\colon[c_{r},d_{r}]\times\mathbb{R}/\mu^{\prime\prime}_{ij}\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow S
, which satisfies compatibility with
\pi^{\prime}
up to a vertical translation.

We then say that a choice of finite cylinders and cylindrical ends on
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
is a
consistent cylindrical data
if for all points
[S,\pi]\in(\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi})^{\vee}_{H}
represented by points of the form (
2.69
) the finite cylinders and cylindrical ends are induced from the boundary by the procedure above and these choices vary smoothly over
\left(\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\right)_{0}
. Here we assume that finite cylinders
\delta^{\cdot}_{i,r}
only show up for
R_{i}\geq R^{\prime}
(as in Section
2.6
) and for
R_{i}=R^{\prime}
they have length
0
.
We claim that such data exists and can be obtained inductively for any
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
.

Remark 2.28
.

We point out that only for
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
we can associate finite cylinders near the boundary with cylindrical ends of points in the boundary of
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
. This would not be possible for
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
as boundary components do not come naturally with cylindrical ends for all levels.

Definition 2.29
.

A
Floer datum
on
[S,\pi]\in\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
is a collection
(H_{(S,\pi)},F_{(S,\pi)},J_{(S,\pi)})
where (note that we make here the same choices for all
(S,\pi)
representing the same point in
\left(\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\right)^{\vee}_{H}
):

(1)

H_{(S,\pi)}\colon S\rightarrow\mathcal{H}(\hat{M})
is a domain-dependent Hamiltonian function, required to coincide with
H_{0}
on the cylindrical ends and finite cylinders;

(2)

H_{(S,\pi)}\colon S\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\hat{M})
is a domain-dependent function on
S
such that

•

for each cylinder, i.e., cylindrical end
\delta^{j}_{i,\pm}
or finite cylinder
\delta^{j}_{i,r}
, the function
F_{(S,\pi)}
coincides with
F_{\mu^{\prime\prime}_{ij},ord_{j}(\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{i})}
away from a small collar region (for a more precise description of collar regions and behavior of
F_{(S,\pi)}
see
[
Gan12
, Section 4]
);

•

F_{(S,\pi)}
is weakly monotonic, i.e.
\partial_{s}F_{(S,\pi)}\leq 0
on finite cylinders and cylindrical ends;

•

F_{(S,\pi)}
is locally constant away from finite cylinders and cylindrical ends;

(3)

J_{(S,\pi)}\colon S\rightarrow\mathcal{J}_{\hat{M}}
is a domain-dependent almost complex structure coinciding with
J_{t}
as in (
2.19
) on each cylindrical end and on each finite cylinder.

Given some Floer data as above we introduce the function
H^{\text{tot}}_{(S,\pi)}=H_{(S,\pi)}+F_{(S,\pi)}
. We point out that
H^{\text{tot}}_{(S,\pi)}
coincides with
H^{\text{tot}}_{\mu_{i},ord_{i}(\bm{\mu})}
at the positive cylindrical end corresponding to
p_{i}^{+}
, with
H^{\text{tot}}_{\mu^{\prime}_{i},ord_{i}(\bm{\mu}^{\prime})}
at the negative cylindrical end corresponding to
p_{i}^{-}
and with
H^{\text{tot}}_{\mu^{\prime\prime}_{ij},ord_{j}(\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{i})}
on each finite cylinder
\delta^{j}_{i,r}
.

We further associate a
1
-form
K_{(S,\pi)}\in\Omega^{1}(S,C^{\infty}(\hat{M}))
of the form

(2.70)

K_{(S,\pi)}=H^{\text{tot}}_{(S,\pi)}\cdot\pi^{*}(dt).

The
1
-form
K_{(S,\pi)}
determines a vector-field-valued
1
-form
Y_{(S,\pi)}\in\Omega^{1}(S,C^{\infty}(T\hat{M}))
, such that
Y_{(S,\pi)}(\xi)
for
\xi\in TS
is the Hamiltonian vector field
of
K_{(S,\pi)}(\xi)
.

We denote by
X_{H^{\text{tot}}_{(S,\pi)}}
the domain-dependent Hamiltonian vector field associated with
H^{\text{tot}}_{(S,\pi)}
. For
K_{(S,\pi)}
as above the
1
-form
Y_{(S,\pi)}
is given by

(2.71)

Y_{(S,\pi)}=X_{H^{\text{tot}}_{(S,\pi)}}\pi^{*}(dt).

Following
[
Sei08a
, Section 9i]
and
[
Gan12
, Section 4]
we introduce the notion of
consistent Floer data
on
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
. That is, a choice of Floer datum for all
\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime},\chi
and for all
[S,\pi]\in\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
is said to be consistent if it varies smoothly over each
\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
and for
[S,\pi]\in\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
it coincides with the one induced from the boundary (in the charts of the form (
2.67
)).

Remark 2.30
.

One may extend Floer data to the full compactification
\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
by requiring that it is equal to a pair
(H_{0},J_{0})
at each bubble component. For instance, it can be accomplished by requiring that for each branched cover building given Floer datum is equal to the constant one in small neighborhoods of all ramification points.

Consider a universal family
\mathscr{S}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\rightarrow\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
(see
[
HM98
, Theorem 1.53]
for the algebraic analog) whose fiber over a point
[S,\pi]\in\left(\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\right)_{0}
is the domain
S
of the cover
\pi
.
Then a choice of consistent Floer data on
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
provides a tuple

H\in C^{\infty}(\mathscr{S}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi},\mathcal{H}(\hat{M})),\,F\in C^{\infty}(\mathscr{S}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi},C^{\infty}_{b}(\hat{M})),\,J\in C^{\infty}(\mathscr{S}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi},\mathcal{J}(\hat{M})).

We also have a
1
-form
K
associated with
H^{\text{tot}}=H+F
:

K\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathscr{S}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}/\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}}(\mathscr{S}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi},C^{\infty}(\hat{M})).

A vector-field-valued
1
-form
Y
is assigned to each such
K
(see (
2.71
)).
Consistency allows one to extend this tuple to an analogous tuple
(\widehat{H},\widehat{F},\widehat{J})
for
\penalty\ \widehat{\mathscr{S}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\penalty\ \rightarrow\penalty\ \widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
.

Given consistent Floer data one may assign a domain-dependent almost complex structure
\underline{J}^{H}
on
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times\hat{M}
as follows:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\underline{J}^{H}\text{ preserves $\{s\}\times\{t\}\times T\hat{M}$ and }J|_{T\hat{M}}=J_{(S,\pi),z}\text{ for }z\in S,\\
\underline{J}^{H}_{(S,\pi),\,z}(\partial_{s})=\partial_{t}+X_{H^{\text{tot}}_{(S,\pi)}(z)}\text{ for $z\in S$}.\end{array}\right.

Observe that
\underline{J}^{H}_{(S,\pi),\,z}
is
adjusted
in the sense of
[
BEHWZ03
]
to the stable Hamiltonian structure on
S^{1}\times\hat{M}
given by

(2.74)

(\omega^{H^{\text{tot}}_{(S,\pi)}(z)}=\omega+dH^{\text{tot}}_{(S,\pi)}(z)\wedge dt,\lambda^{H^{\text{tot}}_{(s,\pi)}(z)}=dt),

with the symplectic vector bundle and the Reeb vector field given by

(2.75)

\xi^{H^{\text{tot}}_{(S,\pi)}(z)}=T\hat{M}\,\,\,\text{ and }\,\,\,R^{H^{\text{tot}}_{(S,\pi)}(z)}=\partial_{t}+X_{H^{\text{tot}}_{(S,\pi)}(z)}.

2.9.
Pseudo-holomorphic curves

Let us pick two tuples of Hamiltonian orbits
\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}
as in Section
2.2
and consistent Floer data on
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
as in Section
2.6
and set
\bm{\mu}=\bm{\mu}(\bm{x})
and
\bm{\mu}^{\prime}=\bm{\mu}(\bm{x}^{\prime})
.

Consider the set of pairs

(2.76)

\Big([(S,\pi,\bm{p}^{\pm},\bm{a}^{\pm},B)]\in\left(\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\right)_{0},\,u=(\pi^{\prime},v)\colon S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times\hat{M}\Big)

such that

(2.77)

\begin{cases}[S,\pi^{\prime}]=[S,\pi];\\
(dv-Y_{(S,\pi)})^{0,1}=0,\text{ with respect to }J_{(S,\pi)};\\
\lim_{s\rightarrow+\infty}v\circ\delta_{i}^{+}(s,\cdot)=x_{i}(\cdot)\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,\ell(\bm{\mu});\\
\lim_{s\rightarrow-\infty}v\circ\delta_{i}^{-}(s,\cdot)=x_{i}^{\prime}(\cdot)\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,\ell(\bm{\mu}^{\prime}).\end{cases}

We say that the pairs

\Big([(S_{1},\pi_{1},\bm{p}^{\pm},\bm{a}^{\pm},B)],\,u=(\pi^{\prime}_{1},v_{1})\Big)\text{ and }\Big([(S_{2},\pi_{2},\bm{p}^{\pm},\bm{a}^{\pm},B)],\,u=(\pi^{\prime}_{2},v_{2})\Big)

are
equivalent
if
[S_{1},\pi_{1}]=[S_{2},\pi_{2}]
and
v_{1}=v_{2}
.

Lemma 2.31
.

A curve
u=(\pi,v)\colon S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times\hat{M}
with
[S,\pi]\in\left(\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\right)_{0}
is
\underline{J}^{H}_{(S,\pi)}
-holomorphic precisely when
v\colon S\rightarrow\hat{M}
satisfies

(2.78)

(dv-Y_{(S,\pi)})^{0,1}=0.

Proof.

This is a restatement of
[
Fab10
, Proposition 2.2]
.
∎

Remark 2.32
.

The consequence of Lemma
2.31
is that one may apply both the SFT technology and the Hamiltonian Floer approach depending on the circumstance.

We define
\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})

as a weighted branched groupoid with
\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})_{0}
given by the equivalence classes of pairs as in (
2.77
) (i.e., objects of this groupoid are equivalence classes of pairs of a branched cover and a pseudo-holomorphic curve up to
s
-translation). We write
\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
for the space of pairs as above, not equivalence classes.

The manifold
\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})_{1}
of morphisms and the weight function come from the wnbc groupoid structure on
\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
as explained in the discussion below and in Section
2.6
.

For a small open
V_{0}\subset\left(\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\right)_{0}
and the corresponding groupoid
\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}|_{V_{0}}
one may associate a trivial Banach fiber orbibundle
\mathscr{B}|_{\mathscr{V}}\rightarrow\mathscr{V}
whose fiber over
[(S,\pi,\bm{p}^{\pm},\bm{a}^{\pm},B)]\penalty\ \in\penalty\ \left(\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\right)_{0}
is the space
\mathscr{B}_{(S,\pi)}
of maps in
W^{1,p}_{\text{loc}}(S,\hat{M})
converging at infinity on cylindrical ends associated with
\bm{p}^{\pm}
to some pair
(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})
.
The groupoid
\mathscr{B}|_{\mathscr{V}}
inherits a wnb groupoid structure from
{\mathscr{V}}
. Further, there is a vector orbibundle
\mathscr{E}|_{\mathscr{V}}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}|_{\mathscr{V}}
with fibers
\mathscr{E}_{[S,\pi]}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}_{[S,\pi]}
given by
L^{p}(S,\Omega^{0,1}_{S}\otimes v^{*}T\hat{M})
, for
v\in\mathscr{B}_{[S,\pi]}
.
Then one may interpret
(dv-Y_{(S,\pi)})^{0,1}
as a section
\sigma
of this vector orbibundle and the zero-set of this section is the union of
\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})_{\mathscr{V}}
for all pairs
(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})
. The derivative of this section at
((S,\pi),u)
is a
linearized operator
:

D_{[S,\pi],u}\colon(T\mathscr{B}|_{\mathscr{V}})_{[S,\pi],u}=T_{[S,\pi]}\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\times T_{v}\mathscr{B}_{[S,\pi]}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow(\mathscr{E}_{[S,\pi]})_{u}.

Then
\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
is
transversely cut out
if for any such
U_{0}
the section
\sigma
is transverse in the sense of Section
2.6
. This is equivalent to the surjectivity of
D_{[S,\pi],u}
for any point in the zero set of
\sigma
. We refer the reader to
[
Sei08a
, Remark 9.4]
for the reasoning behind considering local orbibundle
\mathscr{B}|_{\mathscr{U}}
instead of global one
\mathscr{B}_{\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}}
(which would not be a Banach manifold).

Theorem 2.33
.

For a generic Floer data on
\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
the moduli space
\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
is a wnb groupoid.

Proof.

Case
\chi=0
.
First, consider the special case when
\chi=0
. The moduli space
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
is nonempty only if
\bm{\mu}=\bm{\mu}^{\prime}
and consists of a finite number of points each corresponding to a collection of
l(\bm{\mu})
cylinders
C_{1},\ldots,C_{l(\bm{\mu})}
. According to the classical transversality results in symplectic homology there is a comeagre set of perturbations of
F_{0}
which guarantee transversality for each of these cylinders alone (see, for instance,
[
AD14
, Section 8.6]
). Since, for a collection of disjoint cylinders the linear differential operator assigned to (
2.78
) splits there is a comeagre set of perturbations of
l(\bm{\mu})
copies of
F_{0}
(considered on different circles
\mathbb{R}/2\mu_{i}\pi\mathbb{Z}\cong S^{1}
).
Varying over all
\bm{\mu}\vdash\kappa
one may pick a collection of Hamiltonians in
{\mathcal{\bm{H}}}_{S^{1}}^{\kappa}(\hat{M})
such that transversality is achieved for each cylinder alone for all Hurwitz spaces as above. Notice that different permutations may have shared desired Hamiltonian function assigned to them (e.g.,
H^{\text{tot}}_{1,1}
), but the transversality as above still can be achieved since the intersection of comeagre sets is comeagre.

Case
\chi<0
.

We start by picking a family
\Omega_{[S,\pi]}\subset\text{Int}(S)
of open neighborhoods smoothly depending on
[(S,\pi,\bm{p}^{\pm},\bm{a}^{\pm},B)]\in(\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi})^{\vee}
, such that each such
\Omega_{[(S,\pi)]}
is disjoint from cylindrical ends
\delta^{i}_{\pm}
(in case of cylinder components that means the whole component) and finite cylinders
\delta^{j}_{i,r}
. We require that
\Omega_{[S,\pi]}
intersects non-trivially with every non-cylindrical component of
S
.

We assume that we are already given a consistent Floer data
(H,F,J)
such that its restriction to
\partial\left(\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\right)_{0}
guarantees regularity. Let
\mathscr{F}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
denote the groupoid of Floer data on
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
with fixed
F
. Following
[
Sei08a
, Section 9k]
we
now will pick a perturbation
(\delta H,\delta J)\in\mathscr{T}=T\mathscr{F}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
such that for perturbed Floer data the regularity holds for the interior of
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
as well (note that we are not going to perturb
F
). With
\delta H
there is an associated perturbation
\delta K
of the
1
-form
K
. We require that and
(\delta K,\delta J)
vanishes away from
\Omega
.

To each point
([S,\pi],u=(\pi^{\prime},v))\in\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})_{0}
there is an associated universal linearized operator
D_{\mathscr{T},[(S,\pi)],u}
which differs from
D_{[(S,\pi)]),u}
by additional terms responsible for varying Floer data (note that
D_{\mathscr{T},[(S,\pi)],u}
only depends on
v
-part of
u
). In a local trivialization, it is given by

D_{\mathscr{T},[(S,\pi)],u}\colon\mathscr{T}\times(T\mathscr{B}|_{\mathscr{U}})_{[S,\pi],u}\rightarrow(\mathscr{E}_{[S,\pi]})_{u},

(\delta K,\delta J,Z,X)\mapstochar\rightarrow(\delta Y)^{0,1}+\delta J\circ\frac{1}{2}(dv-Y)\circ j_{S}+D_{([S,\pi],u)}(Z,X).

Since
u
is somewhere injective by assumptions
2
and
3
on
H^{\text{tot}}_{m,j}
’s, the Sard-Smale theorem then would imply regularity if one shows the surjectivity of the above map.
The image of
D_{\mathscr{T},[(S,\pi)],u}
is closed since the last summand in the above is Fredholm. If
\eta
is an element of the cokernel it must be smooth on
\text{int}(S)
and satisfy
D^{*}_{([S,\pi],u)}\eta=0
. Additionally,

\int_{S}\langle(\delta Y)^{0,1},\eta\rangle=0

for all
\delta Y
. This implies that
\eta
has to vanish on all of
\Omega_{[S,\pi]}
(for computation see
[
Fab10
, Lemma 4.3]
).
By the unique continuation theorem, it has to vanish on all non-cylindrical components of
S
, and cylindrical components are dealt with in the previous case. Notice that for this argument to work it suffices to consider only the perturbations for which
\delta Y(\partial_{s})=0
in an appropriate local chart.

The transversality of the section then implies that there is a wnb groupoid structure on the zero set as pointed out in Section
2.6
.
∎

Remark 2.34
.

One could adopt the proof in
[
CM07
]
to show the transversality via the SFT approach. This could be used to extend our invariants to more generic stable Hamiltonian structures where modeling the general problem on Hurwitz spaces instead of Deligne-Mumford moduli spaces is still applicable.

Lemma 2.35
.

The dimension of
\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
for generic
(H,F,J)
is given by

(2.79)

\text{dim}_{\mathbb{R}}\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})=(n-2)\chi+|\bm{x}^{\prime}|-|\bm{x}|-1.

Proof.

Let
([(S,\pi)],u)
be a representative of an element of
\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})_{0}
. For
v
and fixed
S
one may apply standard results
[
Bou02
]
to get

(2.80)

\text{ind}(v)=n\chi+|\bm{x}^{\prime}|-|\bm{x}|.

Adding the dimension of the Hurwitz space
\text{dim}_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}=-2\chi-1
to
\text{ind}(v)
gives the result.
∎

Lemma 2.36
.

Assuming that
\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
is a wnb groupoid there is a canonical isomorphism

(2.81)

\big|\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})\big|\otimes|\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}|\otimes o_{\bm{x}}\cong\big|\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\big|\otimes o_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}\otimes\zeta^{n\chi}.

Proof.

We refer the reader to Section
2.14
for the notations in (
2.81
), e.g., the definition of
\zeta
. The proof follows immediately from
[
Abo10
, Lemma C.4]
.
∎

Now, in the case where
|\bm{x}^{\prime}|-|\bm{x}|+(n-2)\chi=1
, by permuting factors, we get an isomorphism

(2.82)

|\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}|\otimes o_{\bm{x}}\cong o_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}\bigl[-n\chi\bigr]\otimes\big|\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\big|\cong o_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}\bigl[(2-n)\chi\bigr],

where we note that there is no change of sign since
\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
is of even dimension. The last isomorphism in the row above comes from choosing standard orientation on
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
coming from the complex manifold structure on it. Hence, choosing the standard orientation on
\mathbb{R}
the above induces:

(2.83)

\partial_{u}\colon o_{\bm{x}}\rightarrow o_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}\bigl[(2-n)\chi\bigr].

We extend this map to any generator
[x]\hbar^{j}
of
SC^{*}_{\kappa,\text{psym}}(\hat{M})
by tensoring the above isomorphism with
\zeta^{(2-n)j}
and multiplying with
(-1)^{(2-n)j}
:

(2.84)

\partial_{u}\colon\zeta^{(2-n)j}\otimes o_{\bm{x}}\rightarrow\zeta^{(2-n)j}\otimes o_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}\bigl[(2-n)\chi\bigr]\cong o_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}\bigl[(2-n)(\chi-j)\bigr].

The sign appears from the Koszul sign corresponding to the following chain of isomorphisms:

(2.85)

\displaystyle|\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}|\otimes\zeta^{(2-n)j}\otimes o_{\bm{x}}\cong(-1)^{(2-n)j}\zeta^{(2-n)j}\otimes|\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}|\otimes o_{\bm{x}}\cong\zeta^{(2-n)j}\otimes o_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}\bigl[(2-n)\chi\bigr]\cong

\displaystyle\cong o_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}\bigl[(2-n)(\chi-j)\bigr].

Ghost bubbles and Kuranishi replacements.
For
n\geq 3
there is a technical difficulty when considering
1
-parameter families is a possible occurrence of ghost bubbles as discussed in
[
CHT20
]
. That is, a sequence of curves
\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{i=\infty}
, in
\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
, after passing to a subsequence, can limit to

(u_{\infty}=u_{\infty,1}\cup u_{\infty,2}),

where:

(i)

the
main part

u_{\infty,1}
is the union of components that are not ghost bubbles; each component of the
u_{\infty,1}
is somewhere injective; and

(ii)

u_{\infty,2}
is a union of
ghost bubbles
, i.e., locally constant maps where the domain is a possibly disconnected compact Riemann surface and
u_{\infty,2}
maps to points on
\operatorname{Im}(u_{\infty,1})
.

To overcome this issue, we excise the portion
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
that is close to breaking into
u_{\infty}=u_{\infty,1}\cup u_{\infty,2}
with
u_{\infty,2}\not=\varnothing
and replace it with a Kuranishi model
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
of a neighborhood of all possible
u_{\infty,1}\cup u_{\infty,2}
satisfying (i) and (ii).

This provides us with the
Kuranishi replacements
as in
[
CHT20
]

\mathscr{M}^{\chi,\sharp}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
,
\mathscr{M}^{\chi^{\prime},\sharp}
, and for all
\chi
,
\chi^{\prime}
,
\bm{x}
,
\bm{x}^{\prime}
, such that:

(1)

\mathscr{M}^{\chi,\sharp}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
are transversely cut out branched manifolds;

(2)

\mathscr{M}^{\chi^{\prime},\sharp}
is a transversely cut out branched manifold that consists of perturbed holomorphic maps from connected compact Riemann surfaces which are homologous to a constant map; and

(3)

all the strata of
\partial\mathscr{M}^{\chi,\sharp}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
are finite fiber products of moduli spaces of the form
\mathscr{M}^{\chi_{i},\sharp}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x}_{i};\bm{x}_{i+1})
, and
\mathcal{M}^{\chi_{j},\sharp}
.

From now on for
n\geq 3
we pass to necessary Kuranishi replacements for
\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
and omit
\sharp
from the notation. This, in particular, affects the discussion to follow in Section
2.11
.

For a thorough discussion on Kuranishi replacement we refer the reader to the works of Fukaya-Oh-Ohta-Ono
[
FOOO09
,
FOOO09a
,
FOOO20
]
and McDuff-Wehrheim
[
MW17
,
McD19
]
. (Also see virtual fundamental cycle techniques of Hofer-Wysocki-Zehnder
[
HWZ17
]
and Pardon
[
Par16
]
.)

2.10.
Compactness

We first show that

Lemma 2.37
.

For a generic choice of Floer data
(H,F,J)
, given
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}
the groupoid
\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
is empty for all but finitely many tuples
\bm{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}
.

Proof.

For a representative
([(S,\pi)],u=(\pi,v))
of an element of
\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
, the curve
v
is a solution of perturbed Cauchy-Riemann equation (
2.78
). Therefore, the general framework of the proof given in
[
Abo10
, Lemma 5.2]
can be applied. That is, according to
[
Gan12
, Lemma A.1]
for
R
large enough the tuple
\bm{x}^{\prime}
with all its orbits lying in
[R,+\infty)\times\partial M
has (very) negative action
\mathcal{A}(\bm{x}^{\prime})
. Hence, one can apply
[
Gan12
, Theorem A.1]
for
v
connecting
\bm{x}
to any such
\bm{x}^{\prime}
to conclude that
\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
is empty. There are only finitely many tuples of oribts
\bm{x}^{\prime}
inside of any compact subset
\hat{M}
since all orbits are non-degenerate.
∎

Theorem 2.38
.

For a generic consistent choice of
(H,F,J)
, given
(n-2)\chi+|\bm{x}^{\prime}|-|\bm{x}|\leq 2
, the wnb groupoid
\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
admits a compactification
\overline{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
. When
(n-2)\chi+|\bm{x}^{\prime}|-|\bm{x}|=2
, the boundary takes the form

\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}=\chi,\\
(n-2)\chi_{1}+|\bm{x}^{\prime\prime}|-|\bm{x}|=(n-2)\chi_{2}+|\bm{x}^{\prime}|-|\bm{x}^{\prime\prime}|=1\end{subarray}}\mathscr{M}^{\chi_{1}}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime\prime})\times\mathscr{M}^{\chi_{2}}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x}^{\prime\prime};\bm{x}^{\prime}).

Proof.

For the entirety of the proof put
\bm{\mu}=\mu(\bm{x})
and
\bm{\mu}^{\prime}=\bm{\mu}(\bm{x}^{\prime})
. Define
\overline{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
to be a groupoid with
\overline{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})_{0}
consisting of pairs

\Big([(\bm{C},S=S_{1}\cup\ldots\cup S_{k},\pi,\{v^{i}_{j}\},\{m^{i}_{j}\})],\bm{u}=\{u_{1}\colon S_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times\hat{M},\ldots,u_{k}\colon S_{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times\hat{M}\}\Big),

where the former is a representative of an element of
\left(\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\right)_{0}
with possibly some
S_{i}
’s being disjoint unions of cylinders and each
u_{i}\in\mathscr{M}^{\chi(S_{i})}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x}_{i-1};\bm{x}_{i})_{0}
where
\bm{x}_{0}=\bm{x}
,
\bm{x}_{n}=\bm{x}^{\prime}
and the rest of
\bm{x}_{i}
are such that

(n-2)\chi+|\bm{x}^{\prime}|-|\bm{x}|=\text{ind}(u_{1})+\ldots+\text{ind}(u_{k}),

\chi=\chi(S_{1})+\ldots+\chi(S_{n})=\chi_{1}+\ldots+\chi_{k}.

The condition
(n-2)\chi+|\bm{x}^{\prime}|-|\bm{x}|\leq 2
suggests that actually
k\leq 2
since for generic
(K,J)
there are no index
0
curves.
We further denote such a pair as
\left([(\bm{C},S,\pi)],\bm{u}\right)
. The morphisms
\overline{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})_{1}
and the weight function
\Lambda
are induced from that on
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
(one can naturally extend it to those branched cover buildings where cylindrical levels are allowed) as in the case of
\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
; see Theorem
2.33
. We also refer to Figure
5
.

We show that it is indeed a compact closure of
\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
. By this, we mean that

(2.86)

\overline{\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})}=\overline{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime}).

Consider a sequence
([(S_{i},\pi_{i})],u_{i})\in\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
. First, we notice that by
[
Gan12
, Theorem A.1]
there exists a constant
C
depending only on
\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime}
and the choice of
F_{0}
and
F_{m,j}
as in
2
, such that any curve
u
in
\left(\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})\right)_{0}
has
\pi_{\hat{M}}(u)
contained in
\hat{M}\setminus(C;+\infty]\times\partial M
. Therefore the elements of the sequence
u_{i}
as above have their projections to
\hat{M}
contained in a compact region of
\hat{M}
. Additionally, curves
u_{i}
are
\underline{J}^{H}
-holomorphic as shown in Lemma
2.31
. Therefore the SFT compactness theorem of
[
BEHWZ03
]
can be applied.
For the compatibility of the SFT compactness with domain-dependent almost complex structures see
[
CM07
, Section 5]
and
[
Fab10
, Section 3]
.
Let
u_{\infty}
be an SFT limit of the above sequence. The domain of
u_{\infty}
must be an element of
[(\bm{C}_{\infty},S_{\infty},\pi_{\infty})]\in\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
(maybe with some additional inserted unbranched covers of cylinders or spheres). The curve
u_{\infty}
is then pseudo-holomorphic with respect to the almost complex structure described in Remark
2.30
.

First, we show that
(\bm{C}_{\infty},S_{\infty},\pi_{\infty})
is a smooth branched cover building. We notice that
u_{\infty}
restricted to any component of
S_{\infty}
which is a branched cover of some stable tree of spheres in
\bm{C}_{\infty}
must be constant since
\hat{M}
is an exact symplectic manifold. Therefore, any such ghost component may be collapsed and regarded as a collection of interior nodes of
S_{\infty}
. We then consider the restriction
u_{\infty}^{\text{res}}
of
u_{\infty}
to non-ghost components.

If
n=1
the index of
u_{\infty}^{\text{res}}
is at least
2
less than the index of
u_{i}
by Lemma
2.35
since there have to be at least two branching points in the target (on the
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
-side) of the ghost components.
If
n=2
the Euler characteristic does not affect the index of
u_{\infty}^{\text{res}}
. We may apply
[
ES22
, Theorem 1.5]
(see also
[
DW19
, Theorem 1.1]
) to conclude that the presence of ghost components forces the expected dimension of
u_{\infty}^{\text{res}}
to be at least
n=2
less than the index of
u_{i}
. Since
u_{\infty}^{\text{res}}
is transversely cut out this implies that no such ghosts could exist. At last, if
n\geq 3
we apply Kuranishi replacements we introduced in Section
2.9
to conclude that presence of closed components as in
2
would impose at least codimension
2
condition by considering closed curve pinchings as in
[
CHT20
, Lemma 3.9.3]
.

Therefore,
(\bm{C}_{\infty},S_{\infty},\pi_{\infty})\in\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
with possibly some levels consisting only of cylinders (i.e., has Euler characteristic zero). By consistency of the Floer data
(H,F,J)
the restriction of
u_{\infty}
to each of the levels of
S_{\infty}
is regular. If
\operatorname{ind}(u_{i})=(n-2)\chi+|\bm{x}^{\prime}|-|\bm{x}|=1
then we have that the limit is a building of height
1
. This and regularity implies that for fixed
\bm{x}
and
\bm{x}^{\prime\prime}
the space
\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
consists of finitely many points. Moreover, the groupoid

\overline{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})=\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})

is a manifold consisting of a finite number of points with
\Lambda
taking the value of
1
on all of these points. This can be obtained by choosing
R
big enough in the construction of
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
(see Section
2.6
).

If
\operatorname{ind}(u_{i})=2
, it follows that if there is more than
1
level in the limit, the curve
(\bm{C}_{\infty},S_{\infty},\pi_{\infty})
is a height
k=2
branched cover building
(u_{\infty}^{1},u_{\infty}^{2})
.

Let
u^{1}_{\infty}
connects
\bm{x}
with
\bm{x}^{\prime\prime}
, and
u^{2}_{\infty}
connects
\bm{x}^{\prime\prime}
with
\bm{x}^{\prime}
and none of
u^{1}_{\infty}
,
u^{2}_{\infty}
has domain with
\chi_{i}=0
. By Lemma
2.37
there are only finitely many
\bm{x}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}
for which such a pair
(u_{\infty}^{1},u_{\infty}^{2})
may exist. And as we already saw, for fixed
\bm{x}^{\prime\prime}
there are only finitely many such pairs.

Then we may conclude that for
(n-2)\chi+|\bm{x}^{\prime}|-|\bm{x}|=2
the boundary

\partial\overline{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})^{\vee}_{H}

is given by the union of images of

(2.87)

\mathscr{M}^{\chi_{1}}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime\prime})\times\mathscr{M}^{\chi_{2}}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x}^{\prime\prime};\bm{x}^{\prime})

in
\overline{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(H^{\text{tot}};J;\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})^{\vee}_{H}
where

\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}=\chi,

(n-2)\chi_{1}+|\bm{x}^{\prime\prime}|-|\bm{x}|=(n-2)\chi_{2}+|\bm{x}^{\prime}|-|\bm{x}^{\prime\prime}|=1.

The value of
\Lambda
on a stratum (
2.87
) equals
\frac{1}{N_{\bm{\mu}(\bm{x}^{\prime\prime})}}
if
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}<0
. Otherwise, it is equal to
1
since such a case corresponds to one of the levels being a collection of cylinders.

∎

From now on we will assume that
(H,F,J)
are generic enough to satisfy all the results above and drop it from the notations for respective moduli groupoids.

2.11.
The differential

We now define the differential on
SC_{\kappa,\text{psym}}^{*}(\hat{M})
by

(2.88)

\displaystyle d\colon SC_{\kappa,\text{psym}}^{*}(\hat{M})\rightarrow SC_{\kappa,\text{psym}}^{*+1}(\hat{M}),

\displaystyle d[\bm{x}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{x}^{\prime},\chi\colon|\bm{x}^{\prime}|-|\bm{x}|=1,\\
u\in\mathscr{M}^{\chi=0}(\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\partial_{u}([\bm{x}])+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{x}^{\prime},\chi\colon(n-2)\chi+|\bm{x}^{\prime}|-|\bm{x}|=1,\\
u\in\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\frac{\partial_{u}([\bm{x}])}{N_{\bm{\mu}(\bm{x}^{\prime})}},

where
[\bm{x}]\in o_{\bm{x}}^{\mathbb{Q}}
is a generator.
One can write
d
in the form

d=d^{0}+d^{1}\hbar+d^{2}\hbar^{2}+\ldots,

where
d_{i}
is a count of curves of Euler characteristic
-i
. The relation
d^{2}=0
is equivalent to an infinite system of equations on variables
d_{i}
’s starting with
d_{0}^{2}=0
. We summarize the discussion above via the following construction.

Construction 2.39
.

Given a cochain complex
(C^{*},d_{0})
over
\mathbb{Q}
and a sequence of maps
d_{i}\colon C^{*}\rightarrow C^{*+1-(n-2)i}
for
i\geq 1
satisfying the relations

(2.89)

d^{i}d^{0}+d^{i-1}d^{1}+\ldots+d^{1}d^{i-1}+d^{0}d^{i}=0,

we denote by
(\widetilde{C}^{*},d)
a cochain complex with

\widetilde{C}^{m}=\sum_{i-(n-2)j=m}C^{i}\hbar^{j},\text{ where }|\hbar|=2-n,

which can be regarded as a subspace in
C^{*}[[\hbar]]
. The differential
d
is given by the formula

d\coloneqq d^{0}+d^{1}\hbar+d^{2}\hbar^{2}+\ldots.

Clearly the relations (
2.89
) imply
d^{2}=0
. We also notice that one may obtain
(C^{*},d^{0})
by setting
\hbar=0
and we simply write this as
(\widetilde{C}^{*}|_{\hbar=0},d|_{\hbar=0})=(C^{*},d^{0})
.

Remark 2.40
.

Analogously, given a chain complex
(C_{*},\partial_{0})
and maps
\partial_{1},\partial_{2},\ldots
one similarly constructs a chain complex
(\widetilde{C}_{*},\partial)
.

Clearly,
SC^{*}_{\kappa}(\hat{M},d)
is an instance of the above construction and we are left to prove that relations (
2.89
) are satisfied.

Theorem 2.41
.

The differential
d
makes
SC^{*}_{\kappa,\text{psym}}(\hat{M})
into a chain complex.

Proof.

The right-hand side of (
2.88
) has non-zero terms for only finitely many
\bm{x}^{\prime}
due to Lemma
2.37
.
Moreover,
\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
is finite by Lemma
2.38
.

By Theorem
2.38
, given
(n-2)\chi+|\bm{x}^{\prime}|-|\bm{x}|=2
, the boundary of the compactification
\mathscr{M}^{\chi}(\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})
consists of a finite number of broken curves as in (
2.87
).

Then for generators
[\bm{x}]\in o_{\bm{x}}^{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\hbar^{-\chi}[\bm{x}^{\prime}]\in o_{\bm{x}}^{\mathbb{Q}}
in the count
\langle d^{2}[\bm{x}],\hbar^{-\chi}[\bm{x}^{\prime}]\rangle
each such height
2
curve
([S_{1},\pi_{1}],u_{1}),([S_{2},\pi_{2}],u_{2})
contributes

(2.90)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{\bm{\mu}(\bm{x}^{\prime\prime})}}\cdot\frac{1}{N_{\bm{\mu}(\bm{x}^{\prime})}}\cdot(\partial_{u_{2}}\circ\partial_{u_{1}}),\text{ if }\chi_{1},\chi_{2}<0,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{\bm{\mu}(\bm{x}^{\prime})}}\cdot(\partial_{u_{2}}\circ\partial_{u_{1}}),\text{ otherwise.}

The Hausdorff quotient
\overline{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})^{\vee}_{H}
can be given a consistent choice of orientation on the smooth part and regarded as an oriented weighted graph satisfying the Kirchhoff junction rule at each interior vertex, where the interior vertices correspond to the branching locus and the exterior vertices correspond to points on the boundary
\partial\overline{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})^{\vee}_{H}
.
Each exterior vertex then induces a map
o_{\bm{x}}^{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow o_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{Q}}[(n-2)\chi)]
applying (
2.81
) and using the trivialization
\overline{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})^{\vee}_{H}
induced by the outward pointing vector. Then the signed count of such maps is equal to
0
.

We then claim that for any pair
([S_{1},\pi_{1}],u_{1})
and
([S_{2},\pi_{2}],u_{2})
representing a point of
\partial\overline{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})^{\vee}_{H}
the composition map on orientation lines

(2.91)

\partial_{u_{2}}\circ\partial_{u_{1}}\colon o_{\bm{x}}^{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow o_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{Q}}

coincides with the one induced by the outward pointing vector at this pair to
\overline{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})^{\vee}_{H}
. More explicitly one has to compare two isomorphisms

(2.92)

o_{\bm{x}^{\prime\prime}}[(2-n)\chi]\cong\big|\overline{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})\big|\otimes|\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}u|\otimes o_{\bm{x}},

(2.93)

o_{\bm{x}^{\prime\prime}}\bigl[(2-n)\chi\bigr]\cong|\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}u_{2}|\otimes o_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}\bigl[(2-n)\chi_{1}\bigr]\cong|\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}u_{2}|\otimes|\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}u_{1}|\otimes o_{\bm{x}},

where
((S,\pi),u)
is some curve near the boundary. The claim then follows since
\partial_{s}u_{2}
is an outward pointing vector.

Therefore the sum of contributions (
2.90
) corresponding to a given component of
\overline{\mathscr{M}}^{\chi}(\bm{x};\bm{x}^{\prime})^{\vee}_{H}
equals
0
, providing that
d^{2}|_{o_{\bm{x}}}=0
. We leave it to the reader to verify that
d^{2}|_{o_{\bm{x}}[(n-2)j]}=0
for any
j>0
.
∎

We denote the homology of
(SC^{*}_{\kappa,\text{psym}}(\hat{M}),d)
by
SH^{*}_{\kappa,\text{psym}}(M)
or by
SH^{*}_{\kappa,\text{psym}}(\hat{M})
.
We also write
SC^{*}_{\kappa,\text{psym}}(\hat{M};\bm{H},J_{t})
to indicate the specific choice of
\bm{H}=(H^{\text{tot}}_{1,1},\ldots,H^{\text{tot}}_{\kappa,1})
as in
1
-
3
and
J_{t}
as in Section
2.3
.

2.12.
Linear Hamiltonians.

In this section we give an alternative definition of partially symmetrized version of Heegaard Floer symplectic cohomology via collections of linear Hamiltonians. We show that the two definitions coincide. The advantage of the linear Hamiltonian perspective is that the invariance under choices of Floer data is evident.

Since we assume everywhere in this section that we are working in the partially symmetrized context the corresponding subscript would be sometimes dropped from the notation.

Definition 2.42
.

We say that a collection
\bm{H}=\{H_{j}^{m}\}
of Hamiltonian functions
H_{j}^{m}\penalty\ \colon\penalty\ \mathbb{R}/m\mathbb{Z}\penalty\ \times\penalty\ \hat{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for
j\in\mathbb{Z},1\leq j\leq\lfloor\frac{\kappa}{m}\rfloor
, is
linear of slope
b
if each of these functions is equal to
b\cdot r
on the portion
[b,\infty)\times\partial M
of the symplectization end
[1,\infty)\times\partial M
.

Claim 2.43
.

For almost any
b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
all tuples of orbits in
\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}(\bm{H})
for a tuple
\bm{H}
of linear Hamiltonians of slope
b
are contained in
M
.

Proof.

It follows by considering
b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
which are not equal to periods of any of the Reeb orbits in
\partial M
.
∎

Claim 2.44
.

For a generic collection of linear Hamiltonians
\bm{H}=\{H_{j}^{m}\}
, all time-
m
orbits of each
H_{j}^{m}
are non-degenerate. Moreover, it can be assured that these orbits are disjoint.

Proof.

See
[
Abo15
, Lemma 1.2.13]
.
∎

Combining the two claims above, we conclude that for a generic tuple
\bm{H}
properties
2
and
3
are satisfied.

For a collection of linear Hamiltonians one may also define a chain complex

(SC_{\kappa,\text{psym}}^{*}(M;\bm{H},J_{t}),d)

by choosing a consistent Floer data on
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
adapted to
\bm{H}
. The proofs of the regularity and compactness of the associated moduli spaces are similar to those of Theorems
2.33
and
2.38
and are less technical.

We define a preorder on collections of linear Hamiltonians as follows:

Definition 2.45
.

Given tuples
\bm{H}^{+}
and
\bm{H}^{-}
of linear Hamiltonians of slopes
b^{+}
and
b^{-}
, respectively, we say that

(2.94)

\bm{H}^{+}\preceq\bm{H}^{-}

if
b^{+}\leq b^{-}
.

Given two collections
\bm{H}^{+}\preceq\bm{H}^{-}
we construct the continuation map

(2.95)

\mathfrak{c}\colon SC_{\kappa,\text{psym}}^{*}(M;\bm{H}^{+},K^{+},J^{+})\rightarrow SC_{\kappa,\text{psym}}^{*}(M;\bm{H}^{-},K^{-},J^{-})

where
(K^{+},J^{+})
is some consistent Floer data compatible with
\bm{H}^{+}
and
(K^{-},J^{-})
is compatible with
\bm{H}^{-}
.

First of all, we set
H_{j}^{m}(s,t)
to satisfy

(2.96)

\displaystyle H_{j}^{m}(s,t)=b_{s}r\text{, on the symplectization end for }r\geq b^{-},

(2.97)

\displaystyle H_{j}^{m}(s,t)=(H_{j}^{m})^{+}\text{ for }s\gg 0\text{ and }H_{j}^{m}(s,t)=(H_{j}^{m})^{-}\text{ for }s\ll 0,

where
b_{s}
is a value interpolating between
b^{+}
and
b^{-}
and can be regarded as a smooth function
\mathbb{R}\rightarrow[b^{+},b^{-}]
.
Similarly, we set
J_{t}^{s}
to be
s
-dependent almost complex structure on
\hat{M}
coinciding with
J_{t}^{\pm}
near
\pm\infty
.

These choices essentially fix Floer data on
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
for
\chi=0
. Notice that this data is no longer
\mathbb{R}
-invariant, hence such data does not induce a choice of data on
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
.

Definition 2.46
.

We say that a choice of Floer data
(\tilde{K},\tilde{J})
for all
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
is a
continuation Floer data
for the pair
(\bm{H}^{+},J^{+})
and
(\bm{H}^{-},J^{-})
if it satisfies:

(1)

for
\chi=0
it coincides with Floer data induced by
H^{\text{tot}}_{m,j}(s,\cdot)
,
J_{t}^{s}
as above;

(2)

H_{(S,\pi)}(z)=b_{s}r
on the symplectization end, where
z\in S
and
\pi_{\mathbb{R}}(z)=s
for any
(S,\pi)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
;

(3)

\tilde{K}_{(S,\pi)}(z)=K_{(S,\pi)}^{+}(z)
for
z\in S
such that
\pi_{\mathbb{R}}(z)\geq 1
, similarly
\tilde{K}_{(S,\pi)}(z)=K_{(S,\pi}^{-}(z)
for
z\in S
such that
\pi_{\mathbb{R}}(z)\leq-1
, for any
(S,\pi)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
;

(4)

\tilde{J}_{(S,\pi)}(z)=J^{+}(z)
for
z\in S
such that
\pi_{\mathbb{R}}(z)\geq 1
; similarly
\tilde{J}_{(S,\pi)}(z)=J_{(S,\pi)}^{-}(z)
for
z\in S
such that
\pi_{\mathbb{R}}(z)\leq-1
, for any
(S,\pi)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
.

We notice that since
\mathcal{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
is a quotient of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
the wnb groupoid
\mathscr{H}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
may be adapted to introduce a branched structure
\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
associated with
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
.

Given tuples of orbits
\bm{x}^{\pm}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}(\bm{H}^{\pm})
, and a choice of continuation Floer data
(\tilde{K},\tilde{J})
we define a
continuation moduli space

\mathscr{K}^{\chi}(\tilde{K};\tilde{J};\bm{x}^{+};\bm{x}^{-})
to be the groupoid with elements of

\Big(\mathscr{K}^{\chi}(\tilde{K};\tilde{J};\bm{x}^{+};\bm{x}^{-})\Big)_{0}
consisting of pairs:

(2.98)

\Big((S,\pi,\bm{p}^{\pm},\bm{a}^{\pm},B)\in\left(\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}\right)_{0},\,u=(\pi,v)\colon S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times\hat{M}\Big)

such that

(2.99)

\begin{cases}(dv-\tilde{Y})^{0,1}=0\text{ with respect to }\tilde{J}_{(S,\pi)};\\
\lim_{s\rightarrow+\infty}v\circ\epsilon_{i}^{+}(s,\cdot)=x_{i}^{+}(\cdot);\\
\lim_{s\rightarrow-\infty}v\circ\epsilon_{i}^{-}(s,\cdot)=x_{i}^{-}(\cdot).\end{cases}

The morphisms
\left(\mathscr{K}^{\chi}(\bm{x}^{+};\bm{x}^{-};\tilde{K};\tilde{J})\right)_{1}
and the weight function are induced from
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
; see the discussion in Section
2.9
.

Proposition 2.47
.

For a generic choice of continuation Floer data
(\tilde{K},\tilde{J})
the moduli spaces
\mathscr{K}^{\chi}(\tilde{K};\tilde{J};\bm{x}^{+};\bm{x}^{-})
are wnb groupoids of dimension

(2.100)

(n-2)\chi+|\bm{x}^{-}|-|\bm{x}^{+}|.

Proof.

We refer the reader to the proof of Theorem
2.33
for details.
∎

We now define the continuation map
\mathfrak{c}
by counting curves in the moduli space above, i.e., given
\bm{x}^{+}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}(\bm{H}^{+})
we set

(2.101)

\mathfrak{c}([\bm{x}^{+}])=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{x}^{-}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{psym}}(\bm{H}^{-}),\,u\in\mathscr{K}^{\chi}(\tilde{K};\tilde{J};\bm{x}^{-};\bm{x}^{+})_{H}\\
(n-2)\chi+|\bm{x}^{-}|-|\bm{x}^{+}|=0,\,\chi<0\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{u}([\bm{x}^{+}])}{N_{\bm{\mu}(\bm{x}^{-})}},

where
[\bm{x}^{+}]\in o_{\bm{x}^{+}}^{\mathbb{Q}}
is some generator and
\mathfrak{c}_{u}
is the morphism on orientation lines associated with
u
, as in (
2.82
):

(2.102)

\mathfrak{c}_{u}\colon o_{\bm{x}^{+}}\rightarrow o_{\bm{x}^{-}}[(2-n)\chi].

We point out that one again should pass to Kuranishi replacements for
n\geq 3
whenever necessary as in Section
2.9
.

Lemma 2.48
.

The map
\mathfrak{c}
is a chain map.

Proof.

We claim that
\mathscr{K}^{\chi}(\tilde{K};\tilde{J};\bm{x}^{-};\bm{x}^{+})
with
(n-2)\chi+|\bm{x}^{-}|-|\bm{x}^{+}|=1
admits a compactification (in the sense of (
2.86
)) with boundary
\partial|\mathscr{K}^{\chi}(\bm{x}^{+};\bm{x}^{-};\tilde{K};\tilde{J})|_{H}
covered by images of

(2.103)

\bigsqcup_{\chi_{1},\chi_{2};\,\bm{x}^{-}_{0}\in SC^{*}_{\kappa}(M,\bm{H}^{-})}\mathscr{K}^{\chi_{1}}(\tilde{K};\tilde{J};\bm{x}^{+};\,\bm{x}^{-}_{0})\times\mathscr{M}^{\chi_{2}}(K^{-};J^{-};\bm{x}^{-}_{0};\bm{x}^{-}),

where

\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}=\chi,

(n-2)\chi_{1}+|\bm{x}^{-}_{0}|-|\bm{x}^{+}|=(n-2)\chi_{2}+|\bm{x}^{-}|-|\bm{x}^{-}_{0}|-1=0;

and

(2.104)

\bigsqcup_{\chi_{1},\chi_{2};\,\bm{x}^{+}_{0}\in SC^{*}_{\kappa}(M,\bm{H}^{+})}\mathscr{M}^{\chi_{1}}(K^{+};J^{+};\bm{x}^{+};\bm{x}^{+}_{0})\times\mathscr{K}^{\chi_{2}}(\tilde{K};\tilde{J};\bm{x}^{+}_{0};\bm{x}^{-}),

where

\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}=\chi,

(n-2)\chi_{1}+|\bm{x}^{+}_{0}|-|\bm{x}^{+}|-1=(n-2)\chi_{2}+|\bm{x}^{-}|-|\bm{x}^{+}_{0}|=0.

The proof is similar to that of Theorem
2.38
but easier since for linear Hamiltonians all orbits are contained in the compact region. Each such building has the associated weight
N_{\bm{\mu}(\bm{x}^{0})}
. We leave it to the reader to verify that compositions of morphisms on orientation lines associated with (
2.103
) and (
2.104
) for given
o_{\bm{x}^{+}}^{\mathbb{Q}}
and
o_{\bm{x}^{-}}^{\mathbb{Q}}[(2-n)\chi]
differ by
(-1)
(also compare with the proof of Theorem
2.41
).

Taking weights into account we conclude that

(2.105)

\mathfrak{c}\circ d-d\circ\mathfrak{c}=0.

∎

Lemma 2.49
.

The continuation map
\mathfrak{c}
is independent of the choice of continuation Floer data
(\tilde{K},\tilde{J})
.

Proof.

We omit the proof here and refer the reader to
[
Abo15
, Lemma 1.6.13]
for the heuristic of the argument that can be adapted in the context of Hurwitz spaces.
∎

Lemma 2.50
.

Given three tuples of Hamiltonians satisfying
\bm{H}^{+}\preceq\bm{H}^{\text{✩}}\preceq\bm{H}^{-}
, there is a commutative diagram
SC_{\kappa}^{*}(\hat{M};\bm{H}^{+})
SC_{\kappa}^{*}(\hat{M};\bm{H}^{\text{✩}})
SC_{\kappa}^{*}(\hat{M};\bm{H}^{-})
\mathfrak{c}_{+\rightarrow\text{✩}}
\mathfrak{c}_{+\rightarrow-}
\mathfrak{c}_{\text{✩}\rightarrow-}

Proof.

Let
(\tilde{K}^{+},\tilde{J}^{+})
and
(\tilde{K}^{-},\tilde{J}^{-})
be Floer data defining continuation maps
\mathfrak{c}_{+\rightarrow\text{✩}}
and
\mathfrak{c}_{{}^{\prime}\rightarrow-}
respectively.
We pick the following collection of Floer data
(\tilde{K}^{R},\tilde{J}^{R})
for
R>0
on all
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
, satisfying that for any
(S,\pi)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}
:

(1)

\tilde{K}^{R}_{(S,\pi)}(z)=\tilde{K}^{+}_{(S,\pi^{-R})}(z)
for
z\in S
such that
\pi_{\mathbb{R}}(z)\geq 0
where
\pi^{-R}=\pi+(-R,0)
; similarly
\tilde{K}^{R}_{(S,\pi)}(z)=\tilde{K}^{-}_{(S,\pi^{R})}(z)
for
z\in S
such that
\pi_{\mathbb{R}}(z)\leq 0
, where
\pi^{R}=\pi+(R,0)
;

(2)

\tilde{J}^{R}_{(S,\pi)}(z)=\tilde{J}^{+}_{(S,\pi^{-R})}(z)
for
z\in S
such that
\pi_{\mathbb{R}}(z)\geq 0
; similarly
\tilde{J}^{R}_{(S,\pi)}(z)=\tilde{J}^{-}_{(S,\pi^{R})}(z)
for
z\in S
such that
\pi_{\mathbb{R}}(z)\leq 0
.

We notice that for
z
satisfying
-R\leq\pi_{\mathbb{R}}(z)\leq R
the Floer data as above coincides with some
(K^{\text{✩}},J^{\text{✩}})
defining differential on
SC_{\kappa}^{*}(\hat{M},\bm{H}^{\text{✩}})
.

The data
(\tilde{K}^{R},\tilde{J}^{R})
also defines the continuation map
\mathfrak{c}_{+\rightarrow-}
on cohomology. Now we fix
\bm{x}^{\pm}\in SC_{\kappa}^{*}(\hat{M},\bm{H}^{\pm})
and consider a pair
(u^{+},u^{-})
with

u^{+}\in\mathscr{K}^{\chi_{1}}(\tilde{K}^{+};\tilde{J}^{+};\bm{x}^{+};\,\bm{x}^{\text{✩}}_{0})\text{ and }u^{-}\in\mathscr{K}^{\chi_{2}}(\tilde{K}^{-};\tilde{J}^{-};\bm{x}^{\text{✩}}_{0};\,\bm{x}^{-}),\text{ where}

(n-2)\chi_{1}+|\bm{x}^{\prime}_{0}|-|\bm{x}^{+}|=(n-2)\chi_{2}+|\bm{x}^{-}|-|\bm{x}^{\text{✩}}_{0}|=0.

We claim then that for
R\gg 0
there is a glued curve

u^{+}\#_{R}u^{-}\in\mathscr{K}^{\chi}(\tilde{K}^{R};\tilde{J}^{R};\bm{x}^{+};\,\bm{x}^{-}),

where
\chi=\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}
. Moreover, we claim that for
R\gg 0
all elements of
|\mathscr{K}^{\chi}(\tilde{K}^{R};\tilde{J}^{R};\bm{x}^{+};\,\bm{x}^{-})|
are obtained in this fashion. The only nuance is that this map is not injective and the curve
u^{+}\#_{R}u^{-}
as above is obtained in
N_{\bm{\mu}(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{0})}
many ways in the same way as in the description of boundary degenerations in Theorem
2.38
. Therefore, there is a bijection between elements of the finite set
|(\tilde{K}^{R};\tilde{J}^{R};\bm{x}^{+};\,\bm{x}^{-})|
taken with weights as above and

\bigsqcup_{\chi_{1},\chi_{2};\,\bm{x}^{\prime}_{0}\in SC^{*}_{\kappa}(M,\bm{H}^{\prime})}|\mathscr{K}^{\chi_{1}}(\tilde{K}^{+};\tilde{J}^{+};\bm{x}^{+};\,\bm{x}^{\text{✩}}_{0})|\times|\mathscr{K}^{\chi_{2}}(\tilde{K}^{-};\tilde{J}^{-};\bm{x}^{\text{✩}}_{0};\bm{x}^{-})|.

This implies

(2.106)

\mathfrak{c}_{+\rightarrow-}=\mathfrak{c}_{\text{✩}\rightarrow-}\circ\mathfrak{c}_{+\rightarrow\text{✩}}

∎

Corollary 2.51
.

For
\bm{H}
and
\bm{H}^{\text{✩}}
of the same slope, their symplectic cohomologies are isomorphic via the continuation map:

\mathfrak{c}\colon SH_{\kappa}^{*}(\hat{M},\bm{H})\xrightarrow{\cong}SH_{\kappa}^{*}(\hat{M},\bm{H}^{\text{✩}}).

Lemmas
2.49
and
2.50
allow one to define Heegaard Floer symplectic homology as a direct limit:

(2.107)

SH_{\kappa,\text{psym}}^{*}(\hat{M})=\lim_{\mathfrak{c}}SH_{\kappa}^{*}(\hat{M},\bm{H})

At last, one may restrict to a sequence
\{\bm{H}^{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
with unbounded slopes, i.e.,

Lemma 2.52
.

For any sequence of tuples of linear Hamiltonians
\bm{H}^{m}
with slopes
b_{k}
satisfying
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}b_{k}=+\infty
, there is an isomorphism

(2.108)

\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}SH_{\kappa}^{*}(\hat{M},\bm{H}^{i})=SH_{\kappa}^{*}(\hat{M})

Proof.

We omit the proof which follows the lines of
[
Abo15
, Lemma 1.6.17]
.
∎

Theorem 2.53
.

Given a tuple
\bm{H}^{\text{tot}}\in{\mathcal{\bm{H}}}_{S^{1}}^{\kappa}(\hat{M})
and
J_{t}\in\mathcal{J}(\hat{M})
, there is an isomorphism

(2.109)

SH_{\kappa,\text{psym}}^{*}(\hat{M};H^{\text{tot}},J)\cong SH_{\kappa,\text{psym}}^{*}(\hat{M})

Sketch of the proof.

Here, we only sketch the proof and refer the reader for a more detailed treatment in case of
\kappa=1
to
[
Rit13
, Appendix 3]
.
Set
\bm{H}^{k}
equal to the tuple of linear Hamiltonians of slope
k
and equal to
\bm{H}^{\text{tot}}
on the complement of
(k,+\infty)\times\partial M\subset\hat{M}
. Let us denote by
SC_{\kappa}^{*}(\bm{H}^{\text{tot}};\mathcal{A}>c)
the subcomplex of
SC_{\kappa}^{*}(\hat{M};\bm{H}^{\text{tot}})
generated by tuples of orbits
\bm{x}
with action
\mathcal{A}(\bm{x})>c
. Note that for
c\ll-k
we may naturally define a continuation map by interpolating between
\bm{H}^{\text{tot}}
and
\bm{H}^{k}
which on the level of cohomology takes the form

SH_{\kappa}^{*}(\bm{H}^{k})\rightarrow SH_{\kappa}^{*}(\bm{H}^{\text{tot}};\mathcal{A}>c).

Additionally, for
k^{\prime}\gg-c
there is natural cochain map induced by inclusion at the level of tuples of orbits

SC_{\kappa}^{*}(\bm{H}^{\text{tot}};\mathcal{A}>c)\rightarrow SC_{\kappa}^{*}(\bm{H}^{k^{\prime}}).

Composing the above for
k^{\prime}\gg-c\gg k
we get

(2.110)

SH_{\kappa}^{*}(\bm{H}^{k})\rightarrow SH_{\kappa}^{*}(\bm{H}^{\text{tot}};\mathcal{A}>c)\rightarrow SH_{\kappa}^{*}(\bm{H}^{k^{\prime}})

and clearly, this composition is the continuation map.

On the other hand, for
-c^{\prime}\gg k^{\prime}\gg-c
the composition of maps

(2.111)

SC_{\kappa}^{*}(\bm{H}^{\text{tot}};\mathcal{A}>c)\rightarrow SC_{\kappa}^{*}(H^{k^{\prime}})\rightarrow SC_{\kappa}^{*}(\bm{H}^{\text{tot}};\mathcal{A}>c^{\prime})

is the natural inclusion. The claim then follows by Lemma
2.52
and since

SH_{\kappa}^{*}(\bm{H}^{\text{tot}})=\lim_{c\rightarrow-\infty}SH_{\kappa}^{*}(\bm{H}^{\text{tot}};\mathcal{A}>c).

∎

As a corollary, we get that
SH^{*}_{\kappa,\text{psym}}(\hat{M};\bm{H},J_{t};(H,F,J)
is independent of the choice of
\bm{H}^{\text{tot}}\in{\mathcal{\bm{H}}}_{S^{1}}^{\kappa}(\hat{M})
and
J_{t}\in\mathcal{J}(\hat{M})
and the choice of Floer data
(H,F,J)
that we made in our construction. It is also well known that the standard symplectic cohomology
SH^{*}_{\kappa=1}(\hat{M})
defined as a direct limit is an invariant under
Liouville isomorphisms
; see
[
Sei08
, Section 3]
,
[
Rit10
, Theorem 8]
. The same argument works for any
\kappa
and is similar to the one we provide in the proof of Theorem
2.53
. Hence we conclude that Theorem
1.1
holds true for
SH^{*}_{\kappa,\text{psym}}(M)
.

2.13.
Heegaard Floer symplectic cohomology with unsymmetrized orbit tuples

In this section, we give another version of Heegaard Floer symplectic cohomology
SC_{\kappa,\text{unsym}}(\hat{M})
which we expect to produce closely related chain complex to
SC_{\kappa,\text{psym}}(\hat{M})
even at the level of cohomology. On the chain level
SC_{\kappa,\text{unsym}}(\hat{M})
will have more generators in every degree than
SC_{\kappa,\text{psym}}(\hat{M})
.

First, we fix some notation. For a given permutation
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa}
we assign its cycle decomposition
\sigma=c_{1}(\sigma)\circ\ldots\circ c_{\ell(\sigma)}(\sigma)
, with
\bm{\mu}(\sigma)=(\mu_{1}(\sigma),\ldots,\mu_{\ell(\sigma)}(\sigma)
being a cycle type (i.e.,
\mu_{i}(\sigma)
is the length of cycle
c_{i}(\sigma)
) of
\sigma
and
\ell(\sigma)
denotes the length of the partition
\bm{\mu}(\sigma)
. We apply here the following order on cycles: we say that the cycle
c>c^{\prime}
for two cycles
c,c^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa}
if
c
is longer than
c^{\prime}
or if the least element permuted by
c
is smaller than the least element permuted by
c^{\prime}
. Note that for a given permutation
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa}
the above relation provides a total order on the elements of the cyclic decomposition of
\sigma
and
c_{i}(\sigma)
in the above is assumed to be the
i
-the largest cycle in the decomposition of
\sigma
under this relation.

Let
H_{0}\in\mathcal{H}(\hat{M})
and
F_{0}\in C^{\infty}(S^{1}\times\hat{M})
satisfy
1
and
2
.

We then choose
\kappa
different functions
F_{i}\colon S^{1}\times\hat{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for
i=1,\ldots,\kappa
satisfying

(1)

each
F_{i}\colon\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\times\hat{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a non-negative function such that
|F_{i}-F_{0}|
is absolutely bounded by a small constant and vanishes away from the locus of Hamiltonian orbits of
H_{0}^{\text{tot}}=H_{0}+F_{0}
of all integer periods, and for
t\in[0,\delta]\cup[1-\delta,1]\subset S^{1}
it holds that
F_{0}(t)=F_{i}(t)
;

(2)

for any cyclic permutation
c=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{k})\in\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa}
we define
F_{c}
to be the function
F_{c}\colon\mathbb{R}/k\mathbb{Z}\times\hat{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
F_{c}(i-1+t)=F_{j_{i}}(t)
for
t\in[0,1)
and
i\in Z
such that
1\leq i\leq k
(note that
F_{c}
is smooth by
1
); moreover, it is required that any time-
k
orbit of
H_{c}^{\text{tot}}=H_{0}+F_{c}
is non-degenerate and lies in a small neighborhood of the corresponding time-
k
orbit of
H_{0}^{\text{tot}}
;

(3)

Hamiltonian time-
k
orbits for different
H^{\text{tot}}_{c}
’s are disjoint and embedded.

We leave it to the reader to verify that such collections
\{F_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\kappa}
exist and are sufficiently generic (see also Section
2.2
).

Consider the set
\mathcal{P}_{\text{unsym}}
of ordered
\kappa
-tuples of Hamiltonian chords defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\text{unsym}}=\left\{\bm{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\kappa})\left|\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{$x_{i}\colon[0,1]\rightarrow\hat{M}$ is the integral flow of $\phi^{t}_{H^{\text{tot}}_{i}}$,}\\
\text{$x_{i}(1)=x_{\sigma(i)}(0)$ for some permutation $\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa}$.}\end{array}\right.\right\}

For each
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{unsym}}
we would denote the associated permutation by
\sigma(\bm{x})\in\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa}
. For a given
\bm{x}
with
\sigma=\sigma(\bm{x})
to each cycle
c_{i}(\sigma)
with
i=1,\ldots,\ell(\sigma)
we associate a loop
x^{c_{i}(\sigma)}
in
\hat{M}
formed of chords
x_{j}
with
j
such that
c_{i}(\sigma)(j)\neq j
. Clearly,
x^{c_{i}(\sigma)}
is a Hamiltonian orbit with respect to
H^{\text{tot}}_{c_{i}(\sigma)}
. See Figure
2.13
for an example.
We would also use the notation
\ell(\bm{x})
for
\ell(\sigma)
.

Figure 6
.
A
\kappa
-tuple (
\kappa=5
) of Hamiltonian chords
\bm{$}asanelementof

_
unsym
,where

x_(1), x_(2), x_(345)
.

_

.\begin{definition}The \emph{Hurwitz space} $\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}_{\kappa,\chi}$ is the space of ismorphism classes \emph{branched covers}, where a branched cover
\begin{equation*}(S,\pi\colon S\penalty\ \mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\penalty\ \mathbb{R}\penalty\ \times\penalty\ S^{1},\bm{p}^{+},\bm{a}^{+},\bm{p}^{-},\bm{a}^{-},B)\end{equation*}is the following collection of data:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@A Riemann surface $S$ without boundary of Euler characteristic $\chi$ punctured at positive punctures $\bm{p}^{+}=\{p^{+}_{1},\ldots,p^{+}_{l(\bm{\mu})}\}$ and negative punctures $\bm{p}^{-}=\{p^{-}_{1},\ldots,p^{-}_{l(\bm{\mu}^{\prime})}\}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@For each positive puncture $p_{i}^{+}$ we pick $\mu_{i}(\sigma)$ asymptotic markers $a_{i1}^{+},\ldots a_{i\mu_{i}(\sigma)}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}_{p_{i}^{+}}S$. We assume the total order on the positive asymptotic markers $a_{1}^{+},\ldots,a_{\kappa}^{+}$ such that the asymptotic markers belonging to $\mathcal{S}^{1}_{p_{i}^{+}}S$ appear on this circle in the order prescribed by the cycle $c_{i}(\sigma)$ (note that $\mathcal{S}^{1}_{p_{i}^{+}}S$ posses the induced orientation from $S)$. There is an analogous choice of negative asymptotic markers $a_{1}^{-},\ldots,a_{\kappa}^{-}$ satisfying similar conditions with respect to $\sigma^{\prime}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@A holomorphic branched cover $\pi\colon S\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}$ with simple branched points away from $\pm\infty$. At $+\infty$ (resp. at $-\infty$) the branching partial is given by $\bm{\mu}(\sigma)$ (resp. by $\bm{\mu}(\sigma^{\prime})$), i.e., a point $p_{i}^{+}$ (resp. a point $p_{i}^{-}$) is a ramification point of multiplicity $\mu_{i}(\sigma)$ (resp. multiplicity $\mu_{i}(\sigma^{\prime})$) of an extended map $\overline{\pi}\colon\overline{S}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow S^{2}$. We additionally require that the map induced by $\pi$ from $\mathcal{S}^{1}_{p_{i}^{+}}\overline{S}$ to $\mathcal{S}^{1}_{+\infty}S^{2}$ sends all associated with $p_{i}^{+}$ asymptotic markers to the marker $1\in\mathcal{S}^{1}_{+\infty}S^{2}$, together with similar requirements for negative punctures of $S$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@An ordered tuple $B$ of points $q_{1},\ldots q_{b}$ in $\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}$ of simple branching, where $b=-\chi$.
\end{enumerate}\par Two branched covers $(S,\pi,\bm{p}^{\pm},\bm{a}^{\pm},B)$ and $(S^{\prime},\pi^{\prime},\bm{p}^{\prime\pm},\bm{a}^{\prime\pm},B^{\prime})$ are said to be \emph{isomorphic} if there exists a biholomorphism $\alpha\colon\overline{S}\rightarrow\overline{S^{\prime}}$ such that $\alpha(p^{\pm}_{i})=p_{i}^{\prime\pm}$, $\alpha(a^{\pm}_{i})=a_{i}^{\prime\pm}$ and $\pi^{\prime}\circ\alpha=\pi$.
\end{definition}\par Withoutgoingintomuchdetail,weclaimthatthereisacompactification
