2.14.
Orientation lines conventions

Here we introduce some basics on orientation lines that we use to have all chain complexes and chain maps appearing in this text well-defined over
\mathbb{Q}
. We use the conventions from
[
Abo15
]
with slight adjustements.

Given a finite dimensional real vector space
V
, its determinant line is the
1
-dimensional
\mathbb{Z}
-graded real vector space
\operatorname{det}V=\Lambda^{\operatorname{dim}(V)}V
supported in degree
\operatorname{dim}(V)
.

To any
1
-dimensional graded real vector space
\delta
we associate an orientation line
|\delta|
, which is the rank
1
graded free abelian group generated by the two possible orientations of
\delta
, modulo the relation that their sum vanishes. The orientation line
|\delta|
is supported in the same degree as
\delta
. When
\delta=\operatorname{det}V
we denote its orientation line by
|V|
.
Given an orientable manifold
M
we will denote by
|M|
the orientation line generated by the orientations of
M
and supported in degree
\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{R}}M
.

Given two vector spaces
U
and
V
, let
W=U\oplus V
. There is then an isomorphism
—U— ⊗—V— ≅—W—
given by first choosing a basis corresponding to a given orientation of
U
and then extending it to the basis of
W
by adding (on the right) a basis of
V
corresponding to a given orientation of
V
. Notice that this procedure gives rise to the
Koszul sign
:

(2.130)

|W|\cong|U|\otimes|V|\cong(-1)^{\operatorname{dim}(U)\cdot\operatorname{dim}(V)}|V|\otimes|U|\cong(-1)^{\operatorname{dim}(U)\cdot\operatorname{dim}(V)}|W|.

More generally, for an exact sequence of vector spaces
0 →V →W →U →0
which splits, we assign an isomorphism

(2.131)

|U|\otimes|V|\cong|W|.

We note that this convention is different from the one used in
[
Abo15
]
. We are motivated by the following example. Let
N
be an orientable submanifold of an orientable manifold
M
. Let us denote by
\eta
the normal bundle
TM/TN
over
N
. There is then a short exact sequence
0 →TN →TM—_N →
η
→0.
The standard convention for inducing an orientation on
N
from an orientation on
M
is to first set a positive orientation on
\eta
(which in some cases is straightforward as
\eta
could be a trivial bundle) and then declare an orientation on
N
to be positive if a basis with such orientation extends a basis with positive orientation on
\eta
to a basis with positive orientation on
M
. In other words,
—
η
— ⊗—N— ≅—M—.
Given a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded line
\ell
(rank
1
free abelian group), its dual line
\ell^{-1}=

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\ell,\mathbb{Z})
is, by definition, supported in the opposite degree as
\ell
. Here, we use the convention that
\mathbb{Z}
is supported in degree
0
. There is a canonical isomorphism
\ell^{-1}\otimes\ell\cong\mathbb{Z}
induced by evaluation. According to (
2.130
) there is an isomorphism
(-1)^{\operatorname{deg}(\ell)}\ell\otimes\ell^{-1}\cong\mathbb{Z}
.

We will denote by
\zeta
the trivial rank
1
free abelian group (i.e., with prescribed isomorphism with
\mathbb{Z}
) supported in degree
1
.

Given a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded rank
1
free abelian group
\ell
, we denote by
\ell[k]
the rank
1
group obtained by shifting the degree down by
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, i.e.,
\ell[k]
is supported in degree
\operatorname{deg}(\ell)-k
. We will use the agreement

(2.132)

\displaystyle\ell[-k]\cong\ell\otimes\zeta^{k},\text{ for }k>0,

\displaystyle\ell[-k]\cong\zeta^{-k}\otimes\ell,\text{ for }k<0.

For any such rank
1
free abelian group
\ell
we associate

(2.133)

\ell^{\mathbb{Q}}=\ell\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q},

which is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space supported in
\operatorname{deg}(\ell)
.

3.
The free multiloop complex

In this section, we introduce a certain Morse chain complex which is intended to be a counterpart of the unsymmetrized version of HFSH. We note that this complex is the closed string analogue of the Morse complex introduced in
[
HKTY
]
.
Throughout this section,
Q
is a closed orientable
n
-manifold.

3.1.
Free multiloops

The analogue of
SH_{\kappa}(T^{*}Q)
on the Morse side should on the chain level be based on time-
1
free loops in the configuration space
\operatorname{UConf}_{\kappa}(Q)
.
To model this, we consider
\kappa
-strand multiloops on
Q
. We start by recalling the setup of
[
AS10
]
for the Morse complex of paths alone.

Consider the space of continuous time-1 paths on
Q
with free endpoints:

\Omega(Q)=\{\gamma\in C^{0}([0,1],Q)\}.

We denote the subset of
\Omega(Q)
in the class
W^{1,2}
by
\Omega^{1}(Q)
.

Fix a generic Riemannian metric
g
on
Q
.
Let L=L(t,q,v):[0,1]×TQ→
ℝ
be a
Lagrangian function
which is the
Fenchel dual
of a Hamiltonian
H
that we introduced in Section
2.13
.

We then define the Lagrangian action functional
\mathcal{A}_{L_{i}}\colon\Omega^{1}(Q)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

(3.1)

\mathcal{A}_{L}(\gamma)=\int_{0}^{1}L(t,\gamma(t),\dot{\gamma}(t))\,dt.

Let
\varphi^{t}_{L}
be the integral curve on
Q
satisfying the Euler-Lagrangian equation:

(3.2)

\frac{d}{dt}\partial_{v}L(t,\gamma(t),\dot{\gamma}(t))=\partial_{q}L(t,\gamma(t),\dot{\gamma}(t)).

The critical points of
\mathcal{A}_{L}
are exactly the solutions of (
3.2
).
For
L
as one of the functions above, the Lagrangian action functional
\mathcal{A}_{L}
is not of class
C^{2}
, unless
L
is a polynomial of degree at most two on each fiber of
TQ
, hence in general one may not construct a Morse complex on
\Omega(Q)
by means of the gradient vector field of
\mathcal{A}_{L}
. To overcome this obstacle in
[
AS09
]
the authors introduce the notion of pseudo-gradient vector field
X
for such functions on Hilbert manifolds and prove the existence of pseudo-gradients for Lagrangian action functional.
The following summarizes the key properties of
\mathcal{A}_{L}
and its pseudo-gradient vector fields (see
[
AS09
, Theorem 4.1]
,
[
AS10
, Appendix A.1]
):

Theorem 3.1
.

(1)

For a generic choice of
L
all critical points of
\mathcal{A}_{L}
are non-degenerate.

(2)

The action functional
\mathcal{A}_{L}
is bounded below, and there exists a smooth pseudo-gradient vector field
X
on
\Omega^{1}(Q)
, i.e.,

(a)

X
is forward-complete, i.e., the flow
\Phi(t,x)
is defined for all
t>0
and
x\in\Omega^{1}(Q)
;

(b)

\mathcal{A}_{L}
is a Lyapunov function for
X
;

(c)

X
is a Morse vector field whose singular points (= critical points of
\mathcal{A}_{L}
) have finite Morse index;

(d)

the pair
(X,\mathcal{A}_{L})
satisfies the Palais-Smale condition;

(e)

X satisfies the Morse-Smale condition, i.e., for each pair of critical points
x,y\in\Omega^{1}(Q)
, the unstable manifold of
x
is transverse to the stable manifold of
y
.

(3)

Any sufficiently small perturbation of
X
which coincides with
X
on a neighborhood of the critical points of
\mathcal{A}_{L}
, is still a pseudo-gradient (i.e., satisfies (PG1)-(PG5)).

Let
\mathcal{K}_{L}
be a set of small perturbations of
X
which are pseudo-gradients (one may restrict their attention to those vector fields which have
Whitney distance
from
X
less than
1
; see
[
AS10
,
AM06
]
).

For the generalization of the above to the set of what we call multiloops, choose Lagrangians
L_{i}
to be
Fenchel duals
(see
[
AS10
]
for the precise definition) of
H^{\text{tot}}_{i}
’s that we picked in Section
2.13
for
H_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\lvert p\rvert^{2}
.

By the assumption
1
on the
H_{i}^{\text{tot}}
it follows that for
\delta
such that
0<\delta<1/2
as in
1

(3.3)

L_{i}(t,\cdot,\cdot)=L_{0}(t,\cdot,\cdot)\,\text{ for }\,t\in[0,\delta]\cup[1-\delta,1].

To each permutation
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa}
we associate the set
\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)
of
\kappa
-tuples of multipaths on
Q
which satisfy

(3.4)

\displaystyle\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)=\left\{\bm{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{\kappa})\in\Omega^{1}(Q)^{\kappa}\,|\gamma_{i}(1)=\gamma_{\sigma(i)}(0)\right\}.

We call elements of such a set
free multiloops
since each such multipath consists of
\ell(\sigma)
loops.

We denote by
\bm{L}
the collection
(L_{1},\ldots,L_{\kappa})
and define the Lagrangian action functional on
\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)
by

(3.5)

\mathcal{A}_{\bm{L}}(\bm{\gamma})=\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa}\mathcal{A}_{L_{i}}(\gamma_{i}).

One can also view
\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)
as set of paths
\bm{\gamma}\in\Omega^{1}(Q^{\kappa})
with boundary conditions

(3.6)

(\bm{\gamma}(0),\bm{\gamma}(1))\in R_{\sigma}\subset Q^{\kappa}\times Q^{\kappa},

where
R_{\sigma}=\left\{(\bm{x},\bm{y})\in Q^{\kappa}\times Q^{\kappa}\,|\,x_{\sigma(i)}=y_{i}\,\right\}
.

The critical points of
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{L}}
form a subset
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}^{L}\subset\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)
defined as

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}^{\bm{L}}=\left\{\bm{\gamma}\in\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)\,|\,\text{$\gamma_{i}\colon[0,1]\rightarrow Q$ is the integral flow of $\varphi^{t}_{L_{i}}$}\right\}.

When there is no ambiguity, we also denote
(\bm{\gamma},\sigma)
simply by
\bm{\gamma}
.
We denote the permutation element
\sigma
associated to
\bm{\gamma}
by
\sigma({\bm{\gamma}})
.
If we write
\sigma({\bm{\gamma}})
in its cyclic form, then each cycle corresponds to a loop in
Q
.
We denote the number of cycles associated with
\bm{\gamma}
by
m({\bm{\gamma}})
.
We further require that for all
\bm{\gamma}\in\mathcal{R}_{\kappa}

(1)

\gamma_{i}(t)\neq\gamma_{j}(t)
for all
t
and
i\neq j
;

(2)

each
\gamma_{i}
is a non-degenerate critical point of
\mathcal{A}_{L_{i}}
.

The following lemma follows immediately from Theorem
3.1
.

Lemma 3.2
.

For a generic choice of metric and
\{H_{1}^{\text{tot}},\ldots,H_{\kappa}^{\text{tot}}\}
, the above conditions can be achieved. Moreover,
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{L}}
is a Morse function on
\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)
such that the set of critical points of
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{L}}
is
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}^{\bm{L}}
, all of which are non-degenerate and have finite Morse indices.

Clearly for
\bm{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{\kappa})\in\mathcal{P}^{\bm{L}}_{\sigma}
the Morse index
|\bm{\gamma}|
can be computed as a sum of Morse indices of
\gamma_{i}
:

(3.8)

|\bm{\gamma}|=I_{L_{1}}(\gamma_{1})+\ldots+I_{L_{\kappa}}(\gamma_{\kappa})

We denote the set of all multiloops by

(3.9)

\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)=\bigsqcup_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa}}\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)

and the set of all critical points of
\bm{L}
by

(3.10)

\mathcal{P}^{\bm{L}}_{\kappa}=\bigsqcup_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa}}\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}^{\bm{L}}.

3.2.
The free multiloop complex

We first recall the definition of the Morse chain complex
CM_{*}\left(\Lambda^{1}(Q)\right)
in the standard
\kappa=1
setting following
[
AS06
,
AS10
]
. The chains are given by the graded vector space over
\mathbb{Q}
generated by the critical points of
\mathcal{A}_{L}
. By Theorem
3.1
there exists a pseudo-gradient vector field
X
. The Morse differential is then given by counting index
1

pseudo-gradient trajectories
of
\mathcal{A}_{L}
associated with the flow of
X

(3.11)

\frac{\partial\gamma}{\partial s}=X(\gamma),

where we are parametrizing paths in
\Lambda^{1}(Q)
by the variable
s
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\left(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime};X\right)
be the moduli space of pseudo-gradient trajectories from
\gamma
to
\gamma^{\prime}
with respect to
X
and
\mathcal{M}\left(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime};X\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\left(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime};X\right)/\mathbb{R}
be the quotient by the
\mathbb{R}
-translation. Then we define

∂:C M

*

(

Λ

1

(Q)

)

→CM

*

(

Λ

1

(Q)

)

∂
γ
=∑

—
γ
’—=—
γ
—-1

#
M

(

γ
,
γ
’ ; X

)

⋅
γ
’ .

The homology of this complex is independent of the choice of
X\in\mathcal{K}_{L}
according to
[
AS10
]
.
More generally, given a critical point
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}^{L}
one may define the
stable
and
unstable
manifolds of
\gamma
with respect to
X
:

(3.12)

\displaystyle W^{s}(\gamma;X)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\tilde{\gamma}\in\Lambda^{1}(Q)\mid\varphi_{X}^{t}(\tilde{\gamma})\rightarrow\gamma\text{ for }t\rightarrow+\infty\},

\displaystyle W^{u}(\gamma;X)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\tilde{\gamma}\in\Lambda^{1}(Q)\mid\varphi_{X}^{t}(\tilde{\gamma})\rightarrow\gamma\text{ for }t\rightarrow-\infty\}.

Note that by condition
2c
the unstable manifold is always finite-dimensional.
For the above manifolds, the vector field
X
will be suppressed from the notation below when it is understood or unimportant.

For
\kappa\geq 1
and
\bm{\gamma}\in\Omega_{\sigma}^{1}(Q)
, there is a straightforward definition of a pseudo-gradient trajectory on
\Omega_{\sigma}^{1}(Q)
given by

∂
γ

∂s

=
X
(
γ
)≔∏_i=1^
κ
X_i
(
γ
_i
)
.

The Morse cohomology whose differential is defined by counting pseudo-gradient multi-trajectories between critical points as above gives nothing but
\ell(\sigma)
copies of the Morse homology
HM_{*}\left(\Omega^{1}(Q)\right)
of the loop space of
Q
. In order to mimic higher genus curves appearing in the Floer differential (
2.88
) we need to modify the definition of the Morse differential so that,
given a gradient trajectory
\bm{\gamma}(s)
starting at
\bm{\gamma}
, whenever there exists
s
such that
\bm{\gamma}(s)
crosses the big diagonal of
\text{Sym}^{\kappa}(Q)
, additional trajectories that bifurcate from the original one are created.

Specifically, given two multiloops
\bm{\gamma}\in\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)
and
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}\in\Omega^{1}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(Q)
belonging to
\mathcal{P}_{\kappa}^{\bm{L}}
and a choice of
\ell+1
tuples
\bm{X}^{0},\ldots\bm{X}^{\ell}
each consisting of pseudo-gradient vector fields
X^{j}_{i}\in\mathcal{K}_{L_{i}}
we define
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{X}^{0},\ldots,\bm{X}^{\ell})
as follows:

Definition 3.3
.

A
piecewise pseudo-gradient
trajectory
\bm{\Gamma}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};l)
is a tuple

\bm{\Gamma}=\left((\Gamma_{0},\ldots,\Gamma_{\ell}),\bm{s},\bm{\sigma},\bm{\rho}\right),

where
\bm{s}=(s_{0},\ldots,s_{\ell+1})
with
s_{0}=+\infty
,
s_{\ell+1}=-\infty
and
+\infty>s_{1}>\ldots>s_{l}>-\infty
,
\bm{\sigma}=(\sigma_{0},\ldots,\sigma_{\ell})
and
\bm{\rho}=(\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{\ell})
are two collections of permutations in
\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa}
.
We require that:

(1)

\Gamma_{i}=(\Gamma_{i}(\cdot)_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{i}(\cdot)_{\kappa})\colon[s_{i+1},s_{i}]\rightarrow\Omega^{1}_{\sigma_{i}}(Q)
is a continuous map.

(2)

\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}(s)=\bm{X}^{i}(\Gamma_{i}(s))
.

(3)

\Gamma_{0}(+\infty)=\bm{\gamma}
,
\Gamma_{\ell}(-\infty)=\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
.

(4)

For each
i\colon 0\leq i\leq\ell-1
there is a transposition
(a_{i}b_{i})\in\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa}
such that

(3.13)

(\rho^{-1}_{i+1}\circ\sigma_{i+1}\circ\rho_{i+1})\circ\sigma_{i}^{-1}=(\sigma_{i}(a_{i})\sigma_{i}(b_{i})).

(5)

For
a_{i},b_{i}\in\{1,\ldots,\kappa\}
satisfying (
3.13
) for
i=0,\ldots,\ell-1
there exists
t_{i}\in(0,1)
, so that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i+1}(s_{i+1})_{\rho_{i+1}(a_{i})}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{$\Gamma_{i}(s_{i+1})_{a_{i}}(t)$ for $t\in[0,t_{i+1}]$}\\
\text{$\Gamma_{i}(s_{i+1})_{b_{i}}(t)$ for $t\in[t_{i+1},1]$}\end{array},\right.

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i+1}(s_{i+1})_{\rho_{i+1}(b_{i})}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{$\Gamma_{i}(s_{i+1})_{b_{i}}(t)$ for $t\in[0,t_{i+1}]$}\\
\text{$\Gamma_{i}(s_{i+1})_{a_{i}}(t)$ for $t\in[t_{i+1},1]$}\end{array}.\right.

(6)

\Gamma_{i+1}(s_{i+1})_{\rho_{i+1}(j)}=\Gamma_{i+1}(s_{i+1})_{j}
for
j\neq a_{i},b_{i}
.

Proposition 3.4
.

For a generic choice of collections of pseudo-gradients
\bm{X}^{0},\ldots,\bm{X}^{\ell}

and any
\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}\in CM_{*}(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q))
,
the space
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{X}^{0},\ldots,\bm{X}^{\ell})
is a smooth manifold of dimension
|\bm{\gamma}|-|\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}|-(n-2)\ell
. Furthermore, given some orientations of unstable manifolds of
\bm{\gamma}
and
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
there is a canonical orientation on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\bm{X}^{0},\ldots,\bm{X}^{\ell})
.

This proposition is a consequence of the discussion that follows.
For
\{a,b\}\subset\{1,\ldots\kappa\}
consider the subset
\Delta_{ab}\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)\subset\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)
given by

(3.14)

\Delta_{ab}\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)=\{\bm{\gamma}\in\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)\,|\,\text{ there exists }t\in(0,1)\text{ s.t. }\gamma_{a}(t)=\gamma_{b}(t)\}.

Let us consider the submersion

\displaystyle ev^{\kappa}_{[0,1]}\colon[0,1]\times\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}\rightarrow Q^{\kappa},

\displaystyle(t,\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{\kappa})\mapstochar\rightarrow(\gamma_{1}(t),\ldots,\gamma_{\kappa}(t)).

Then
\Delta_{ab}\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)
is obtained from the inverse image of Δ_ab Q^
κ
={(q_1, …, q_
κ
) ∈Q^
κ
—   q_a=q_b} ⊂Q^
κ
under
ev^{\kappa}_{[0,1]}
by projecting to
\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)
.
Therefore,
\Delta_{ab}\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)
is a codimension
(n-1)
submanifold of the Hilbert manifold
\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)
. Moreover, an orientation on
Q
then induces an orientation on
\Delta_{ab}\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)
(as it induces a coorientation on
\Delta_{ab}Q^{\kappa}
).

The diagonal
\Delta_{ab}\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)
has an open subset
\Delta^{\text{sm}}_{ab}\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)\subset\Delta_{ab}\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)
given by

(3.15)

\Delta^{\text{sm}}_{ab}\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)=\{\bm{\gamma}\in\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)\,|\,\text{ there exists a unique }t\in(0,1)\text{ s.t. }\gamma_{a}(t)=\gamma_{b}(t)\}.

For
\bm{\gamma}\in\Delta^{\text{sm}}_{ab}\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)
we denote by
t_{\Delta}(\bm{\gamma})
the associated
t\in(0,1)
for which
\gamma_{a}(t)=\gamma_{b}(t)
.
The
switching
described in (5) of Definition
3.3
comes from the following
smooth

(3.16)

\displaystyle sw_{ab}\colon\Delta^{\text{sm}}_{ab}\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)\rightarrow\Delta^{\text{sm}}_{ab}\Omega^{1}_{(\sigma(a)\sigma(b))\circ\sigma}(Q),

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}\mapstochar\rightarrow\bm{\gamma}^{\text{sw}}=(\gamma^{\text{sw}}_{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\text{sw}}_{\kappa}),\text{ where }

\displaystyle\gamma_{a}^{\text{sw}}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{$\gamma_{a}(t)$ for $t\in[0,t_{\Delta}(\bm{\gamma})]$}\\
\text{$\gamma_{b}(t)$ for $t\in[t_{\Delta}(\bm{\gamma}),1]$}\end{array},\right.\hskip 9.24994pt\gamma_{b}^{\text{sw}}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{$\gamma_{b}(t)$ for $t\in[0,t_{\Delta}(\bm{\gamma})]$}\\
\text{$\gamma_{a}(t)$ for $t\in[t_{\Delta}(\bm{\gamma}),1]$}\end{array},\right.

\displaystyle\gamma_{i}^{\text{sw}}=\gamma_{i},\text{ for }i\neq a,b.

We claim that
\text{sw}_{ab}
is, moreover, a diffeomorphism.
There is a conjugation action on
\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)
by any
\rho\in\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa}
given by the following map

(3.17)

\displaystyle\text{c}_{\rho}\colon\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)\rightarrow\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q),

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}\mapstochar\rightarrow(\gamma_{\rho(1)},\ldots,\gamma_{\rho(\kappa)}).

Notice that c_

We now state, without proof, two general lemmas from the Morse theory on Hilbert manifolds. For more details we refer the reader to
[
AS10
, Appendix A.2]
and
[

]
.

Lemma 3.5
.

(1)

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a Hilbert manifold with a pair
(X,f)
that satisfies the conditions of Theorem
3.1
. Let
x\in\text{crit}(f)
,
N
be an
m
-dimensional submanifold of
\mathcal{M}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{0}
be a codimension
m_{0}
submanifold, such that both of them are disjoint from
\text{crit}(f)
. Then for a generic perturbation of
X
supported away from the set of critical points the following sets

(3.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}^{x}=W^{u}(x;X)\cap\mathcal{M}_{0},

(3.19)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}^{N}=W^{u}(N;X)\cap\mathcal{M}_{0}

are submanifolds of respective dimensions
\operatorname{ind}(x)-m_{0}
and
(m+1)-m_{0}
.

(2)

Denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{0}^{x}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{0}^{N}
the stable manifolds of
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{x}
and
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{N}
under
X
, respectively.
Then, if the submanifolds
W^{u}(x;X)
and
N
are oriented, and the submanifold
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is cooriented, then the manifolds
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{0}^{x}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{0}^{N}
carry canonical orientations. This can be expressed in terms of orientation lines (see Section
2.14
) as

(3.20)

\zeta^{-1}\otimes|\eta_{\mathcal{M}_{0}}|\otimes|\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{0}^{x}|\cong|W^{u}(x)|,

(3.21)

\zeta^{-1}\otimes|\eta_{\mathcal{M}_{0}}|\otimes|\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{0}^{N}|\cong|W^{u}(N)|.

Lemma 3.6
.

(1)

Let
\varphi\colon\mathcal{M}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{2}
be a smooth map between Hilbert manifolds with
\mathcal{M}_{2}
admitting a pair
(f_{2},X_{2})
as in Theorem 3.1. Then for a given smooth
m
-dimensional submanifold
N
of
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and a critical point
y\in\mathcal{M}_{2}
the set

(3.22)

N^{y}=N\cap\varphi^{-1}(W^{s}(y;X_{2}))

is a submanifold of dimension
m-\operatorname{ind}(y)
for generic perturbation of
X_{2}
supported away from the critical points.

(2)

Denote by
\widetilde{N}
and
\widetilde{N}^{y}
the stable manifolds of
N
and
N^{y}
, respectively. Then, if the manifold
W^{s}(y;X_{2})
is cooriented and the manifold
N
is oriented, the manifold
N^{y}
carries canonical orientation. This can be expressed in terms of orientation lines as

(3.23)

|\eta_{W^{s}(y)}|\otimes|\widetilde{N}^{y}|\cong|\widetilde{N}|.

Remark 3.7
.

There is a
(-1)^{\operatorname{ind}(y)}
sign difference between the isomorphism in (
3.23
) and the one dictated by our convention for orienting a submanifold.

We are now ready to define the Morse free multiloop complex. For a critical multiloop
\bm{\gamma}\in\mathcal{P}^{\bm{L}}_{\kappa}
we associate rank-

generated by orientations of

and we set as before

Then we set

(3.29)

V^{\kappa}=\bigoplus_{\bm{\gamma}\in\mathcal{P}_{\kappa}^{\bm{L}}}o_{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathbb{Q}},

and

(3.30)

V^{\kappa}_{i}=\bigoplus_{|\bm{\gamma}|=i}o_{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathbb{Q}}.

Definition 3.8
.

The Morse chain free multiloop complex
CM_{*}\left(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)\right)
is a graded vector subspace of
W_{\kappa}\llbracket\hbar\rrbracket
given by

(3.31)

CM_{m}\left(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)\right)=\sum_{i+(n-2)j=m}V_{i}^{\kappa}\hbar^{j}=\sum_{i+(n-2)j=m}V_{i}^{\kappa}[(2-n)j].

By composing isomorphisms in equations (
3.24
), (
3.25
), (
3.27
) and permuting the terms (respecting the Koszul sign rule; see Section
2.14
) we get a canonical isomorphism

(3.32)

\big|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\ell)\big|^{-1}\otimes o_{\bm{\gamma}}\cong o_{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}\otimes\zeta^{(n-2)\ell}\cong o_{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}\bigl[(2-n)\ell\bigr].

When
|\bm{\gamma}|-|\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}|-(n-2)\ell=1
, given a piecewise pseudo-gradient trajectory
[\bm{\Gamma}]\penalty\ \in\penalty\ \mathcal{P}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\ell)
, the above isomorphism can be rewritten as

(3.33)

|\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}|^{-1}\otimes o_{\bm{\gamma}}\cong o_{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}\bigl[(2-n)\ell\bigr].

Fixing the upward orientation (arising from the positive gradient direction), which corresponds to the opposite orientation on
\mathbb{R}
, induces a morphism

(3.34)

\partial_{[\bm{\Gamma}]}\colon o_{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow o_{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{Q}}\bigl[(2-n)\ell\bigr].

We extend this map to any generator
[\bm{\gamma}]\hbar^{j}
of
CM_{*}\bigl(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)\bigr)
by tensoring the above isomorphism with
\zeta^{(n-2)j}
on the right:

(3.35)

\partial_{[\bm{\Gamma}]}\colon o_{\bm{x}}\otimes\zeta^{(n-2)j}\rightarrow\otimes o_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}\bigl[(2-n)\ell\bigr]\otimes\zeta^{(n-2)j}\cong o_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}\bigl[(2-n)(\ell+j)\bigr].

We may now define the differential on Morse free multiloop complex
CM_{*}\bigl(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)\bigr)
by

(3.36)

\displaystyle\partial\colon CM_{*}(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q))\rightarrow CM_{*-1}(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)),

\displaystyle\partial([\bm{\gamma}]\hbar^{j})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\geq 0,|\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}|=|\bm{\gamma}|-(n-2)\ell-1,\\
[\bm{\Gamma}]\in\mathcal{P}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\ell)\end{subarray}}\partial_{[\bm{\Gamma}]}([\bm{\gamma}]\hbar^{j}).

where
[\bm{\gamma}]\hbar^{j}
is some generator of
o_{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathbb{Q}}[(2-n)j]
for
j\geq 0
.
We devote the following section to showing that this is indeed a chain complex.

For the later use we define the
reflected
cochain complex
CM^{*}_{-}(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q))
as follows. First, set
V_
κ
, -^i= ⨁_—

—=-i(o_

^-1)^

.
Then
CM^m_-(Λ^1_

(Q))=∑_-(i+(n-2)j)=mV^i_

, -[(n-2)j].

Applying our conventions (

) we notice that

^(2-n)j⊗o_

Then clearly the isomorphism (

) also induces a morphism

(3.37)

The differential on the reflected Morse miltiloop complex is then given by

(3.38)

\displaystyle\partial\colon CM^{*}_{-}(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q))\rightarrow CM^{*+1}_{-}(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)),

\displaystyle d([\bm{\gamma}_{-}]\hbar^{-j})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\geq 0,|\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}|=|\bm{\gamma}|-(n-2)\ell-1,\\
[\bm{\Gamma}]\in\mathcal{P}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\ell)\end{subarray}}d_{[\bm{\Gamma}]}([\bm{\gamma}]\hbar^{-j}).

At last, we claim that there is a natural reflection chain map between the two complexes, inducing a homology isomorphism

(3.39)

\mathcal{I}\colon CM_{-}^{*}\bigl(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)\bigr)\rightarrow CM_{-*}\bigl(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)\bigr),

which one obtains by applying the isomorphism
\ell\cong\ell^{-1}
for orientation lines and inspecting Koszul sign rule, see Section
2.14
.

3.3.
Compactness

Since a piecewise gradient trajectory is not action-decreasing, the nonemptiness of
\mathcal{P}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\ell)
does not imply
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{L}}(\bm{\gamma})\geq\mathcal{A}_{\bm{L}}(\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})
.
However, we can still show that
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{L}}(\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})-\mathcal{A}_{\bm{L}}(\bm{\gamma})
is
C^{0}
-bounded, so for each
\ell\geq 0
,
\mathcal{P}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\ell)
is empty for all but finitely many
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
.
Therefore, the chain complex
CM^{*}_{\kappa}(\Lambda Q)
is locally finite.

Lemma 3.9
.

Fix a generic choice of a collection of pseudo-gradient vector field tuples
\bm{X}^{0},\ldots,\bm{X}^{\ell}
, given
|\bm{\gamma}|-|\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}|-(n-2)\ell\leq 2
, the space
\mathcal{P}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\ell)
admits a compactification.

Sketch of the proof.

Given
(2-n)l+|\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}|-|\bm{\gamma}|\leq 2
,
\mathcal{P}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};l)
is a smooth manifold of dimension
\leq 1
by Lemma
3.4
.
We need to consider all possible limiting behaviors of a sequence of elements in
\mathcal{P}_{*}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};l)
.
The proof is similar to
[
HKTY
, Lemma A.9]
.
We sketch the cases similar to
[
HKTY
, Lemma A.9]
and emphasize the difference.

Let

[\Gamma^{r}]=[((\Gamma^{r}_{0},\ldots,\Gamma^{r}_{l}),(\bm{s}^{r},\bm{\sigma}^{r},\bm{\rho}^{r})]

be a sequence of piecewise gradient trajectories in
\mathcal{P}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};l)
parametrized by
r\in\mathbb{N}
.
We can assume that the premutation data
((\sigma_{1}^{r},\ldots,\sigma_{\ell}^{r}),(\rho^{r}_{1},\ldots,\rho^{r}_{\ell}))=(\bm{\sigma},\bm{\rho})
is constant for this sequence.
As
r\rightarrow\infty
, if
[\Gamma^{r}]
does not limit to an interior element of
\mathcal{P}_{*}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};l)
, then there are four possibilities:

(1)
s^{r}_{i}-s^{r}_{i+1}\rightarrow+\infty
for some
1\leq i\leq l-1
.
This corresponds to breaking along
\Gamma^{r}_{i}
.
Breaking along
\Gamma^{r}_{0}
or
\Gamma^{r}_{l}
is similar.
In the limit
r\rightarrow\infty
,
\Gamma_{r}
degenerates to a broken piecewise pseudo-gradient trajectory which lies in
\mathcal{P}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime};\ell_{1})\times\mathcal{P}(\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\ell_{2})
, where
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}=\ell
.
The sum of the expected dimensions of
\mathcal{P}_{*}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime};\ell_{1})
and
\mathcal{P}(\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\ell_{2})
is equal to
|\bm{\gamma}|-|\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}|-(n-2)\ell-2
.
Therefore, if
|\bm{\gamma}|-|\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}|-(n-2)\ell\leq 1
,
s^{r}_{i}-s^{r}_{i+1}\rightarrow+\infty
does not occur for a generic choice of pseudo-gradient vector fields.
If
|\bm{\gamma}|-|\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}|-(n-2)\ell=2
, this is the required degeneration for the differential on
CM^{*}_{\kappa}(\Lambda_{\kappa}^{1}Q)
to be well-defined.

(2)
s^{r}_{i}-s^{r}_{i+1}\rightarrow 0
for some
1\leq i\leq\ell-1
.
In the limit
r\rightarrow\infty
, two switchings occur at the same
s
.
This case is discussed in
[
HKTY
, Lemma A.9]
.
We refer the reader to Figure
9
for an illustration.

Figure 9.
The image of
\sqcup_{i,s}\Gamma_{i}(s)
viewed as the graph in
Q\times\mathbb{R}_{s}
. The blue arc labelled by
j
denotes the image of
\Gamma_{i}(s)_{j}(0)
. As
r\rightarrow\infty
,
s^{r}_{i}-s^{r}_{i+1}\rightarrow 0
as shown by (A); the limit is shown by (B), where two switchings occur simultaneously; the family (A) is paired up with another family (C).
Note that the allowance of all permutation sequences in Definition
3.3
is necessary for the existence of (C).

(3) Generically, a 1-parameter family of piecewise pseudo-gradient trajectories may undergo a
birth-death bifurcation
.
This is also discussed in
[
HKTY
, Lemma A.9]
and we omit the proof here.

(4)
t_{i+1}^{r}\rightarrow 0
for some
i=0,\ldots,\ell-1
. Let us assume that
t_{i}\rightarrow 0^{+}
, meaning that all these times are in
[0,\epsilon)
for
i\gg 0
and a small
\epsilon>0
. For the limit of the paths
\Gamma^{r}_{i}(s_{i+1})_{a_{i}}
and
\Gamma^{r}_{i}(s_{i+1})_{b_{i}}
we have that
\Gamma^{\infty}_{i}(s_{i+1})_{a_{i}}
and
\Gamma^{\infty}_{i}(s_{i+1})_{b_{i}}
intersect at
t_{i+1}^{\infty}=0
.

We consider a subset of the closure
\overline{\Delta}_{ab}\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)\subset\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)
given by

(3.40)

\Delta^{\text{sm},0,1}_{ab}\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)=\{\bm{\gamma}\in\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)\,|\,\text{ there exists a unique }t\in[0,1]\text{ s.t. }\gamma_{a}(t)=\gamma_{b}(t)\}

which can be also given as a disjoint union
Δ^
sm
, 0,1_abΩ^1_
σ
(Q)=Δ^
sm
_abΩ^1_
σ
(Q) ⊔Δ^
sm
, 0_abΩ^1_
σ
(Q) ⊔Δ^
sm
, 1_abΩ^1_
σ
(Q)
, where

Δ^
sm
, 0_abΩ^1_
σ
(Q)={
γ
∈Δ^
sm
, 0,1_abΩ^1_
σ
(Q)   —
γ
_a(0)=
γ
_b(0)},
Δ^
sm
, 1_abΩ^1_
σ
(Q)={
γ
∈Δ^
sm
, 0,1_abΩ^1_
σ
(Q)   —
γ
_a(1)=
γ
_b(1)}.
Notice that both
\Delta^{0}_{ab}\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)
and
\Delta^{1}_{ab}\Omega^{1}_{\sigma}(Q)
are codimension
n
submanifolds. Additionally, we point out that

(3.41)

As an immediate corollary of Lemma
3.9
we obtain

Proposition 3.10
.

\partial^{2}=0
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.9
,
\#\mathcal{P}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\ell)
is finite whenever
|\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}|=|\bm{\gamma}|-(n-2)\ell-1
. We also point out that for a given
\gamma
the space
\#\mathcal{P}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\ell)
is empty for all but finitely many
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
by the Palais-Smale condition
2d
.
Given
|\bm{\gamma}|-|\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}|-(n-2)\ell=2
, applying the continuation procedure described in Lemma
3.9
the boundary of the compactification of
\mathcal{P}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};l)
may be regarded as a finite number of broken pseudo-gradient trajectories, i.e.,

\partial\overline{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\ell)=\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}=\ell,\\
|\bm{\gamma}|-|\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}|=|\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}|-|\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}|=(n-2)\ell+1\end{subarray}}\mathcal{P}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime};\ell_{1})\times\mathcal{P}(\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\ell_{2}).

To finish the proof it is enough to compare the composition map
\partial_{[\bm{\Gamma}_{2}]}\circ\partial_{[\bm{\Gamma}_{1}]}
and the map induced by boundary orientation. That is,

(3.43)

|\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}|^{-1}\otimes\big|\mathcal{P}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime};\ell)\big|^{-1}\otimes o_{\bm{\gamma}}\cong o_{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}\bigl[(2-n)\ell\bigr],

(3.44)

|\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}\bm{\Gamma}_{2}|^{-1}\otimes|\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}\bm{\Gamma}_{1}|^{-1}\otimes o_{\bm{\gamma}}\cong|\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}\bm{\Gamma}_{2}|^{-1}\otimes o_{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}\bigl[(2-n)\ell_{1}\bigr]\cong o_{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}\bigl[(2-n)\ell\bigr].

Now it remains to notice that
\partial_{s}\bm{\Gamma}_{1}
with the preferred orientation as in (
3.33
) is an outward pointing vector. Therefore,
\partial|_{o_{\bm{\gamma}}}^{2}=0
. We leave it to the reader to verify that
\partial|_{o_{\bm{\gamma}}[(2-n)j]}^{2}=0
for any
j>0
.
∎

We write
CM_{*}(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q);\bm{L},\bm{X})
and
HM_{*}(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q);\bm{L},\bm{X})
to indicate the specific choice of
\bm{L}=\{L_{1},\ldots,L_{\kappa}\}
and pseudo-gradient vector fields
\bm{X}
.

Proposition 3.11
.

The homology groups
HM_{*}(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q);\bm{L},\bm{X})
do not depend on the choice of
\bm{L}=\{L_{1},\ldots,L_{\kappa}\}
and pseudo-gradient vector fields
\bm{X}
.

Proof.

According to
[
AS10
]
changing
\bm{L}
or pseudo-gradient fields
\bm{X}
induces chain homotopy at the level of standard Morse chain complexes. Hence, if we were considering only pseudo-gradient trajectories with
\ell=0
the statement would immediately follow. We then claim that showing that changing one of
L_{i}
’s or
\bm{X}_{j}
’s alone induces a chain homotopy in the presence of switchings is similar to the above-mentioned line of the argument.
∎

4.
The isomorphism between the Heegaard Floer symplectic cohomology and the homology of the free multiloop complex

In this section, we define a chain map from the Floer complex to the Morse complex

\mathcal{F}\colon SC^{*}_{\kappa,\text{unsym}}(T^{*}Q)\rightarrow CM_{\kappa n-*}^{\kappa}(\Lambda Q),

by counting rigid elements in a mixed moduli space which combines holomorphic curves and piecewise gradient trajectories. We establish an analogue of the Viterbo isomorphism theorem in the context of Heegaard Floer symplectic cohomology by showing that
\mathcal{F}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

In this section, we assume that
Q
is an orientable closed manifold with vanishing second
Stiefel-Whitney class
w_2(TQ) ∈ H^2(T^*Q;
ℤ
/2).
This allows one to fix a spin structure on
Q
regarded as a Lagrangian submanifold in
T^{*}Q
. We also note that the approach in this section is parallel to
[
HKTY
, Section 3]
.

We point out that we work here with the unsymmetrized version of HFSH introduced in Section
2.13
and sometimes we abuse notations and do not specify that we work in this context as it is understood throughout this section.

4.1.
The half-cylinder Hurwitz space

We start by introducing the space of branched covers of a half-cylinder in the context of Section
2.13
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\sigma,\sigma_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa}
. For given
\chi>0
define
\mathcal{H}^{\sigma,\sigma_{0},\geq 0}_{\kappa,\chi}
to be the set of tuples

\left(\pi\colon S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times S^{1},\bm{p}^{+},\bm{a}^{+},\bm{P}^{0},\bm{a}^{0},B\right)\text{, where}

(1)

S
is a Riemann surface of Euler characteristic
\chi
with positive punctures
\bm{p}^{+}=\{p^{+}_{1},\ldots,p^{+}_{l(\bm{\mu})}\}
and boundary components
\bm{P}^{0}=\{P^{0}_{1},\ldots,P^{0}_{\ell(\sigma_{0})}\}
. Denote by
\overline{S}
the compactification of
S
such that
S=\overline{S}\setminus\bm{p}^{+}
.

(2)

\bm{a}^{+}=(a_{1}^{+},\ldots,a_{\kappa}^{+})
is a collection of positive asymptotic markers at positive punctures
p_{i}^{+}
such that for each
p_{i}^{+}
there are exactly
\mu_{i}(\sigma)
asymptotic markers

a_{i_{1}}^{+},\ldots,a_{i_{\mu_{i}}}^{+}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}_{p_{i}^{+}}S=(T_{p_{i}^{+}}S\setminus\{0\})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, and these are exactly the indices permuted by
c_{i}(\sigma)
abd they appear on
\mathcal{S}^{1}_{p_{i}^{+}}
in the order prescribed by the cycle
c_{i}(\sigma)
.

Analogously we choose marked points, which we simply call
markers
, on
\bm{P}^{0}
such that for each boundary component
P^{0}_{i}
there are exactly
\mu_{i}(\sigma_{0})
markers.The permutation on the set of markers induced by walking along
P_{i}^{0}
in the boundary orientation direction is required to be equal to
c_{i}(\sigma_{0})
.

(3)

\pi\colon S\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times S^{1}
is a holomorphic branched cover with simple branched points away from

and boundary

. At

and for each boundary component

-cover of

.

(4)

The map induced by

from

to

sends all asymptotic markers assigned to

to the asymptotic marker

. The markers

’s are all sent to

under
\pi
.

(5)

B=(q_{1},\ldots q_{b})
, is an ordered collection of points in
\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times S^{1}
of simple branching, where
b=-\chi
.

The tuple

4.3.

Lemma 4.3

For a tuple of Hamiltonian orbits

\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0}}_{\chi,\ell}(H^{\text{tot}},J,\bm{X};\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})

)) to obtain an operator

For the later isomorphism, we use the fixed orientation on

Next, we claim that for generic Floer data, the image of the space

As for the orientation on

(4.7)

By combining (

.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

For a generic choice of Floer data on
\mathcal{H}^{\sigma,\sigma_{0},\geq 0}_{\kappa,\chi}
, and a collection
\bm{X}
of pseudo-gradient vector fields, and an arbitrary tuple of Hamiltonian orbits
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{unsym}}(T^{*}Q)
and
m\geq 0
, the space

\underset{\ell-\chi=m,\,\sigma_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa}}{\bigsqcup}\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0}}_{\chi,\ell}(H^{\text{tot}},J,\bm{X};\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})

is empty for all but finitely many
\bm{\gamma}\in\mathcal{P}_{\kappa}^{\bm{L}}
.

Proof.

We omit the proof as it follows from the Palais-Smale condition
2d
, see
[
AS06
]
for details.
∎

From now on we will omit the auxiliary data in the notation for the mixed moduli spaces.

Asside on Kuranishi replacements.

As in Section
2.9
, for
n\geq 3
a sequence of elements in the mixed moduli space may converge to an pair with Floer side admitting ghost bubbles. More precisely, a sequence of curves
(u_{i},\Psi_{(i)})
,
i=1,2,\dots
, in
\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0}}_{\chi,\ell}(H^{\text{tot}},J,\bm{X};\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
, after passing to a subsequence, can limit to
(u_∞= u_∞,1∪u_∞,2, Ψ_(∞)),
where:

(i)

the main part
u_{\infty,1}
is the union of components that are not ghost bubbles; each component of the
u_{\infty,1}
is somewhere injective; and

(ii)

u_{\infty,2}
is a union of ghost bubbles, i.e., locally constant maps where the domain is a possibly disconnected compact Riemann surface (possibly with boundary) and
u_{\infty,2}
maps to points on
\operatorname{Im}(u_{\infty,1})
.

To remedy this we introduce we introduce Kuranishi replacements
\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0},\sharp}_{\chi,\ell}(H^{\text{tot}},J,\bm{X};\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
satisfying similar requirements to
1
-
3
(see
[
HKTY
, Section 3.2.2]
for more specifics). As before, we will pass to Kuranishi replacements whenever neccessary and drop
\sharp
from the notation.

Before diving into the discussion of the compactification of mixed moduli spaces of dimension
1
we introduce two new types of boundary behavior. Let us fix tuples
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{unsym}}(T^{*}Q)
and
\bm{\gamma}\in\mathcal{P}^{\bm{L}}_{\kappa}
satisfying for some
\chi\leq 0,\ell\geq 0
:

κ
n-—
x
—-1=—
γ
—+(n-2)(ℓ-
χ
).

Definition 4.5
.

The Type I singular mixed moduli space

\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0},\mathrm{I}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
is a space of tuples

\left((S,\pi),u,\bm{\Psi}\right)

as in (
4.2
) with the following differences:

(1)

(S,\pi)
represents a point in
\overline{\mathcal{H}}^{\sigma,\sigma_{0},\geq 0}_{\kappa,\chi}
, such that (only) one of the branch points in
B
has
s
-coordinate equal to
0
;

(2)

\bm{\Psi}=\left((\Psi_{1},\ldots,\Psi_{\ell}),\bm{s}=(s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{\ell+2}),\bm{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{\ell}),\bm{\rho}=(\rho_{2},\ldots,\rho_{\ell+1})\right)

is a tuple (note the different number of elements in each tuple) with conditions that
s_{0}=s_{1}
and
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{0}
.

(a)

Figure 12.
Approaching a Type I singularity.

Remark 4.6
.

Heuristically, a Type I boundary singularity is a result of a ramification point approaching the boundary and adding a new switching point on the Morse side at
s=0
(see Figure
12
). The number of switching points of the curves in
\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0},\mathrm{I}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
is equal to
\ell+1
. We point out here that
s_{1}
is not a genuine switching point since there is no naturally assigned permutation
\rho_{1}
with it and the pseudo-gradient flows from
s_{1}=s_{0}
to
s_{2}
are prescribed by the old
\sigma_{0}
.

Definition 4.7
.

The Type II singular mixed moduli space

\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0},\mathrm{II}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
is the space of tuples

\left((S,\pi),u,\bm{\Psi}\right)

as in (
4.2
) with the following differences:

(1)

(S,\pi\colon S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times S^{1})
is a branched cover with a set of branched points consisting of
|B|=-\chi+1
points with (only) one of these points having
s
-coordinate equal to
0
;

(2)

\bm{\Psi}=\left((\Psi_{1},\ldots,\Psi_{\ell}),\bm{s}=(s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{\ell+1}),\bm{\sigma}=(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{\ell}),\bm{\rho}=(\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{\ell})\right)

is a tuple with conditions that
s_{0}=s_{1}
, and for markers
a^{0}_{a_{0}}
,
a^{0}_{b_{0}}
such that positive arcs connecting these markers to the next (in counterclockwise order) markers on
\partial S
contain the ramification point it holds that

(\rho^{-1}_{1}\circ\sigma_{1}\circ\rho_{1})\circ\sigma_{0}^{-1}=(\sigma_{0}(a_{0})\sigma_{0}(b_{0})).

(a)

Figure 13.
Approaching a Type II singularity.

Remark 4.8
.

A Type II boundary singularity is a result of
s_{1}
approaching
s_{0}
accompanied by the creation of a new ramification point on
S
(as well as a branch point on the target; see Figure
13
for a schematic picture). The Euler characteristic of curves in
\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0},\mathrm{II}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
is equal to
\chi-1
.

Remark 4.9
.

Following discussion of Remarks
4.6
and
4.8
, we note that given a curve in
\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0},\mathrm{I}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
we may obtain a curve in
\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0}^{\prime},\mathrm{II}}_{\chi+1,\ell+1}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
by arbitrary adding
\sigma_{0}^{\prime}
and
\rho_{1}
to the data in Definition
4.5
satisfying condition (2) in Definition
4.7
. Hence one geometrical curve of
\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0},\mathrm{I}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
corresponds to
\kappa!
curves of

\bigsqcup_{\sigma_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S_{\kappa}}}\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0}^{\prime},\mathrm{II}}_{\chi+1,\ell+1}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma}).

This phenomenon is crucial for the map defined via counting mixed curves to be a chain map.

Lemma 4.10
.

For a generic choice of consistent Floer data on
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}^{\sigma,\sigma_{0},\geq 0}_{\kappa,\chi}
and a collection of pseudo-gradient vector fields
\bm{X}
, a tuple of Hamiltonian orbits
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{unsym}}(T^{*}Q)
and a multiloop
\bm{\gamma}\in\mathcal{P}_{\kappa}^{\bm{L}}
satisfying

(4.8)

\kappa n-|\bm{x}|-|\bm{\gamma}|-(n-2)(\ell-\chi)=1,

the wnb groupoid
\mathscr{T}^{\sigma_{0}}_{\chi,\ell}(H^{\text{tot}},J,\bm{X};\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})(U)
admits a compactification
\overline{\mathscr{T}}^{\sigma_{0}}_{\chi,\ell}(H^{\text{tot}},J,\bm{X};\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
.

Sketch of the proof.

As in the proof of
2.38
one may show that there is a portion of the boundary corresponding to the vertical stretching, and we leave it to the reader to fill in the details. Points of this type on the boundary of
\overline{\mathscr{T}}^{\sigma_{0}}_{\chi,\ell}(H^{\text{tot}},J,\bm{X};\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
correspond to elements of

(4.9)

\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{x}^{\prime}\in SC^{*}_{\kappa}(T^{*}Q),\\
\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}=\chi\end{subarray}}\mathscr{M}^{\chi_{1},\text{ind}=1}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})\times\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0},\text{ind}=0}_{\chi_{2},\ell}(\bm{x}^{\prime},\bm{\gamma})

counted with weights
\frac{1}{\kappa!}
.

The second type of boundary degeneration corresponds to stretching on the Morse side and is treated in Lemma
3.9
. These points of the boundary are elements of

(4.10)

\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}\in CM_{\kappa n-*}(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)),\\
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}=\ell\end{subarray}}\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0},\text{ind}=0}_{\chi,\ell_{1}}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})\times\mathcal{P}^{\text{ind}=1}(\bm{\gamma}^{\prime},\bm{\gamma};\ell_{2}).

One last case that we have to analyze is the boundary nodal degeneration with a possible occurrence of almost ghost bubbles attached along the boundary. Let us first treat this scenario. Namely, assume that
(u_{i},\Psi_{i})
limits to
(u_{\infty}=u_{\infty,1}\cup u_{\infty,2},\Psi_{\infty})
, where
u_{\infty,2}
is null-homologous (see
2
) has domain attached to the domain of
u_{\infty,1}
along
k
boundary nodes. We then have

ind
u_∞=
ind
u_i+k(n-2)
by (
4.5
). On the other hand, the appearance of a boundary node along the Lagrangian
\{0\}\times S^{1}\times Q
is a codimension
(n+1)-2\cdot 1=n-1
condition since the Lagrangian is
(n+1)
-dimensional and the boundary of the domain is mapped to a real
1
-dimensional curve. In total, we get a dimension drop of
k(n-1)-k(n-2)=k.
Hence,
u_{\infty,2}
can be attached only along the boundary node
1
and it must have only
1
boundary component. This component can not be a disk, since there have to be at least two ramification points on it. Therefore,
\operatorname{ind}(u_{\infty,2})<0
and it violates
3
and
2
.

Finally, from the above analysis, it follows that there could be no more than
1
boundary node. If no ghost bubbles appear, this leaves us with the two singular types of boundary points
\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0},\mathrm{I}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
and
\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0},\mathrm{II}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
.
∎

4.4.
Chain map

To define a morphism
\mathcal{F}

(4.11)

\mathcal{F}\colon SC^{*}_{\kappa,\text{unsym}}(T^{*}Q)\rightarrow CM_{\kappa n-*}\bigl(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)\bigr),\\

we first consider the canonical isomorphism

(4.12)

o_{\bm{\gamma}}[(2-n)(\ell-\chi)]\otimes o_{\bm{x}}\cong\big|\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})\big|^{-1}\otimes\zeta^{\kappa n}.

When the dimension of the mixed moduli space is
0
the above provides the isomorphism

(4.13)

o_{\bm{x}}\cong\bigl(o_{\bm{\gamma}}[(2-n)(\ell-\chi)]\bigr)^{-1}\otimes\zeta^{\kappa n},

which induces a morphism

(4.14)

f_{[u,\bm{\Psi}]}\colon o_{\bm{x}}\rightarrow\bigl(o_{\bm{\gamma}}[(2-n)(\ell-\chi)]\bigr)^{-1}[-\kappa n].

We extend it to
o_{\bm{x}}[(n-2)j]
for
j>0
by simply multiplying the above with
\zeta^{(2-n)j}
on the left.
We first define
\mathcal{F}^{-}\colon SC^{*}_{\kappa}(T^{*}Q)\rightarrow CM_{-}^{*}\bigl(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)\bigr)[-\kappa n]
as above to be

(4.15)

\mathcal{F}^{-}([\bm{x}]\hbar^{j})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\bm{\gamma}|=\kappa n-|\bm{x}|-(n-2)(\ell-\chi),\\
\ell\geq 0,\chi\leq 0,\,\sigma_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa},\\
(u,\bm{\Psi})\in\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|\bm{x}|+(n-2)j}\cdot\frac{f_{(u,\bm{\Psi})}([x]\hbar^{j})}{(\kappa!)^{\ell}}.

The morphism
\mathcal{F}
is then obtained by composing
\mathcal{F}^{-}
with a
\kappa n
-shift of
CM_{-}^{*-\kappa n}\bigl(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)\bigr)[-\kappa n]
and the reflection chain map
\mathcal{I}\colon CM_{-}^{*}\bigl(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)\bigr)\rightarrow CM_{-*}\bigl(\Lambda^{1}_{\kappa}(Q)\bigr)
; see (
3.39
).

By Lemma
4.10
,
\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
is finite, hence the maps
\mathcal{F}^{-}
and
\mathcal{F}
are well-defined.

Theorem 4.11
.

The map
\mathcal{F}^{-}
is a chain map.

Proof.

Sketch of proof of Theorem

.

We convert the desired perturbations of a nodal curve into a smooth one into a commonly used SFT-type gluing result (see, for example,
[
FOOO
]
which treats holomorphic curves before/after Lagrangian surgery).
“Zooming in” near the intersection point, we consider the following local model:

(1)

the ambient symplectic/almost complex manifold is
\mathbb{C}^{n}
with the standard symplectic form,

(2)

the Lagrangian boundary condition is the standard
\mathbb{R}^{n}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
, and

(3)

the punctures of the finite energy holomorphic curves under consideration limit to chords (in a Morse-Bott family) from
S^{n-1}
to itself, where
S^{n-1}\subset S^{2n-1}
is the Legendrian boundary at infinity of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We consider pairs of half-planes of the form

Proof of Claim

.

By transversality for mixed moduli, we know that

This finishes the proof of the fact that any point of
\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0},\mathrm{II}}_{\chi+1,\ell+1}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})

Conversely, one may show in a similar way that for a

Together, the above two statements imply that counting points of singular type I moduli spaces
\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0},\mathrm{I}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
with weights
\kappa!
and the signs induced by boundary orientation of
\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
gives the opposite of the sum of points of
\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0},\mathrm{II}}_{\chi+1,\ell+1}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
with signs. That is

(4.16)

\kappa!\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa},\\
(u_{\mathrm{I}},\bm{\Psi}_{\mathrm{I}})\in\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0},\mathrm{I}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{\mathrm{I}}_{(u_{\mathrm{I}},\bm{\Psi}_{\mathrm{I}})}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa},\\
(u_{\mathrm{II}},\bm{\Psi}_{\mathrm{II}})\in\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0},\mathrm{II}}_{\chi+1,\ell+1}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{\mathrm{II}}_{(u_{\mathrm{II}},\bm{\Psi}_{\mathrm{II}})}=0.

According to Lemma
4.10
, the remaining codimension-

-direction, and correspond to composing

Now, by counting elements of the boundaries of the spaces
\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})

and

with signs induced by boundary orientations and respective weights

we obtain

(4.17)

(-1)^{\kappa n}\partial\circ\mathcal{F}^{-}-(-1)^{\kappa n}\mathcal{F}^{-}\circ d=0,

where vanishing of terms corresponding to points of singular moduli spaces follows from (
4.16
).
It only remains to show that boundary orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{T}}^{\sigma_{0}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
indeed provides the sign as stated above.

Let us consider a pair (u, (v,

)) ∈

^

χ
_2, ℓ(
x
’,
γ
) ⊂∂
T
^
σ
_0_
χ
, ℓ(
x
,
γ
)
with

(4.18)

|\bm{x}^{\prime}|-|\bm{x}|+(n-2)\chi_{1}=1,\,\kappa n-|\bm{x}^{\prime}|-|\bm{\gamma}|-(n-2)(\ell-\chi_{2})=0\text{ and }\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}=\chi,

and compare the map
f_{(v,\bm{\Psi})}\circ d_{u}
with the map
o_{\bm{x}}\rightarrow(o_{\bm{\gamma}}[(2-n)(\ell-\chi)])^{-1}[-\kappa n]
induced by boundary orientation at this pair. We notice that under the condition
\kappa n-|\bm{x}|-|\bm{\gamma}|-(n-2)(\ell-\chi)=1
the isomorphism (
4.12
) is equivalent to

(4.19)

(-1)^{|\bm{\gamma}|+(n-2)(\ell-\chi)+1}\big|\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})\big|\otimes o_{\bm{x}}\cong\bigl(o_{\bm{\gamma}}[(2-n)(\ell-\chi)]\bigr)^{-1}\otimes\zeta^{\kappa n}.

Recalling (
2.82
) we note that composition
f_{(v,\bm{\Psi})}\circ d_{u}
is given by isomorphisms

(4.20)

|\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}u|\otimes o_{\bm{x}}\cong o_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}[(2-n)\chi_{1}]\cong\bigl(o_{\bm{\gamma}}[(2-n)(\ell-\chi)]\bigr)\otimes\zeta^{\kappa n}.

Clearly,
\partial_{s}u
is an outward pointing vector on
\partial\overline{\mathcal{T}}^{\sigma_{0}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})
, which implies that
|\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}u|\cong\big|\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})\big|
. We also point out that

(4.21)

|\bm{\gamma}|+(n-2)(\ell-\chi)+1=\kappa n+1-|\bm{x}^{\prime}|-(n-2)\chi_{1},

which follows from (
4.18
). Now recalling the sign in front of
f_{(v,\bm{\Psi})}
in (
4.15
) we conclude that the map induced by (
4.19
) is equal to the restriction of
-(-1)^{\kappa n}\mathcal{F}^{-}\circ d
.

As for the other type of boundary contributions we consider
((u,
Ψ
), [
Γ
]) ∈
T
^
σ
_0_
χ
, ℓ_1(
x
,
γ
’) ×
P
(
γ
’,
γ
; ℓ_2)
with

(4.22)

\kappa n-|\bm{x}|-|\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}|-(n-2)(\ell_{1}-\chi_{2})=0,\,|\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}|-|\bm{\gamma}|-(n-2)\ell_{2}=1,\text{ and }\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}=\ell.

The composition
\partial_{[\bm{\Gamma}]}\circ f_{(u,\Psi)}
is then given by the composition

(4.23)

(-|\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}\bm{\Gamma}|)\otimes o_{\bm{x}}\cong(-|\partial_{s}\bm{\Gamma}|)\otimes\big(o_{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}[(2-n)(\ell_{1}-\chi)]\big)^{-1}[-\kappa n]\cong\big(o_{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}[(2-n)(\ell-\chi)]\big)^{-1}\otimes[-\kappa n]

The vector
-\partial_{s}\bm{\Gamma}
is outward pointing, hence
-|\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}\bm{\Gamma}|\cong\big|\mathcal{T}^{\sigma_{0}}_{\chi,\ell}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})\big|
, which means that
\partial_{[\bm{\Gamma}]}\circ f_{(u,\Psi)}
differs from the map induced by boundary orientation only by a term
(-1)^{|\bm{\gamma}|+(n-2)(\ell-\chi)+1}
. Now, noting that
|\bm{\gamma}|+(n-2)(\ell-\chi)+1=\kappa n-|\bm{x}|
, we conclude that boundary induced map coincides with
(-1)^{\kappa n}\partial\circ\mathcal{F}^{-}
.
∎

Remark 4.15
.

We point out that our proof of Theorem
4.11
is based on the proof of the analogous statement for HDHF that is worked out in
[
HKTY
]
.

4.5.
The isomorphism

For
\kappa=1
, the complex
SC_{\kappa,\text{unsym}}^{*}(T^{*}Q)
coincides with the symplectic chain complex
SC^{*}(T^{*}Q)
computing the symplectic cohomology of
T^{*}Q
, whereas the complex
CM^{*}_{\kappa}(\Lambda Q)
coincides with the Morse chain complex
CM^{*}(\Lambda)
. Originally due to Viterbo
[
Vit95
,
Vit99
]
, the homology groups of these two chain complexes are known to be isomorphic. In
[
Abo11
]
Abouzaid gives a refined proof of this fact accounting for the twisting of the symplectic homology depending on the second Stiefel-Whitney class of
Q
. We claim that the map constructed there (see
[
Abo11
, Section 3.2]
) coincides with
\mathcal{F}
on the level of homology. First, we notice that
the morphism
\mathcal{V}
in
[
Abo11
]
on the chain level has as its target
generic
(in Morse-theoretic sense) singular chains
C_{*}(\Lambda Q)
of the loop space. We claim that
\mathcal{F}
is chain homotopic to a composition of
\mathcal{V}
with a map
\mathcal{A}_{L}
from generic singular chains to Morse complex given by counting rigid flows from singular chains to critical points of the appropriate Morse function (see, e.g.
[
HL99
, Section 2]
; we also refer the reader to a similar discussion in the case of based loop space in
[
Abo12
, Section 5]
). Therefore, we conclude:

Theorem 4.16
.

For
\kappa=1
,
\mathcal{F}
induces a homology isomorphism.

Remark 4.17
.

In the context of linear Hamiltonians the map constructed in
[
Abo15
, Section 4]
is heuristically the same map that we construct for quadratic at infinity Hamiltonians. One may argue that the limit of the maps given there is equal to
\mathcal{F}
.

We also point out that the morphism
\mathcal{EV}
is proved to be an isomorphism in
[
Abo11
]
by showing that it is an inverse of the map constructed in
[
AS06
]
and is known to be an isomorphism. It would be of its own interest to construct an analogue of this morphism for HFSH.

Lemma 4.18
.

The restriction of
\mathcal{F}
to
\hbar=0
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of
[
HTY a
, Lemma 6.5]
.
We only sketch the difference here.
Recall that with each tuple
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{unsym}}(T^{*}Q)
(resp.
\bm{\gamma}\in CM^{*}_{\kappa}(\Lambda Q)
) there is an associated permutation
\sigma(\bm{x})\in\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa}
(resp.
\sigma(\bm{\gamma})\in\mathfrak{S}_{\kappa}
).
By definition the restriction of
SC^{*}_{\kappa,\text{unsym}}(T^{*}Q)
to
\hbar=0
is split according to permutation type:

(SC^{*}_{\kappa}(T^{*}Q)|_{\hbar=0},d^{0})=\bigoplus_{\sigma\in S_{\kappa}}(SC^{*}_{\kappa}(T^{*}Q)|_{\hbar=0,\sigma},d^{0}),

where
SC^{*}_{\kappa}(T^{*}Q)|_{\hbar=0,\sigma}
is generated by tuples of Hamiltonian orbits with associated permutation equal to
\sigma
.
Similarly,

(CM_{*}^{\kappa}(\Lambda Q)|_{\hbar=0},\partial_{0})=\bigoplus_{\sigma\in S_{\kappa}}(CM_{*}^{\kappa}(\Lambda Q)|_{\hbar=0,\sigma},\partial_{0}).

Note that
\mathcal{F}|_{\hbar=0}
also respects the splitting according to permutation type.
It suffices to show

(4.24)

\mathcal{F}|_{\hbar=0}\colon SC^{*}_{\kappa}(T^{*}Q)|_{\hbar=0,\sigma}\rightarrow CM^{\kappa}_{\kappa n-*}(\Lambda Q)|_{\hbar=0,\sigma}

is an isomorphism. Note that for any generator
[\bm{x}]\in SC^{*}_{\kappa}(T^{*}Q)|_{\hbar=0,\sigma}

with
[\bm{x}]\in o_{\bm{x}}^{\mathbb{Q}}
for some
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{unsym}}(T^{*}Q)
the count
\mathcal{F}|_{\hbar=0}(\bm{x})
has non-zero entries only for elements in
o_{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathbb{Q}}
with
\bm{\gamma}
such that
\sigma(\bm{\gamma})=\sigma(\bm{x})
.
We denote by
\gamma^{c_{1}(\sigma)},\ldots,\gamma^{c_{\ell(\sigma)}(\sigma)}
the loops constituting
\bm{\gamma}
. Then we have the following.

\mathcal{T}^{\text{Id}}_{0,0}(\bm{x},\bm{\gamma})\simeq\prod_{i=1}^{i=\ell(\sigma)}\mathcal{T}_{0,0}(x^{c_{i}(\sigma)},\gamma^{c_{i}(\sigma)}).

It then suffices to show (
4.24
) holds when
\sigma
is a cycle, which is given by Theorem
4.16
.
∎

Lemma 4.19
.

Given cochain complex
(C^{*},d^{0})
and a chain complex
(D_{*},\partial_{0})
.
Let
F
be a degree
m
chain map

F\colon(\widetilde{C}^{*},d)\rightarrow(\widetilde{D}_{*},\partial)

between associated complexes as in Construction
2.39
given by

F=F_{0}+F_{1}\hbar+F_{2}\hbar^{2}+\ldots.

If
F_{0}\colon(C^{*},d^{0})\rightarrow(D_{*},\partial_{0})
is a quasi-isomorphism, then
F
is a quasi-isomorphism as well.

Proof.

For simplicity, we replace
(D_{*},\partial_{0})
with the associated cochain complex
(D_{s}^{*},\partial^{0})
obtained by first “reversing arrows” and replacing the degree with its negative and then shifting the degree by
m
. Similarly, we obtain
(\widetilde{D}_{s}^{*},\partial)
. Then
F_{s}\colon(\widetilde{C}^{*},d)\rightarrow(\widetilde{D}_{s}^{*},\partial)
is a chain map of degree
0
.

Now, we notice that
Cone(F_s)—_ℏ=0=Cone(F_0).
We recall that a chain map is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if its cone is acyclic. According to the conditions of the lemma,
Cone(F_{0})
is acyclic, and hence
Cone(F_{s})|_{\hbar=0}
is acyclic. It remains to show that it implies that
Cone(F_{s})
is acyclic. From now on, denote this cochain complex via
(E^{*},d_{E})
.
Let v=v_0+v_1ℏ+v_2ℏ^2+… be a cocycle of degree
k
in
E^{k}
. Here
|v_{i}|=k+i(n-2)
. Let us show that there is
w\in E^{k-1}
such that
d_{E}w=v
. First, since
(E^{*}|_{\hbar=0},d_{E}^{0})
is a quasi-isomorphism, there is
w_{0}\in E^{k-1}|_{\hbar=0}
such that

(4.25)

d_{E}^{0}w_{0}=v_{0}.

Then we may write
v-d_Ew_0=
~

v

_1ℏ+
~

v

_2ℏ+….
Clearly,
v-d_{E}w_{0}
is a cocycle, and since multiplication by
\hbar
is a chain injective map, hence

\tilde{v}=\tilde{v}_{1}+\tilde{v}_{2}\hbar+\ldots

is a cocycle. Arguing as in (
4.25
) there is a
w_{1}\in E^{k+(n-2)-1}
satisfying
d_E^0 w_1=
~

v

_1.
Clearly, one has
v-d_E(w_0+w_1ℏ)=
~

~

v

_2ℏ^2+….
Repeating the procedure above one obtains a sequence
w_{0},w_{1},\ldots,
such that d_E(w_0+w_1ℏ+w_2ℏ^2+…)=v. ∎

Theorem 4.20
.

\mathcal{F}
induces an isomorphism on homology.

Proof.

This statement follows from Lemmas
4.18
and
4.19
.
∎
